## M: Mob–mystic mitt

### Mob
 
 <i>n.</i> the Mongrel Mob gang (See [cf mobster](mob-mys.md#mobster))

>  Manaia and Reihana were brothers. Their dad was Mob and their mother was a tiny woman who could drink a crate and scrap with the best of them. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,197</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> The following New Zealand expressions derive from British dialects, ... <i>mob:</i> gang <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),116</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> He said he was a mongrel. Then in the police station, according to the constable's evidence, he broke down and said he wanted to leave the mob. <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,4</i>)

>  Whatever cause the mob sets itself every mobster is required to adhere to it. <i>(Dec 17, 1986,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>) [NOTES: Morning Report: George Mamfredos, Leader of the Mongrel Mob]

### mob
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [farm stock] to gather or be gathered together.

>  <b>MASTERS</b> They mobbed, then with tails and manes flying took off full gallop up the valley. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,185</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> Spent some hours driving the main mob of sheep from the fence where they mob day and night. <i>(Mar 10, 1878,Chudleigh Diary,267</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Once the sheep are mobbed, care must be taken to avoid .. a lamb break. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,150</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> ... they found the dogs had about one hundred and fifty ewes mobbed against a boundary fence, and were worrying for all they were worth. <i>(1970,Tat,61</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> When the strings of sheep were mobbing on Jack's Flat, the end of the muster was in sight. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,78</i>)

>  By mobbing stock, Ruakura experts showed that sheep could be kept free from facial eczema if the farmer acted quickly enough. <i>(Sep 10, 1958,Auckland Weekly News,3</i>)

>  Dogs are efficient in wide open spaces for mobbing and driving sheep but they should be chained up on reaching yards,' Dr Holmes concluded. Got all that Wal? <i>(May 6, 1980,Evening Post</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> any crowd or large collection of people; a large number of anything (See [(cf bush mob)](../(#(cf-bush-mob)))

>  <b>McNAB</b> [A sailor's account] A mob of natives came running into the hut where we stopped. <i>(1834,Old Whaling Days,424</i>)

>  The Pah is small, and occupied by a few native, the crops, as I understood, being the property of several `mobs' in different parts of the Sounds. <i>(Mar 24, 1845,NZ Company Reports,XIV/</i>) [NOTES: (Fox to Wakefield)]

>  I ... by boat and we all went ... to Goashore by 4p.m., meeting a mob of Moeraki natives at Kai-tiri-tiri. [Torlesse uses `mob' frequently of natives/Maoris]. <i>(Feb 27, 1886,Torlesse Papers,632,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mob n</i>. crowd of people (first used in N.Z. in 1845); flock of sheep or herd of cattle. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>BURROWS</b> A considerable mob of Heke's people passed through our settlement. [The word used in the 1845 MS, Tues 27 May 1845 is <i>party</i>.] <i>(1886,Heke's War,32</i>) [NOTES: [quote from 1845 diary]]

>  In Major Hornabrook's words, `the Steadfast's mob is a much jollier mob than that of the Duke of Bronte' (The word `mob' in colonial diction only means a `set of people') <i>(1851,Australian & NZ Gazette,322/11,1852 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Journal of a Canterbury settler.)]

>  I live in hopes that more of our `Mob' have decent homes of their own [the usual family name used of themselves as a group, in this correspondence] <i>(1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/351 et passim</i>)

>  It was `our mob' coming to the rescue. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,106</i>) [NOTES: check page, could be 206]

>  <i>mob ... 5. Austral. & N.Z.</i> a large number of anything. <i>6. Austral</i>. & <i>N.Z.</i> a flock or herd of animals. <i>7. mobs of. Austral.</i> &<i> N.Z. inf</i>. lots of. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,724</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 482; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 314]]

>  It was good to see the splendid eagerness of the "mob" generally for work. <i>(Oct 16, 1916,Chronicles NZEF,I/4 : 87</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> He was most pleased with the eccentricities of the `Whaling mob' he had to rule. <i>Ibid.</i> 319 The [whaling] parties enrolled in Sydney received an advance and spent it there; a brig, or schoaner, then carried the whole `mob', as the party was sometimes called, to their stations in New Zealand. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/309</i>)

>  Commence shearing with a strong mob of shearers. <i>(1860,St Leonard's Journal</i>)

>  <b>DUPPA</b> ... a strong mob of shearers. <i>(1861,Diary</i>) [NOTES: (1861 Duppa Diary) in 1948 Crawford Sheep and Sheepmen 48]

>  Sending messages to Booth about the mob [of paupers] he has got. <i>(Oct 10, 1905,NZ Truth,17/7/4</i>)

>  <b>KOEBEL</b> He had but a few hours ago formed one of their `mob,' and [was] the most skilful bushwhacker in the district. <i>(1907,Return of Joe,257</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The `mob' of natives, having rushed in upon their fallen foe. <i>(1845,Adventure in NZ,1/437</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grouping of farm stock [ as distinct from herd or flock in New Zealand.]

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> mob: as distinct from herd or flock which, though grammatically incorrect, mean the sum total of a man's cattle or sheep, a mob means an integral part of that herd or flock. (eg held in a particular paddock.) A mob also means a collection of beasts, possibly of one or more owners, all boxed together and being driven, in which case it is never a herd or flock. When a mob of sheep or cattle is put up for sale, it is then referred to as a line. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,53</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> How to get your Sheep out of a mixed mob - that's a trial for human patience. <i>(1856,Journal,II 215</i>) [NOTES: Journal (1980)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was more horrible to see the drowning .. huddled-up 'mob' (as sheep en masse are technically called.) <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> Dave and I had the sales mob out at Cascade ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,107</i>)

>  About 100 bulls are bought midsummer at 18 months old and used as clean-up mob and then set in winter grazing mobs and sold as they reach processing weights from December onward. <i>(May 31, 2002,Dominion,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a group of sheep held together [specific usage; often used for the total number of sheep a farmer owns.]

>  A herd [of cattle], or mob as we [? in New South Wales] call it.) <i>(1842,NZ Journal,70/22</i>)

>  <i>(1851,Australian & NZ Gazette,No. 33 (New Series) 11</i>) [NOTES: [?dated Jan 10 1857 OED??]]

>  <b>DEAN</b> I should like to put all the calvers in one mob. <i>(Sep 24, 1853,Deans' Letters,295,1937 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Robinson Clough)]

>  <b>CHAPMAN</b> a Mob of sheep. <i>(1853,Diary,13</i>)

>  ... he is therefore of the opinion that this was not the first mob Mackenzie had driven off. <i>(Apr 14, 1855,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Apr 14 Otago Witness in 1937 Ayson Pioneering in Otago 50]

>  <b>SIDEBOTTOM</b> I should tell you I have found old sheep tracks (large tracks of a good mob), ... <i>(Mar 6, 1855,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1855 Mar 6 Sidebottom's letter in 1958 Gillespie South Canterbury (1971) 460]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> how to get Sheep out of a mixed mob - that's a trial for human patience. <i>(1856,Journal,2/215,1980 ed.</i>)

>  [up to here uses <i>flock</i> with sheep] Worthington brought a mob of cattle. <i>(Nov 23, 1859,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  One sheep will infect others, and the whole mob will soon become diseased; ... <i>Ibid.</i> vii/89 Many stations have a small mob of cattle from whence to draw their working bullocks, so that a few more or a few less makes little or no difference. <i>(Feb 10, 1860,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,IV/42</i>)

>  <i>[of sheep; first use with sheep, (usu.</i> flock)] Phillips over with Wedders, joined the lot in the pens & counted mob (see Sheep book). [by July 1860 <i>mob</i> seems to replace <i>flock</i> e.g.] July 23 Drafted 200 ewe lambs ... from Brasseys mob and joined them with Chapman's mob. <i>Ibid.</i> June 6 Drafted also 43 lambs ... out of McDonalds flock and joined them to lamb mob - <i>(Mar 7, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  so Amy took his place which is to ride ... between the bullocks that have been cut out and the old mob ... <i>(Mar 14, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,28,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> As your mob increases, you can put an out-station on the other side of the run. <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,x/150</i>)

>  [We] began mouthing sheep. Polhill who has bought the run and the sheep took down the mouths on paper as the sheep are to be paid for according to their ages. ... You catch a sheep in your arms, carry it to the gate where the mouther stands, hold the mouth open for him to look at and you at the same time call out two, four, six, full or whatever it may be. ... So you know the number age sex etc. of your whole mob. <i>(Oct 1, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,105,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MUTER</b> A `mob of cattle', to use the colonial term, after a few expressions of `My word', and `No fear' would be sold at some ten pounds per head. <i>(1864,Travels and Adventures,2/273</i>)

>  I am off in the morning with my first mob of sheep for the new run. <i>(1865,McLean Papers,25/55</i>) [NOTES: (Ormond)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... `mobs' of horses to look at. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,27</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was horrible to see .... the puddled-up `mob' (as sheep en masse are technically called). <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,169</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> As a rule, to drive a mob of sheep for any distance, takes not less than four hands ... <i>Ibid.</i> 46 It needs incessant care to prevent stragglers breaking away from the head and sides of the mob, and the rear of the flock, made up of the weaker sheep, requires an equally constant lookout. The horses are led or ridden, as the ground allows; ... but in driving through unknown country, the halts are made very much where the explorers find themselves at sundown. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,45</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Saw large mob of sheep on the new burn outside Hughie's fence <i>(Feb 4, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 4 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 125]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mob, n</i>. a large number, the Australian noun of multitude, and not implying anything low or noisy. It was <i>not</i> used very early, as the first few of the following quotations show. -->[1811 - 1846 - herds of kangaroos); droves] Of <i>Men</i> [1874] Of <i>Blackfellows</i> [1822] of <i>Cattle </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,296</i>)

>  <b>DONALDSON</b> `Now to the stockyard crowds the mob;/ `Twill soon be milking time.' of <i>Sheep </i> <i>(1860,Bush Lays and Rhymes,14</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> `All the animals to make friends with, mobs of horses to look at.' <i>Ibid.</i> 169 `It was more horrible to see the drowning, or just drowned, huddled up `mob' (as sheep <i>en mass</i> eare technically called) which had made the dusky patch we noticed from the hill.' of <i>Horses </i> <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,27</i>)

>  <i>mob n.</i> a large number, esp. of cattle or sheep. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <i>Mob</i>.- Any number of sheep, cattle, or horses together. The words <i>herd</i> and <i>flock </i>(q.v.) are not used in this sense in New Zealand. <i>M</i>. is also applied to birds wild animal; e.g., ducks, quail, deer, or pigs. When a <i>m.</i> of sheep or cattle is offered for sale, it becomes a <i>line</i> (q.v.). <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The cattle were left near a Maori kaik ... and it was arranged that the Maoris ... should take the mob through the bush. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,113</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A few more cases [of our indebtedness to Australia for much of our early pastoral vocabulary includes] the word `mob' for a collection of animals whether it be horses, cows, sheep, goats, or pigs. Although the words `herds' (of cattle) and `flocks' (of sheep) were occasionally used, they were too pedantic for ordinary use, and the word `mob' was indiscriminately used on nearly all occasions. <i>(1947,Early Runholding in Otago,12</i>)

>  Its purpose is to divide a mixed flock ... into any required number of smaller mobs, ... <i>(Jul 15, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,7</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>mob</i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  <i>~ </i>In Australia and New Zealand a flock or herd of sheep, cattle, etc. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),262</i>)

>  The farmer walked his horse behind the mob. <i>(1975,NZ Short Stories III,151</i>) [NOTES: 1967 Dibble `A Way of Love' in 1975 NZ Short Stories III 151]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> The vocabulary of New Zealand English has been influenced by changes in the usage of traditional English words, which have acquired new meanings ... Familiar examples of the first group are <i>mob, creek, paddock</i>, and <i>bush</i>, all of which acquired their new meaning first in Australia. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/679</i>) [NOTES: Wall NZ Speech]

>  <i>Mob</i>: Sheep/Cattle/Goats/Horses/ Usually smaller group within a flock or herd. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> I remember ... noticing a mob of sheep in one of my distant paddocks being worried by Jim's dogs. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,127</i>)

>  <b>BAYLISS</b> ... a mob of goats .... a small mob of wethers. <i>(1866,Glimpse of Shepherd Life, A,622</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... there were all the animals to make friends with, `mobs' of horses to look at, rabbits, poultry, and pets of all sorts. <i>(1865,Station Life in NZ,27,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> It was more horrible to see the ... huddled-up `mob' (as sheep <i>en masse</i> are technically called) which had made the dusky patch we had noticed from the hill. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,169,1870 ed.</i>)

>  I also pay another visit to the yards. ... [Sheep] stream over the ground like a fluid and the outside of the mob always consists of a string of animals in rapid motion. <i>(Mar 18, 1867,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/236,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>BARKER</b> At the entrance of the gorge is a large stockyard, and near to it ... a large mob of horses is generally to be found feeding. <i>(1868,Station Life in NZ,234,1870 ed.</i>) [NOTES: of horses]

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> `I'd rather you helped me tail a mob if you've got a few hours.' <i>(1984,Day Hemingway Died, The,16</i>) [NOTES: (Takahe Man)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> extended to groups of any living things

>  When I was 10 years old I watched a mob of kea meticulously dismantle my grandmother's windscreen wipers. <i>(Aug 2009,Forest & Bird,333/18</i>)

>  <b>DONALDSON</b> Now to the stockyard crowds the mob, 'twill soon be milking-time. <i>(1860,Bush Lays and Rhymes,14</i>)

>  <b>WEEKES</b> After a little quiet watching I observed a mob of some fifty pigs hunt down a lamb, and then gobble it up. <i>(1846,My Island</i>) [NOTES: c1846 Weekes My Island in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 130]

>  <b>W H S ROBERTS</b> We now had quite a large mob of breeding mares on the run, with a very useful stamp of horse which we called a roadster, who gloried in the name of `Sir Roger de Coverley'; ... <i>(Jun 1856,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1856 June W.H.S. Roberts Diary in 1947 Beattie Early Runholding 34]

>  Strangers left with Mr M's mob of horses <i>(Sep 17, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  For about 400 birds of this large size [Moas] to have been roasted in so small a compass in one mob would be a physical impossibility. <i>(1874,TrNZI,7/130</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> ... turkeys breed and thrive on the sheep-run without any attention, except when a `gobbler' is required for the table. Then the mob is quietly run into a netted enclosure, with the assistance of a wise-working collie ... The gate is left open and the mob can walk out [when one is captured]. <i>Ibid.</i> 97 In the morning our dogs stuck up a mob of pigs on a small, clear-topped rise. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,34,1935 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1872 in c1875 Meredith Adventuring in Maoriland (1935) 34]

>  <b>CRADDOCK</b> A bevy, or `mob' [of pheasants] (as every collection of any living thing is called in New Zealand) ... <i>Ibid.</i> 203 ... the `mob' of greys [sc ducks] .. <i>(1904,Sport in NZ,57</i>)

>  <b>BOOTH</b> During the time we were snowed up the mob of horses came almost every day to the stock yard for rock salt... <i>(1912,Five Years in NZ,84</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> There were nine of us who fell in ... at Harry Tanner's little bush store and accommodation-house, and each ... picked out a horse from the little mob, of Maori-bred animals that the packer brought along ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,2</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... I pointed to a mob of paradise ducks in his turnip field, he invited me to ... have a shot at them. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,49</i>)

>  <b>HISLOP</b> ... I pointed to a mob of paradise ducks in his turnip field, he invited me to ... have a shot at them. <i>(1943,Pure Gold,49</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Distinctive collective words for various live things are not much use in the Colonies. You refer to a drove of cattle or horses, a flock of sheep, a flight of ducks, or bevy of quail, as just a `mob' <i>(1947,Quondam Quaker,40</i>)

>  <b>CRUMP</b> We saw nine deer in one mob and had no rifle. <i>(1960,Quondam Quaker,21</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Wild turkeys were run in flocks, sometimes 40 to 50 in a mob, would take to wing, and fly hundreds of yards across gullies to a distant hillside. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,120</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> ... it had a good population of ducks. They came in small mobs, five or seven at a time; it wasn't a big stretch of water. <i>(1982,Master of Big Jingles,116</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Sometimes an acre would have to be resown owing to large mobs of quail eating the seed ... <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,31</i>)

### MOB
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Maori Oil Board

>  Mr Powell said the Crown Minerals Act should be changed to require all decisions to give effect to Treaty principles, and that there should be "Mob" (local Maori Oil Board) approval before the Crown issued oil-drilling permits. <i>(Oct 20, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

### mob and demob
 
 <i>n.</i> mobilisation and demobilisation

>  Exploration manager Eric Matthews said "mob and demob" for a rig such as Ocean Bounty cost millions. <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

### mob grazing
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) mob stocking to stimulate growth or to eradicate fern and weeds. (See [hoof and tooth treatment](hom-hyp.md#hoof-and-tooth-treatment); [mob stocking](mob-mys.md#mob-stocking))

>  <b>LEVY</b> Both hard fern and pirpiri decline and disappear if the pasture is stimulated by topdressing and by the adoption of an off-and-on mob grazing system. <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,303</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> During periods of slow growth rotational mob-grazing of paddocks will produce more feed than set-stocking and this method should be applied over the late autumn and winter months. Mob-grazing over the late autumn and winter period ensures that feed is accumulated ahead of the stock ... <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,58</i>)

### mob over
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to drive sheep across an area in heavy numbers.

>  On country on which it is difficult or undesirable to use a roller for seedbed consolidation sheep may be mobbed over the area after it has been sown. <i>(Jan 1965,NZ Journal of Agriculture,37</i>)

### mob stocking
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the applying of stock to pasture in heavy numbers to eliminate fern or weeds or to eat out heavy growth in a crop or pasture.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Exceptionally heavy mob stocking from weaning until lambing is the regulation practice ... <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,76</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> With modern methods of mob stocking, one trough isn't enough to water the paddock because they aren't regular about drinking. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,59</i>)

>  Mob stocking is similar but more aptly describes the practice of using a large mob of stock confined to a restricted area for the particular purpose of clearing away unwanted roughage or rubbish. <i>(Apr 2, 1959,NZ Farmer,15</i>)

>  <b>MACMILLAN</b> 'Mob-stocking' or 'hoof and tooth' treatment will eventually put sufficient pressure on fern and other weeds to virtually eliminate them if the paddocks are small enough in relation to the mob size. <i>(1980,Land Development in the Chatham Islands,5</i>)

>  The resultant downward pressure of a myriad of hooves will squash the offending insects - a kiwi technological invention called 'mob-stocking' ... [mob-stocking] <i>(May 2001,Growing Today,52</i>)

>  Mr Lockhart says the greatest improvement in hill country will come from adoption in some farm or another of the policy found successful on flat farm lands - rotational grazing or, better termed `mob stocking'. <i>(Oct 8, 1953,NZ Farmer,74(16)/47</i>)

>  The shape and topography of the farm made it suitable for mob stocking and the early stages of the plan were directed towards subdivision to facilitate this type of management. <i>(1958,Report of Soil Conservation ...Council,19</i>)

>  <i>mob stocking NZ</i> & <i>Austral</i>. moving grazing livestock from paddock to paddock in rotation (cf. <i>set stocking</i>). <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,482</i>)

### mob striking
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the using of stock to eat fern and weed in pasture. (See [hoof and tooth treatment](hom-hyp.md#hoof-and-tooth-treatment))

>  Experiments in Akitio included the use of mob-striking to clean up rough growth. <i>(Nov 22, 1951,NZ Farmer,6</i>)

### mob up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [farm stock] to gather or be gathered together.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> To straggle a block of country means of course to muster in the stragglers .. Thus the sheep missed previously have the opportunity to 'mob up' and join in with the shorn sheep. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> mob or mob up: to gather up, or to be driven together into one mob. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is essential to see that the rams get well amongst the ewes, which should be well 'mobbed up' if not mustered. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,92</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The third day, as the sheep mobbed up, it was necessary to exercise great care in running a dog. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,123</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> They mobbed up during the night, and when dawn came the musterers saw Merino wethers stringing out through the shingle saddle onto what was known as no-man's-land ... <i>(1969,Open Country Calling,35</i>)

>  <b>SINCLAIR</b> As soon as they were mobbed up, there was practically no drawing left in them, and it was a matter of hard punching all the way. <i>(1973,Boot in the Stirrup,12</i>)

>  <b>NEWPORT</b> When I was young I did see hill sheep calling and mobbing up together when a storm was approaching. <i>(1989,Footprints Farewell,91</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The sheep being mobbed up together near the spot where they are intended to enter the water, the best plan is to split off a small number ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,8/126</i>)

>  <b>McNAUGHTON</b> The farmer made his way through the mob with his dogs, and endeavoured to keep the sheep mobbed up, ... <i>(1970,Tat,106</i>)

>  When the shepherd is riding round the ewes they tend to move in front of him and mob up in a corner of the paddock near a gateway. <i>(1958,NZ Young Farmer, The,10(14)/114</i>)

### mob work
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sheepdogs] mustering and shepherding work.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The obvious thing to do was to get a pup just starting to run and break it in during shearing when there was plenty of 'mob work' where a pup can do no harm. <i>(1959,Tall Tussock,46</i>)

### mob-graze
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [farm stock] to graze in large numbers to stimulate growth or clear pasture of weeds.

>  Ewes can mob graze to clean up paddocks behind young growing stock. <i>(1994,Guide to Feed Planning for Sheep Farmers,36</i>)

### mob-stock
 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to apply stock to pasture in large numbers to eliminate weeds or to starve stock for animal husbandry purposes.

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> One other method of controlling bracken is to fence off and mobstock with cattle. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,195</i>)

>  <b>BARDSLEY</b> We mob-stocked and rotationally grazed, so disease spread. [mobstock] <i>(2000,Land Girls, The,160</i>)

>  All sheep are mob-stocked between weaning and lambing. <i>(Mar 9, 1967,NZ Farmer,7</i>)

>  It was hot, dry weather and the affected stock were heavily mob-stocked to reduce weight. <i>(Sep 24, 1970,NZ Farmer,46</i>)

>  After weaning the flock ewes are mob-stocked through to crutching and eye-clipping in April. <i>(Dec 1988,NZ Farmer,14</i>)

>  They may be mobstocked or scatter grazed. <i>(Jun 1995,NZ Dairy Exporter,13</i>)

>  Previous owners had mobstocked the farm and controlled the ragwort with sheep. <i>(Nov 4, 1999,Taranaki Daily News,17</i>)

>  <i>Mob Stock</i>: To move herd or flock from paddock to paddock at short intervals (also called `intensive grazing' or `rotational grazing <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,7</i>)

### mobbing
 
 <i>n.</i> mob stocking, mob stock grazing

>  The grazing management system was modified to a considerable extent from that of traditional set stocking to one known as mob stocking or mobbing. In such a method of grazing, paddocks are heavily stocked in turn so that the pasture herbage available is used to the fullest extent in the shortest possible time. <i>(1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,95(4)/389</i>) [NOTES: 1950 date a guess - check]



 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  For best results with the mobbing system... <i>(Oct 1957,NZ Journal of Agriculture,389</i>)

### mobbing up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the gathering of stock into a mob or unit.

>  It [feeding out of silage] would have advantages, too, in getting the rams and ewes together almost as effectively as mobbing-up during the day. [mobbing-up] <i>(Jul 1970,NZ Journal of Agriculture,65</i>)

 <i>n.</i> a mobile start (harness racing)

>  <i>(Dec 8, 1965,Weekly News,59/1</i>) [NOTES: OEDS]

### moccasin
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SMITH</b> ... the shearers at the pen gates arrayed in their working clothes, with bowyangs (a strap over their trousers, below the knees, which allows them to bend with more comfort), and moccasins on (a kind of shoe made out of wool-pack, after the style of the foot-covering [76] of the Red Indian) ... <i>(1927,Sheep & Wool Industry,75</i>)

>  <i>Woolshed terms.</i>-The <i>water pot</i> is a tin of water (usually disinfected) which a shearer has tied by him to wet his shears in. To <i>gap</i> shears is to make (accidentally) a g. in the edge. <i>Sweat rag:</i> kept by the shearer to wipe his brow. <i>To grind</i> (shears) is always used without the noun. A shearer never `grinds his shears.' <i>Moccasins</i> are the sacking slippers which most shearers make to work in. Unless the owner of the station is a careful, observant man, he will hardly recognise his own wool pack tops. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>MEEK</b> MOCCASINS: Shearer's footwear, made of jute, cut from a woolpack. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>BOWEN</b> MOCCASIN. A shearer's correct footwear. <i>(1956,Wool Away!,156</i>)

>  <b>MIDDLETON</b> The shearers would be back in the whare: ...They would make new pairs of jute moccasins ... <i>(1959,Stone, The,53</i>) [NOTES: (The Stone)]

>  <b>DICK</b> Then, with bowyangs and moccasins suitably adjusted, and chatting all the while with Allan and the men, he ambled into the pen, ... <i>(1964,High Country Family,71</i>)

>  <i>Moccasin:</i> The shearer's home-made footwear, usually made of sacking or felt. <i>(Feb 26, 1965,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>) [NOTES: (H.A.L. LAING)]

>  <i>MOCCASIN:</i> The shearer's homemade footwear, usually made of sacking or felt. <i>(Feb 21, 1968,Straight Furrow,20</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Shearers nearly all wear ... home-made sacking `moccasins', which are soft on the feet and nonslippery on the greasy shearing board. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/229</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND & TAYLOR</b> He found the cap of a woolbale and cut his shearing moccasins from it with a pair of blades. <i>(1981,Sunrise,44</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> When we had made our sack moccasins and completed our preparations at the shed, we returned to the `homestead' for the evening meal. <i>(1986,Off the Sheep's Back,67</i>)

>  <b>OGONOWSKA-COATES</b> <i>Moccasins</i>. Shearers' preferred footwear, made from jute and cut from a wool bale. <i>(1987,Boards, Blades & Barebellies,97</i>)

>  <i>moccassins</i>:[sic] Carpet, sack or leather footwear worn in the shearing shed. <i>(1989,NZ English Newsletter,3/25</i>) [NOTES: (Wallace Shearing Glossary)]

### moccy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sacking moccasin [traditional shearers' footwear.]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Smoko time - the discarded 'moccies' awaiting the return of the shearers. <i>(1999,Tales from the Shearing Shed,13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> jinx

>  Manolitsis believes he is a "mock" when he goes to the track to watch Young Rufus tackle feature races... "I always felt I'm a mock to the horse and, after so many unlucky runs, I thought I would just stay away the other night." <i>(Dec 8, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B17</i>)

### mock cream
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mock Cream... cup milk, 1 dessertspoon cornflour, 1 tablespoon butter, a small cup flour. Mock Cream... Mix cornflour and milk together <i>(1924,Nelson Cookery Book,80</i>) [NOTES: Date of book is given as c.1924-1930]

>  Mock Cream... Mix cornflour and milk together smoothly, boil for 3 or 4 minutes. Mix butter and sugar to a cream, add cooked cornflour when cold, beat well. Mock Cream Filling 3 oz. butter, 2 oz. flour, 2 oz. sugar, teaspoon gelatine softened in 1 tablespoon cold water.., 2 tablespoons boiling water. <i>(1961,Edmonds Cookery Book,35</i>)

>  As far as I know, 'mock cream' cake fillings are indigenous to this country., There are no similar recipes in any of the English, Continental or American cook-books. Why we needed to invent imitation cream when there was always so much of the real thing around is a mystery to me. <i>(Jul 12, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),52</i>)

### mock whitebait
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Take all skin and bone away from some cold white fish and flake the flesh into pieces about the size of whitebait. Roll in flour, and leave them a little time; then fry in a frying basket with plenty of boiling fat until they are crisp and brown. Serve at once very hot after dusting with a little pepper. Serve lemon and cayenne pepper with them. <i>(May 17, 1900,Otago Witness,56</i>)

>  COOKERY DEMONSTRATION .. Puff Pastry, Fish Vol-ou-Vent, Cheese Tartlets, Mock Whitebait, Vanilla Ice, and Bermaline Bread. <i>(Mar 4, 1919,Evening Post,2</i>)

>  Mock Whitebait. - Raw fish (any kind), 2 tablespoons flour, teaspoon salt, cayenne, egg glazing, breadcrumbs, frying fat, lemon. Free the fish from the bone, and cut into strips about one and a half inches long. Mix flour, salt, and cayenne. Dip the pieces of fish in the flour, then in egg, and coat with crumbs. Fry a golden brown in hot fat. Drain. Serve piles high on a hot dish, garnished with slices of lemon. <i>(Feb 8, 1936,Evening Post,19</i>)

>  <i>MOCK WHITEBAIT </i>One egg ... potatoes ... Pepper and salt. ... Drop in spoonfuls onto boiling fat. <i>(1928,Everybody's Cookery Book of Tested Recipes,67</i>)

 <i>n.</i> <i>nthornis melanura</i>, makomako (See [bellbird](b-bla.md#bellbird))

Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

### mockie/mockey
 
 <i>n.</i> the makomako, bellbird [See note at mocker]

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> 'Mockies' were singing their evening song ... <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,32</i>)

>  As the first sun-ray tipped the hill, the mocky's call sounded. <i>(1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,8/355</i>)

>  <i>Mockie.</i>- Mako-mako, or Moki-mok. (Anthornis melanura). The name is still sometimes used; but the bird is now generally called a `Mokky'. [1951 Glossary merely has after `melanura': The bird is sometimes still called a `mockie'.] ([1874 Lady Barker Station Life],p.93; [1898 Morris Austral-English]) <i>(Sep 9, 1933,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> [glossary] Mako-mako; New Zealand bell-bird, popularly known as the `mocker' or `mockie' <i>(1934,Windy Island</i>)

>  <i>tui</i>, a bird (Prosthemadera novaeseelandiae), the most characteristic of the smaller Maori birds, kept by the Maori as a pet and by them taught to speak, it being an excellent mimic, and so by the settlers called the mocking-bird, shortened to mockie, and called parson-bird because of the two white curled tufts of feathers at its throat. Then, because some folk thought mockie was short for makomako, a name of the bell-bird (korimako) in some parts, some thought it was the korimako which was the mimic, and hence both birds have been called the mocking-birds. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/147</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BELLBIRD <i>Anthornis melanura Other names</i>: Korimako, Makomako, Mockie. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,218</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>`Mockie'</i> (makomako): The little green bellbird. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,255</i>)

>  <i>Bellbird Anthornis melanura</i> (SPARRMAN, 1786) ... <i>Three Kings Bellbird A.m. obscura</i> FALLA, 1948. ... <i>Chatham Island Bellbird A.m. melanocephala</i> GRAY, 1843. ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Korimako, makomako, mockie. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,290</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mockie </i>bellbird. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

>  A second course, modestly listed on Ralph's hand-written menu as Moi Moi Potato Stew and Northland Oysters, in reality proved more elaborate: the oysters had simply been warmed through, but the tiny purple moi moi potatoes had been peeled, cooked, mashed and stuffed inside large tortellini. <i>(Jan 2001,Cuisine,133</i>)

It also has larger eyes and bigger scales. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,224</i>)

### Moeraki Boulders
 
 <i>n.</i> (in North Otago)

>  The Moeraki boulders are an assemblage of near-perfect spheres that lie like discarded cricket balls on an east Otago beach. The largest of them span more than two metres in diameter and weigh over seven tonnes.[15] Claims have been made that the Moeraki boulders are the only rocks of their kind in the world... The truth is that concretions are common, but are usually only a few centimetres in diameter, irregular in shape and unlikely to impress... To Moeraki's Ngai Tahi residents, the boulders' origin is intertwined with the arrival of kumara from distant Hawaiki. According to Te Rungana o Moeraki, the great waka Arai-Te-Uru was wrecked during a fierce storm at Matakaea (Shag Point), just south of Moeraki. The boulders are food baskets that washed ashore after being thrown overboard to lighten the load. The Katiki concretions are said to be kumara tubers and seeds from the same wreck. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/12</i>)

>  Along the way she [sc. Shona McFarlane] also looks at natural art. "Sculptures like the Moeraki boulders, the Cathedral Cliffs at Gore Bay, the Oamaru stone quarry. <i>(Apr 6, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),91</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Expressing a desire to visit and visualise the famous Moeraki Boulders, the trip was made to those strange ever increasing boulders. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,89</i>)

>  The Moeraki boulders are impressive, the largest two metres in diameter and weighing several tonnes. An unexplained phenomon, the boulders are believed to be 60 millions years old and lie scattered on Moeraki Beach like great soccer balls. <i>(May 20, 2003,Dominion Post,A13</i>)

>  The Ngai Tahu Trust Board has completed the purchase of the Moeraki Boulder Park complex near Oamaru. <i>(Jul 3, 1992,National Business Review,6</i>)

>  The irregularity in composition of the Moeraki boulders is so great that it would be practically impossible to manufacture cement from them of a uniform quality. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> After breakfast we walked on to Moerangi whaling station, passing on the way, about two miles from the station some most remarkable boulders, ... which appear to have been formed not by rolling but by crystallization. <i>(Jan 10, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 19 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 15]

>  <b>McCLYMONT</b> [Mantell ? 1848] studied the Moeraki boulders and remarked that the whalers called them `Ninepins' and the spot itself `Vulcan's Foundry'. <i>(1940,Exploration of NZ, The</i>)

>  They appear to be of a dense black basalt, many of them round in shape, and ... somwwhat of the look of Moeraki boulders. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/193</i>)

>  Older than the sands on which they are buried,.. Moeraki's famous boulders are literally "cracking up." <i>(May 11, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,11</i>)

 <i>n.</i> n. A fish. = mo[o]akihaki. [no further identification]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Fam. DISCOBOLI 74. <i>Lepadogaster pinnulatus</i>. - J.R. Forster ... Haunts stony beaches and the mouths of rivulets in Queen Charlotte's Sound. It is named `moyeadoo' [e-grave] by the natives. <i>(1842,Travels in NZ,2/225</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/225]

### mohiki
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [moki](mob-mys.md#moki))



 
 <i>n.</i> a raft made usually of bundles of dry flaxsticks (korari) or raupo. [Maori moki(hi)[o-macron]]);occas. & early used in the North Island,

>  To get into Fiordland Hayden crosses Lake Te Anau on a Maori reed canoe called a mohiki. It was made by.. one of the country's few remaining reed canoe-builders. <i>(Nov 9, 1985,Listener (New Zealand Listener),57</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The war party.. built themselves "mohiki".. and descended the river Matau, till they reached the coast. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,208</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> [We] proceeded at once to the outlet of the lake, which is so broad and deep that it can only be crossed with a mohiki. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,100</i>)

### mohimohi
 
 <i>n.</i> a small sea-fish resembling the pilchard. [Maori]

>  SARDINA NEOPILCHARDUS (Steindachner). <i>Pilchard; Mohimohi</i>. Sold in a smoked condition in Wellington as `Picton herring'. ... Distribution: New Zealand coasts (unknown, Bay of Islands). <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/118</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Sardinia neopilcharda</i> Steindachner. PILCHARD; MOHIMOHI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,12,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Sardinia neopilcharda</i> Steindacher. Pilchard. <i>Mohimohi. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/402</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> PILCHARD OR MOHIMOHI <i>Sardinia neopilcharda</i>(Steindachner). ... Fifty years ago the pilchard was much more common around our southern coasts than it now is. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,7</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> PILCHARD (MOHIMOHI) <i>Sardinops neopilchardus (Steindacher) The Pilchard is known from the northern end of the South Island as the Picton Herring, and off Picton large shoals of these fish have from time to time been recorded. It has no relation to the so-called New Zealand Herring or Yellow-eyed Mullet (Agonostomus forsteri). </i> <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,104</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> THE PILCHARD <i>Sardinops neopilchardus (Steindachner)</i> ... Maori name: MOHIMOHI. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,29</i>)

>  ...<i> large numbers of aua or kataha (Agonostoma forsteri); about 60 lb. ... weight of pilchard or mohimohi (Clupea sagax), two varieties; ... hundreds of young red-cod, rarii (<i>Lotella bacchus</i>), and what I believe are young rock-cod, or kokopu or rawaru (<i>Percis colias</i>). The red-cod were from 3 in. to 4 in. in length, and the rawaru, or, as the natives here call them, `toitoi' or `panepane', from 2 in. to 6 in. long. <i>(1903,TrNZI,35/319</i>) [NOTES: Mair Fish in Piako River]</i>

### mohiotanga
 
 <i>n.</i> Maori knowledge [ORIGIN: Maori, Earlier citation, same page: As someone who has spent much of their life trying to promote te reo Maori and mohiotanga - Maori knowledge - through the film and TV industry ...]

>  But it's also important to have programmes such as <i> Shortland Street</i> that "normalise" the role of Maori, te reo and mohiotanga. <i>(Jun 7, 2007,Dominion Post,B6</i>)

### moho
 
 <i>n.</i> = takahe [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>BULLER</b> NOTORNIS MANTELLI. (MANTELL'S NOTORNIS.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Moho, Takahe, and Tokohea. [191] Mr. Walter Mantell states that ... It was known to the North-Islanders by the name of `Moho', and to the South-Islanders by that of `Takahe'. <i>(1973,Birds of New Zealand,189, 191,{1888 2nd edn 2/85} ed.</i>)

>  <i>Notornis mantelli</i>. - Takahe; Moho. Resolution and Secretary Islands; and at Barepatch, between Maruia and Upokororo Rivers, east of Lake Te Anau. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/113</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <i>Takahe Notornis mantelli</i> OWEN, 1848 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Moho, pass bird.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,170</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

### moho-pereru
 
 <i>n.</i> banded rail (See [also moho](mob-mys.md#moho))

>  <b>BULLER</b> RALLUS PHILIPPENSIS. (STRIPED RAIL.) ... <i>Native names</i>. Patatai, Popotai, Mohotatai, Moho-patatai, Moho-pereru <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,171</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> RALLUS PHILIPPENSIS. (BANDED RAIL.) ... <i>Native names</i>.. Patatai, Popotai, Mohotai, Moho-patatai, Moho-pereru, and Puohotata; `Land-Rail' of the colonists. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/95</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 87. RALLUS PECTORALIS. Less. Striped Rail. Mohopereru. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,32</i>)

>  <i>Rallus philippensis</i>. - Mohopereru, Striped Rail. Both Islands; Australia, Polynesia, Celebes, and Philippines. <i>(1886,TrNZI,18/117</i>) [NOTES: (Martin)]

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> ... the quail (<i>koreke</i>: Coturnix N.Z.), the rail (<i>mohoperu</i> and <i>moeriki</i>: Rallus sp.) and many other land-birds were cooked or potted down. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,105</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> BANDED RAIL <i>Rallus philippensis Other names</i>: Landrail, Striped Rail, Mohopereru, Mioweka. (The old sealers' name for the Macquarie Island race was Wigeon!) <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,104</i>)

>  <i>131.a. Rallus philippensis assimilis</i> Gray, 1843 <i>Banded rail (Moho-pereru) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,41,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Banded Rail Rallus philippensis </i>GRAY, 1843 ... OTHER NAME: <i>Moho-pereru. ... Mainly in northen mangrove swamps from Northland to Bay of Plenty. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,165</i>)

### mohoua
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [mohua](mob-mys.md#mohua))

>  MOHOUA OCHROCEPHALA, Gml. Mohoua. Canary. ... as soon as one ascends the bushy gullies of the hills, the Canary is sure to pay a reconnoitering visit; ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/57</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

### mohua
 
 <i>n.</i> a small South Island bird with yellow head and breast reputed to appear in flocks just before a rain; also called bush canary, rain bird, storm-bird, yellow-head [Compare upokotea, white-head. [Maori]] (See [yellowhead](ydu-yen.md#yellowhead))

>  Operation Ark has swung into action to protect mohua/yellowheads from surging rat numbers in the Catlins. <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,24</i>)

>  Kiwi and mohua had been missing from the park since the 1970s, and saddlebacks since well before that. <i>(Jan 10, 2001,Evening Post,15</i>)

>  In the past two years, seven of the nine largest remaining populations of the mohua, or yellowhead, in the South Island have undergone serious decline. <i>(May 2002,NZ Geographic,57/8</i>)

>  Mohua were once the most common native birds in [sic] South Island; today, this valley is the only place they are found on the mainland.. The mohua's new home is a beautiful place. <i>(Dec 3, 2011,Your Weekend,30</i>)

>  New Zealand's native forests are being over-run by pests - possums, deer, rats and stoats. In the last 12 months, mohua/yellowhead populations were wiped out in the Eglinton Valley of Fiordland and on Mt Stokes in the Marlborough Sounds. <i>(May 17, 2002,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Since the rediscovery of mohua on the mountain in 1985, their numbers had painstakingly been built up from six to 90. But because ship-rat numbers built up over winter and rats moved for the first time into the mohua breeding zone, the result was a crash of the fragile population back to a critical level. <i>(Apr 2000,NZ Geographic,No. 46/13</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 14. <i>Mohoua ochrocephala. G.R.Gray</i>, ... Mohoua houa of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy et Gaim</i>. Popokatea, natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/189]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 14. <i>Mohoua ochrocephala. G.R.Gray</i>, ... Mohoua houa of the natives of Tasman Bay. <i>Quoy et Gaim</i>. Popokatea, natives of Cook's Straits. <i>Dieffenbach</i>. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/189]

>  <i>mohua</i>, a gregarious bird (Mohoua ochrocephala), South Island form of the bush-canary, the North Island form (Mohoua albicilla) being upokotea. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>mohua:</i> yellowhead (forest bird). <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> YELLOWHEAD <i>Mohoua ochrocephala Other names</i>: Mohua, Bush Canary. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,210</i>)

>  <i>253. Mohoua ochrocephala </i>(Gmelin, 1789) <i>Yellowhead (Mohua) </i>... formerly widespread in South Island ... <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,67,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Yellowhead Mohoua ochrocephala </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Mohoua, mohoua houa, bush canary.</i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,277</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

The term was also sometimes applied to a gad used to chip off small pieces of rock. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),327</i>) [NOTES: Glossary of Waihi Mining Terms]

 <i>n.</i> light vegetable matter contamination

>  <b>BREMNER</b> <i>Moit</i> vegetable matter - leaves, fern, hay (but not seeds or burr) - in wool. <i>(1985,Woolscours of NZ,9</i>) [NOTES: Glossary of Old Wool Terms]

>  <i>moit ... NZ & Austral.</i> small pieces of vegetable matter in fleece; <i>moity a</i> [Eng. dial.] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

 <i>adj.</i>

 <i>n.</i> chewing gum [Mo-jo Chewing Gum.]
