 <i>n.</i> probably =tui rather than makomako (See [mocker](mob-mys.md#mocker),[mockie](mob-mys.md#mockie))

### moka
 
 <i>n.</i> ? dogfish; nf Williams; ?Brunner's hearing of si mako (=mango{o]) and crazy spg se

>  <b>BRUNNER</b> ...being a present in return for one of moka, or dog-fish. <i>(1847,Great Journey,54,1952 ed.</i>)

### mokai
 
 <i>n.</i> decoy

>  He is extremely useful as a 'mokai' - that is, a decoy. <i>(May 25, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,62</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> Possessing excellent powers of mimicry, and useful to the natives as a decoy-bird, the kaka is much sought after, and almost every native village has its `mokai'. <i>Ibid.</i> 89 \{1888 2nd edn 1/96\} I had been addressing a large meeting of natives in the Whare-runanga, or Council-house [at Rangitikei], on a matter of considerable political importance, and had been urging my views with all the earnestness that the subject demanded: immediately on the conclusion of my speech, and before the old chief, to whom my arguments were chiefly [sic] addressed, had time to reply, a Tui, whose netted cage hung to a rafter overhead, responded, in a clear emphatic way, `Tito!' (false). The circumstances naturally caused much merriment among my audience, and quite upset the gravity of the venerable old chief Nepia Taratoa. `Friend,' said he, laughing, `your arguments are very good; but my <i>mokai</i> is a very wise bird, and he is not yet convinced.' <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,48</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> There are several modes adopted for catching the Kaka, but the commonest ... is by means of a trained <i>mokai</i> or tame decoy. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/163</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> pet

>  The tiny, bent old lady saw Vincent as her mokai (pet). <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),104</i>)

### moke
 
 <i>n.</i> a horse, a donkey [orig. donkey cf.]

>  <b>HYDE</b> [The Gippo donkey riders] 'd go as fast as their little mokes would carry them.] <i>(1936,Passport to Hell,148-150</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> [On Central Otago naming in c1860s] Frankton was called [by W.G.Rees] after Mrs. Rees' Christian name, Francis; ... the Shotover from Shotover Park, where Mr. Rees' partner, Mr. Gammie, formerly resided; ... and Moke Creek after old Donald, the first horse which fed upon its grassy banks. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,193</i>) [NOTES: c1860s in 1898 Hocken Contributions 193]

>  <b>LAPHAM</b> I caught my moke ... <i>(1880,We Four,31</i>)

>  The country `sport' is an ingenious chap. A bush horse-owner, hauled up to explain the running of his moke, ... <i>(Mar 3, 1906,NZ Truth,37/66</i>)

>  <i>moke ... n</i>. old, decrepit horse. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> Seems they swapped over the bridles and saddles of the horses ... and hitched the mokes to different posts ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,105</i>)

>  <i>moke ... NZ & Austral. sl.</i> very inferior horse. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,484</i>) [NOTES: cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 727; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 315]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>moke</i> an inferior horse, or jocular reference to a horse, as in Frank S. Anthony's <i>Me and Gus</i>; elsewhere a donkey or ass. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

>  And fearing that the moke looked like it might be heading for the knacker's yard, he immediately sent out an SOS on national radio. <i>(Dec 8, 2002,Sunday Star Times,B17</i>)

>  Travellers had to either hobble or tether their mokes near the townships. <i>(Mar 11, 1924,Otago Witness,60</i>)

>  Those who try to get a King's Birthday present for themselves from the books over the Canterbury Park "dub" will need to remember that mud mokes are a necessity at this meeting. <i>(May 23, 1934,NZ Truth,3</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> THE MOKES, Colonial Transport Corps

>  <b>WILLIAM MORGAN</b> By-the-bye, let no gentleman or lady, if sending a letter to any person in this corps, address it to `the Mokes'. Such a derogatory title wil not be recognised ... The communication ... must be addressed to the `Colonial Transport Corps', or what is very much shorter, to the `C.T.C.' <i>(Dec 1863,Journal,135,1963 ed.</i>)

### Moke Valley gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a newly differentiated sub species of gecko, closely related to the Roy's Peak gecko, yet to be fully classified. [<i>Hoplodactylus sp. undet</i>]

>  Moke Valley gecko: Discovered in the South Island high country in 2002. A large robust gecko, which genetic tests have established is different from a similar species discovered near Wanaka in 1997. <i>(Aug 2, 2004,Dominion Post,A4</i>)

### moki
 
 <i>n.</i> New Zealand sea fishes of two families; the South Island species is blue-grey (occasionally called blue moki), the main North Island species is red (red moki or nanua). [early spellings mogge, moka etc.] (See [also painted moki](p-per.md#painted-moki),[moki shark](../M#moki-shark),[moki trumpeter](mob-mys.md#moki-trumpeter))

>  <b>KOHERE</b> The opening of the moki season always roused great interest and was attended with some ritual. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,29</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> We observ'd already that the principal fish caught by the seyne were Mullets ... large Conger eels, and a fish in shape much like the Bream but so large as to weigh five, six or seven pounds. It is blackish with thick lips and calld [sic] Mogge by the natives. [ed. fn Moki, <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i>.] <i>(Feb 1777,Journal,3/807,1967 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Moki. <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> ... The moki is one of the fishes which appear in the city practically all the year around. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/270</i>)

>  LATRIDOPSIS CILIARIS (Forster). Moki. A school fish usually taken in set-nets ... Common as far north as East Cape. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/115</i>) [NOTES: Phillipps Notes on the Edible Fishes]

>  Other fish such as kahawai (Arripis trutta <i>(Forster)), tamure (Pagrosomus auratus</i> (Forster)), snapper and moki (<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> (Forster)), are also caught. <i>Ibid.</i> 631Even fairly large fish, such as snapper and moki, may be caught in it. <i>Ibid.</i> 646 All kinds of fish are caught, from moki and tamure (snapper) to the smaller fish that abound about the rocks and reefs. <i>(1926,TrNZI,58/615</i>) [NOTES: (Buck)]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> (Forster). MOKI. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,39,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SPEIGHT</b> The Trumpeter is rare, but its close relative the Moki (<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> ) is secured in good numbers near the kelp, as also are Butterfish (<i>Coridodax pullus</i>), and Trevally (<i>Longirostrum platessa</i>). <i>(1927,Natural History of Canterbury,199</i>) [NOTES: 1927 Archey `Native Fishes of Canterbury' in Speight et al. Natural History of Canterbury 199]

>  <b>BEST</b> The following fish were caught with hook and line: araara, maomao, moki, mango, ngoiro, pakirikiri, kahawai, tamure, tarakihi, and warehou. The kanae, kehe, kopipiro, moho, nanu and other species were taken by net. <i>(1929,Fishing Methods,43</i>)

>  27. <i>Latris ciliaris</i> Forster. Moki <i>(1929,TrNZI,61/144</i>) [NOTES: (Young)]

>  <i>Latridopsis siliaris</i> : Moki. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,72</i>)

>  <i> ~ n</i>. the blue cod. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <i>Latridopsis siliaris</i> (Forster). <i>Moki. </i> <i>(1938,TrRSNZ,68/411</i>) [NOTES: Graham Fishes of Otago Harbour]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The above list of fish to be caught along the Canterbury coast has some notable omissions, as hoka (red cod) ... though I am not so sure about the rawaru (blue cod). Others omitted are the tarakihi, or terakihi, the moki, the komutumutu (trevalli), the puhaiwhakarua (red-jacket), koiro (conger eel), tuere (blind eel), whai (skate), and the koekohe [sic] (trumpeter). <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,62</i>)

>  <i>moki,</i> a fish (Latris ciliaris), a favourite market fish. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/145</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <i>moki</i>: edible fish. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Moki <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> (Bloch and Schn.) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,408</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 408]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> MOKI <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> (Bloch and Schneider) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,255</i>)

>  <b>PARROTT</b> The Maori name Tarakihi is generally pronounced `Teraki'. On the West Coast of the South Island it is sold under the name of `Moki'. <i>(1957,Sea Anglers' Fishes of NZ,117</i>)

>  ~ The Maori name of an edible New Zealand sea fish. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),262</i>)

>  <i>moki n<</i>. New Zealand fish; a raft [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> MOKI ... <i>Other names: Latridopsis ciliaris;</i> bastard trumpeter (Australia). Only the Maori name moki is in general use in New Zealand. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,252</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Moki .. .. moki .. .. <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> MOKI [<i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> ] ... Known as bastard trumpeter in Australia; only the Maori name is used in New Zealand. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,40</i>)

>  <b>NATUSCH</b> Family LATRIDAE. The moki, <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> (Fig. 69.1), is navy above, silver-grey along its sides, white-bellied; grows up to thirty inches, and is plentiful in Cook Strait and down to Stewart Island ... <i>(1967,Animals of NZ, The,222</i>)

>  No one ever felt embarrassed about going down to the beach to pick up a moki, because there were hundreds of them, and everyone had a right to a feed... He's been calling on the Waitangi Tribunal and the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries to protect the moki fishery. But.. the moki grounds haven't produced big catches or an abundant winter harvest since the early 1970s. <i>Ibid.</i> 88 A secret to eating moki is that there is a green bile in it which makes the body sour unless it is drained out of the mouth, by hanging the fish by its tail. <i>(Dec 1996,Mana,No. 11 : 86</i>)

>  Fish are plentiful, herrings.. moka,.. sometimes Habouka a large fish (which I tasted at Capt Simeon's)something between Cod and Grey Mullet, but not as good as either. <i>(1980,Journal of Henry Sewell,I : 158</i>) [NOTES: Original quote from 16 Feb. 1853]

>  The moki in the next tank is encased in soft blue velvet with fins of electric blue. <i>(Jun 15, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> Of the Fam. <i>Scioenoideae</i>, the <i>Latrisciliaris moki</i> and <i>mohiki</i>, it is distinguished by a triangular black patch on each side of the shoulder, and is one of the best of the New Zealand fish. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,624</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> ... the best [sea fish] are the Hapuka ..., the Moki, the Wharehou[ao-acute], the Patiki (a small plaice), the Kawai (a coarse common fish rising at the fly in tidal waters and sometimes dubbed salmon) a sort of mackerel, and some mullet, rock-cod, bastard-skate, and snapper. <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/122</i>)

>  The Hapuka, Baracouta, Ling, Snapper, Kawai, and Moki, ... and a few varieties of Rock fish, are the sea fish chiefly obtained ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/235</i>) [NOTES: (Travers)]

>  <b>HECTOR</b> The Moki (<i>Latris ciliaris</i>) is an abundant fish in the Wellington market, ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,108</i>)

>  It was no unusual event ...to land ... Moki, <i>Latris ciliaris</i>; Trumpeter,<i> Latris h ... ecateia;</i> Blue cod or Rock cod, <i>Percis colias</i>; and Red cod, <i>Lotella bacchus.</i> <i>(1872,Appendix Proc. Otago Prov. Council,39</i>) [NOTES: (Black Stewart Island)]

>  Rurima is famous for its fish; hapuka (<i>Oligorus gigas</i>), ... moki (<i>Latris ciliaris</i>), king-fish (Seriola lalandii), <i>wharehou (Neptomenus brama),</i> barracoota (<i>Thyrsites atun</i>), mackerel (<i>Scomber australasicus</i>), and the delicious little maomao ... <i>(1873,TrNZI,5/153</i>)

>  [Native or Common Name] Moki .. [Scientific Name] <i>Latris ciliaris</i> <i>(1877,TrNZI,9/485</i>) [NOTES: (P. Thomson, Otago)]

>  The fish for eating are hapuka,<i>Oligorus gigas;</i> rock cods, <i>Percis colias</i>; tarakihi, <i>Chilodactylus macropterus</i>; moki, <i>Latris ciliaris</i>, etc., etc. <i>(1885,TrNZI,17/198</i>) [NOTES: (Reischek)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Moki, n.</i> The Maori name for the <i>Bastard Trumpeter</i> (q.v.). of New Zealand, <i>Latris ciliaris</i> Forst., family<i> Cirrhitida. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,298</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> MOKI (<i>Latris ciliaris</i>). <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,67</i>)

>  Of the sea-fishes that are used as food in New Zealand, we have among the constant residents on all parts of our coast the hapuku, tarakihi, trevally, moki, aua, rock-cod, wrasse, and patiki; and while the snapper, mullet, and gurnet are only met with in the north, the trumpeter, butterfish, and red-cod are confined to the south. <i>(1892,NZ Official Handbook,167</i>)

>  1. <i>Cape Maria van Diemen</i>. - Kahawai, snapper, moki, trevally, king-fish, haku or yellowtail, rock-cod <i>Ibid.</i> 7. <i>Portland Island</i>. - Tarakihi, moki, frost-fish, barracouta, king-fish, john-dory, warehou, maomao, parrot-fish, flying-fish, smooth-hound, soldier fish. <i>Ibid.</i> 24. <i>Puysegur Point</i> - Hapuku, kahawai, trumpeter, moki, pig-fish, trevally, rock-cod, perch, butter-fish, red-cod, dog-fish <i>(1892,TrNZI,24/203</i>) [NOTES: (Thomson)]

>  Moki are very plentiful, but we only get a few trumpeter now and then. Of course, the cod and groper are plentiful, also butter-fish and barracouta. <i>Ibid.</i>... When we see the mackerel splashing along we know they are after squid; the mullet, latris, and parrot-fish are always after them, and even the moki and butter-fish join the hunt.) <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  Moki are very plentiful, but we only get a few trumpeter now and then. Of course, the cod and groper are plentiful, also butter-fish and barracouta. <i>Ibid.</i>... When we see the mackerel splashing along we know they are after squid; the mullet, latris, and parrot-fish are always after them, and even the moki and butter-fish join the hunt.) <i>(1896,TrNZI,28/53</i>) [NOTES: (Henry)]

>  Natives supplied us with a sort of sea-bream, large conger-eels, and a fish of five to six pounds weight called a <i>mogge</i> by the natives. <i>(1875,Cook's Third Voyage,38</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|oki, <i>s</i>. A fish so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,182</i>)

>  12'00 weight of Rays were taken ... in Evans's Bay besides Moki and other fish <i>(Nov 4, 1840,Journal of Ensign Best,261,1966 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> [wordlist] Moki a sea fish peculiar to the East Cape. <i>(Oct 7, 1842,Journal,2</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> Next to the Abuka in size ... is the <i>Moki,</i> varieties of which are of all sizes, the largest weighing about 30lbs. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),48</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 22. <i>Latris ciliaris</i>. - ... This fish is named `Moghee' by the natives of Dusky Bay. It is also an inhabitant of Queen Charlotte's Sound. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/209</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Richardson's Fish) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/209]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> A fish named Moghee by the natives of Dusky Bay. <i>(1843,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/209</i>)

>  <b>MAJORIBANKS</b> The moki, weighing about twenty pounds, is also tolerably good; and the kawai, or New Zealand salmon, though by some amateurs considered equal to ours, would no doubt be much prized by the slaves in Brazil, <i>(1844,Travels in NZ,82</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> The <i>Moki (latris ciliarus)</i> is a very fine-flavoured fish, somewhat resembling the dorey; it weighs from two to five or six pounds. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,160</i>)

>  <i>moki ... n</i>. N.Z. fish; raft [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MOKI </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Moki Trumpeter. (c) Other common names: None known. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,36</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> MOKI Four Mokis occur in our waters - the Red, the Ordinary and two very rare relatives. ... In Australia the Moki is known as the Bastard Trumpeter and the Red Moki as the Banded Morwong. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,161</i>)

>  <b>246</b> <i>BLUE MOKI</i> (Moki) <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> ... The blue moki is a large bottom-living fish, common in southern New Zealand ... <i>(1982,Collins Guide,1984</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Moki is an attractive blue-grey and silver fish of shallow coastal waters south of the Bay of Plenty. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,78</i>)

>  [species name] moki .. [scientific name] <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> .. [other name(s)] blue moki <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <i> moki 1 n. NZ</i> food-fish related to trumpeter. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,484</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 315]]

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] moki 1612-13, 1630 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

>  <b>SAM. STEPHENS</b> [Caught off Nelson] Moka, snapper and abuka ... <i>(1843,Letters & Journals,114</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> Fish are plentiful, herrings (a large kind not so good as English, Rock Cod, a sort of Whiting, moka, small soles Flounders, Eeels [sic] ... <i>(1853,Journal,1/158</i>)

>  <b>SHAW</b> Besides cod, there are seals, trumpeter, moki, barracouta, flounders, skate and warihou, of some of which the most fastidious gastronomist would only be too happy to make their acquaintance for the first time. <i>(1858,Gallop to the Antipodes, A,135</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The warehou, tarakihi, and gurnet are common in the north, and the moki, butterfish, and frost fish in the south, while the kahawai, hapuku (the groper of Dunedin), blue cod, barracouta, the little sea mullet, sometimes called the herring, and the flat fish are found all round the coast. <i>Ibid.</i> 73 -->1902 Drummond and Hutton Nature in NZ 73 The butterfish lives amongst kelp, or sea-weed, and is generally found where kelp is plentiful. Neither it nor the moki takes bait freely. <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,71</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> Snapper, <i>warehou</i>, butterfish, trumpeter, <i>moki,</i> flat-fish (<i>patiki</i>), and other fine sea fish were plentiful. makomako (bellbird) <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,107</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Then the Moki's sweet song peals out like a bell ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,307,1954 ed.</i>) [NOTES: a1917 Effie Studholme in 1940 Studholme Te Waimate (1954) 307]

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> Moki. <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i> ... The moki is one of the fishes which appear in the city practically all the year around. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/270</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> shorter form of mokihi, a raft made usually of bundles of dry flaxsticks or raupo [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  There was no boat, so they collected bundles of dry flax sticks, tied them together into a "moki," and crossed the river on that. <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,119</i>) [NOTES: Writing of 1940]

>  It [sc. a Maori expedition] consisted of twenty <i>mokis</i>, well freighted with pounamu. <i>(Jan 16, 1900,NZ Mines Record,233</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... a bundle of these rushes are often tied together, to anable a person to cross a stream. These ... are called mokis' [sic], are very buoyant, and resist saturation for some time. <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/218</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> In the absence of canoes, a quantity of dried bulrushes are fastened together, on which the native is enabled to cross a stream by sitting astride and paddling with his hands; these humble conveyances are called <i>moki</i>, and resemble those made use of by the Egyptians in crossing among the islands of the Nile. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/226</i>)

>  We crossed the river on mokies. ... By means of large mokis carrying upwards of a ton.... moki navigation. <i>(1858,AJHR,C-3-18</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The river we had passed on our <i>mokies</i>. [c1835] <i>(1889,Missionary Life and Work,26</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Moguey, n.</i> English corruption of <i>Mokihi</i> (q.v.). <i>(1898,Austral English,298</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> `Moguey, a Maori name for a raupo of flax-stick raft.' <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mokihi, moki, n.</i> Maori name for a raft; sometimes anglicised as <i>Moguey.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,299</i>)

>  <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,2/226</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The Maoris possessed a ruder craft ... in the raft (<i>moki</i> or <i>mokihi</i>) made of bulrush (<i>raupo</i>) and formed somewhat into the shape of a canoe. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,121</i>)

>  <i>moki 2 n. NZ hist.</i> (also <i>mokihi</i>) Maori raft made of bundles of flax-sticks or bulrushes. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,484</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]



 
 <i>n.</i> a wood

>  Over the back of the principal mirrors.. are six panels of totara knot, moki, pukaki, and figwood, studded with moki buttons. <i>(Oct 4, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

### moki ground
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KOHERE</b> The white settlers there have their own moki ground where they can fish, and eat their lunch and drink their beer without fear of breaking the immutable laws of the moki. <i>(1951,Autobiography of a Maori,30</i>)

### moki kelp
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  By the same token, a New Zealander wandering along any circumpolar coast on the island of South Georgia in the South Atlantic, Kerguelen in the Southern Indian Ocean, and Tierra del Fuego might not catch a moki, but he would have a good chance of finding moki kelp. <i>(1971,NZ Heritage,1/116</i>)

### moki trumpeter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MOKI </i> (a) Scientific name: <i>Latridopsis ciliaris</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Moki Trumpeter. (c) Other common names: None known. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,36</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### mokihi
 
 <i>n.</i> a flax raft (See [moki](mob-mys.md#moki))

>  <b>MAY</b> Instructed by their Maori companions, Heaphy and Brunner had begun to learn how to live off the country; they knew about fording rivers, building <i>mokihis</i>, snaring wekas and scavenging for sea-foods along the beach. <i>(1962,West Coast Gold Rushes,37</i>)

>  The only passage was by flax korori raft or mokihi. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  <b>short</b> Natives till busy cutting `raupo'. In the afternoon they commenced building the canoes, or rather rafts, which they call `mokihi'. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,203</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 12 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 203]

>  <b>1958</b> Went to look at a mogihi of the Moeraki natives in the afternoon when it cleared up <i>(Apr 8, 1849,Torlesse Papers,73</i>)

>  Our raft, or <i>moki</i>, at the Karamea river was 22 feet in length by 5 in breadth. [cf pp 217-8 for a detailed description of the building of a moki] <i>(1846,Notes of an Expedition to Kawatiri and Araura</i>) [NOTES: 1846 Heaphy Expedit to Kawatiri in 1959 Taylor Early Travellers 218]

>  <b>MONRO</b> The method of crossing [the Waitaki] is upon a mogi, which is a floating body, somewhat in the shape of a boat, made of bundles of bulrushes tied together, and strengthened by the flowering stem of the flax, called koradi. <i>(Jul 29, 1840,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson Examiner July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 240]

>  <b>MANTELL</b> When we reached the river's edge ... we found that Wahinei had crossed on a Korari mokihi and was repairing a large one of raupo. <i>(Oct 24, 1848,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1848 Oct 24 Mantell Diary in 1947 Stephenson Maori and Pakeha 128]

>  Went to look at a mogihi of the Moeraki natives. <i>(Apr 8, 1849,Torlesse Papers,73,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NAIRN & STEPHEN</b> ... the Natives getting Korari [flax stalks] for, a moki [raft] to be ready on our return. <i>(Jan 18, 1852,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1852 Jan 18 Journal of C.J. Nairn and W. Stephen in 1947 Beattie The Pioneers Explore Otago 27]

>  ... they constructed rafts made of flax and brushwood, called by the natives `mugies', and sailed across [the flooded river] on them, ... <i>(Mar 4, 1856,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1856 Mar 4 Hepburn Letter from Otago in Journal (1934) 158]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> They ventured upon [Waiau River] in maggies or rafts of flax stalks. <i>(1858,Reconnaissance Survey  S.Otago</i>) [NOTES: 1858 Thomson Reconn. Surv. Sthn Otago in 1959 Taylor Early Travs. 340]

>  ...he was enabled to get (after navigating the lake on a moggie for two days) to a point where he obtained a view of the northern end of the lake... <i>(Mar 24, 1860,Otago Witness</i>) [NOTES: 1860 March 24 Otago Witness (guotes in 1947 Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 55-56.]

>  <b>LAUPER</b> ... it would take too long to make a mogie; ... <i>(1863,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1863 Lauper in 1960 Pascoe (ed) Over the Whitcombe Pass 50]

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> [We placed] a buoy consisting of a portion of a canoe or mogey which had been found on the beach. [? mistaken or transferred meaning; - or is it non-defining on canoe] <i>(1870,Martin's Bay Settlement,5</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> ... we began to collect dry flax-sticks out of the swamp, ... and carrying them in bundles to the bank of the river, where the<i> mogueys</i> or rafts were to be launched when completed. [fn] Maori name for a raupo or flax-stick raft. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> For the benefit of the unlearned ... let me here explain that a `Mokihi' is constructed of `Koradies'; - <i>Anglice</i>:- the flowering stalks of the flax, - three faggots of which, lashed [83] firmly in a point to the small ends, and expanded by a piece of wood at the stern, constitute the sides and bottom of the frail craft, ... <i>Ibid.</i> 115 ... a `mokihi' is constructed of koradies ... three faggots of which lashed firmly in a point at the small ends, and expanded by a piece of wood at the stern, constitute the sides and bottom of the frail craft which, propelled by paddle, furnishes sufficient means of transpor for a single individual. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,3/iii/82-83,1889,1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The natives build ... `mogueys' of [raupo.] <i>(1874,Boy Colonists,36</i>)

>  Arriving at the outlet ..., we found that the mokihi, or craft, made of flax-sticks, put together by a large party of diggers, was on the other side, ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,96</i>)

>  <b>REID</b> It had been used by the diggers as the keel of their `mogey' <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,53</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Accordingly a raft, constructed with bundles of korari sticks tied together, and called mokihi by the natives, was launched with the next flood tide, and upon this one of the party ... started for the kaik. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> There are five different kinds of rafts. ... They are the Oblong raft, the A-shaped, the Moki [mokihi], the half Moki and the catamaran. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,218</i>) [NOTES: (c1899) in 1957 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas 218]

>  <b>MOFFAT</b> [I] next day started building a moki to sail down to the outlet [of Lake Rotoroa]. I collected twelve big bundles of flax-sticks during the day, ... <i>(1898,Adventures by Sea and Land,68,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CHALMERS</b> [In 1853] Flax was abundant, so we made a big korari raft. ... We got on to the raft, or `mokihi' and paddled down the river so rapidly I could hardly credit our speed. <i>(1910,Letter</i>) [NOTES: (1910 Letter of Nathaniel Chalmers) quoted in H. Beattie Pioneers Explore Otago 37]

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i> moguey</i>. A coll. corruption of Maori<i> moki</i> (or <i>mokihi</i>), a raft: mid C. 19-20. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,526</i>)

>  <i>mokihi n</i>. a raft made of dried bullrushes. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> He ... would return home by means of a raupo mokihi (raft), floating down the Waiaki to Glenavy, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,128,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STEVENSON</b> Mokihis were used up to about 1902, by Maori shearers and shed-hands returning from Station Peak to Waimate via Glenavy. <i>(1947,Maori and Pakeha in North Otago,110fn</i>)

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> He found there that Weller's whaling station on Maturata Island was almost abandoned and the natives away on a mutton-birding expedition. However, one of his party, on a mokihi (raft) constructed of korari sticks floated up the lovely gorge with the flood tide, and returned with two boats manned by natives from the Maori kaik beyond the gorge. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,3</i>)

>  <b>HENIZ</b> His party ... floated down stream on a `mohiki', or raft built of flax sticks. <i>(1952,Prospecting for Gold,6</i>)

>  <b>MIKAERE</b> ... he would help travellers on the lake [Tekapo] by sending a fair wind to push their mokihi (reed boats) in the right direction. <i>(1988,Te Maiharoa,75</i>)

>  <b>HEINZ</b> The [at the Poerua River, West Coast] we found that the mokihi craft made of flax sticks, put together by a large party of diggers, was on the other side. <i>(1974,Bright Fine Gold,62</i>) [NOTES: 1866 June 9 Von Haast in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 62]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> ... he ... set to work to construct a big mohiki raft on Maori lines, with the intention of going up the lake and seeing what was round the corner. <i>(1947,Pioneers Explore Otago,48</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> The <i>mokihi</i> is formed of bundles of rushes, bound tightly together in the form of a boat. ... The <i>mokihis</i> are first built twenty or thirty miles from the mouth ... <i>(Jan 17, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 17 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 14]

>  ... a suggestion to build a mokihi (flax stick raft) was quickly put into execution. <i>(Apr 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,23</i>)

>  <b>LAMBERT</b> He ... sent ... his men over the hill to cut <i>raupo</i> wherewith they made <i>mokihis</i> ... <i>(1936,Pioneering Reminiscences of Old Wairoa,178</i>) [NOTES: (here made of raupo rather than korari [????])]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> He ... would return home by means of a raupo mokihi (raft), floating down the Waitaki to Glenavy, ... <i>(1940,Te Waimate,128,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> These were not the usual mokihi rafts built to descend the larger rivers from their headwaters, but were regarded as temporary canoes. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,39</i>)

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> Went to look at a mogihi of the Maeraki natives. [Fn. Mokihi, a canoe-shaped craft made by lashing bundles of dry flax sticks together]. <i>(1958,Torlesse Papers,73</i>) [NOTES: 1849 citation]

### mokihi/moki
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a raft made of rushes or flax-flower stems. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  We had to cross the flooded Mataura in a 'moggy' made of bulrushes. <i>(1855,BLANK ENTRY,11</i>) [NOTES: in Beattie (1909) Pioneer Recollections I]

>  .. he came down that lake [Wakatipu] to where Queenstown is now on a moki, a raft of flax stems. <i>(Mar 1895,NZ Farmer,70</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> The Maori crossed deep rivers like the Waitaki on mokihis (rafts) which were skilfully constructed of bundles of bundles of dry raupo or korari (flax) sticks. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,36</i>)

>  <b>HEPBURN</b> On Wednesday morning they constructed rafts made of the flax and brushwood, called by the natives 'mugies' and sailed across on them, swimming their horses ... <i>(1856,Journal,158</i>) [NOTES: Journal (1934)]

>  <b>MONEY</b> .. we began to collect dry flax-sticks out of the swamp .. carrying them in bundles to the bank of the river, where the mogueys or rafts were to be launched when completed. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,52</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> During the siege of the island, mokihi or rush-rafts were used, and all sides of the pa attacked. <i>(1921,Tutira,83</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> He usually went on to Station Peak after finishing here and would return home by means of a raupo mokihi (raft) floating down the Waitaki to Glenavy. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,128</i>)

>  <b>WOODHOUSE</b> They wished to return home as soon as possible; there was no boat, so they collected bundles of dry flax sticks, tied them together into a 'moki' and crossed the river on that. [moggy, moguey, mugy] <i>(1988,Tales of Pioneer Women,119</i>)

### Mokikinui spud
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>REID</b> They will show ... specimens of alluvial gold, heavy, water-worn flattened nuggets, `Mokikinui Spuds' they fondly call them, which miners dig out sometimes even with their sheath knives. <i>(1884,Rambles on the Golden Coast,72</i>)

### mokimok
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [makomako](m-mar.md#makomako))

### mokimoki
 
 <i>n.</i> [also moki]

>  <i>moki(moki)</i>, a fern (Polypodium pustulatum), used by the Maori for scenting oil, and highly valued for this purpose. <i>(Jun 1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>)

>  <b>DEWAR</b> Fern and moki and fuchsia, laced through with lawyer bramble, grew dense and hopeless at their feet. But beyond was the native bush [in Southland] <i>(1953,Chaslands,12</i>) [NOTES: [=? makomako wineberry; or fern]]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Mokimoki a species of fern grows profusely on trees, near streams or moist places,it is a pretty little fern, <i>[Glossary</i> 119 <i>Polypodium postulatum</i> (fragrant fern) or <i>Cyophorus serpens?] </i> <i>(1844,NZ Journal,110,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> The gum of the taramea was collected at early dawn; and with it were mixed the fronds of moki-moki (the fern <i>Doodia caudata</i>) and of the piri-piri ... [to make a sweet-smelling sachet]. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,322,1940 p. 341 ed.</i>)

>  <i>mokimoki</i> or <i>moki</i>, a fern (Polypodium pustulatum), used by the Maori for scenting oil, and highly valued for this purpose. A hill in Napier was named Puke-mokimoki because this fern was abundant there; but the hill was levelled to fill a gully and made a recreation reserve in the town. <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|oki m|oki; A plant so called; <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,102</i>)

>  Climbing ferns are much less dominant in New Zealand bush than in tropical rainforest, but mokimoki (<i>Microsorum scandens), Rumohra adiantiformis</i> and thread fern (<i>Blechnum filiforme</i>) are common throughout, while jointed fern (<i>Arthropteris tenella</i>) and mangemange (</i>Lygopodium articulatum</i>) are characteristic of the warmer northern forest. <i>(2001,NZ Geographic,No., 49/76</i>)

### Mokitap track
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>WRIGHT-SINCLAIR</b> In May that year the sensational murders on the Mokitap track took place. <i>(1981,Man of the World,204</i>)

### moko
 
 <i>n.</i> makomako

>  The large membranous leaves of our moko scrub (<i>Aristotelia racemosa</i>), afford a very good example of this phenomenon. <i>(1901,NZ Journal of Education,3/86</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> tattooing on the face or body [cf tattoo v, tatooed is used by Earle NZ 1832 (1966) p71] (See [cf tattoo v](t-ten.md#tattoo),[tatooed is used by Earle NZ 1832 (1966) p71](../T#tatooed-is-used-by-earle-nz-1832-(1966)-p71))

>  <b>BAKER</b> Sean watched the group take in his moko and his dog's head tee short. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,87</i>)

>  As well as more traditional Maori art pursuits like whakairo (carving) and ta moko (tattoo), the open day featured... <i>(Jul 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,9</i>)

>  tattoo - em|ook [e moko] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,111</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 111 see also 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

>  <b>POLACK</b> He was entirely marked with the <i>moko</i>, or tattoo, and moved with the pride and dignity which a New Zealand chief delights in assuming. <i>(1838,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/171</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> The tattoo, or <i>`moko'</i>, which is the native name, is done either with the sharp bone of a bird, or with a small chisel called <i>uhi. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/33</i>)

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> It may not be out of place here to observe, that the tattoo or `moko', as it is termed in native language, is neither intended to constitute a distinctive mark between different tribes, nor to denote rank, as has been variously stated. <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,16</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> ... but the grand ornament of all was the<i> moko</i> or tattoo; this was in general use, all ranks adopted it; a <i>papatea</i>, or plain face, was a term of reproach. <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,319,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> First among the New Zealand list of disfigurations is tattooing, a Polynesian word signifying a repetition of taps, but which term is unknown in the language of the New Zealanders; <i>moko</i> being the general term for the tattooing on the face, and <i>whakairo</i> for that on the body. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/74</i>)

>  It only so far denotes rank as showing that the possessor of a handsome moko must have had the wherewithal to well remunerate the artist. <i>(1894,TrNZI,26/436</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Moko, n</i>. the system of tattooing practised by the Maoris. See <i>Tattoo</i>. It is not a fact - as popularly supposed - that the `moko' was distinctive in different families; serving, as is sometimes said, the purpose of a coat-of-arms. The `moko' was in fact all made on the same pattern - that of all Maori carvings. Some were more elaborate than others. The sole difference was that some were in outline only, some were half filled in, and others were finished in elaborate detail. <i>(1898,Austral English,299</i>)

>  <i>Mo&#769;.ko&#769;.</i>, (m&omacr.&#769;.ko.&#769;.), n. [Maori.] The kind of tattooing practiced among the higher ranks of the Maoris. ... <i>Mo&#769;.ko, v. t.</i> To tattoo in the Maori manner. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2027</i>)

>  <i>moko</i>, tattooing on the face or body: - `their bodies and faces were more marked with the black stains which they call Amoco; they had a broad spiral on each buttock,' <i>Cook, Voyages,</i> 26 Nov., 1769 (Ed. 3, 1785), vol. 3, p. 253. "but the grand ornament of all was the <i>moko</i>, or tattoo; this was in general use, all ranks adopted it; a <i>papatea</i>, or plain face, was a term of reproach."<i> Taylor, Ika</i>, Ed. 2 (1870) 319. `The term tattoo is not known in New Zealand; and the name given to the decorative marks in question ... in in New Zealand moko.' <i>Robley, Moko</i> (1896), 3. `The native name for tattooing ... is <i>moko</i>, the verb being <i>ta</i>, or <i>ta moko</i>, as it is usually employed.' <i>Best, The Maori </i>(1924), vol. 2, 546; (<i>ta</i>, a prefix having apparently a causative force similar to that of <i>whaka</i>.) <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/161</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Te Rauparaha after denied knowledge of the full import of the document to which he had affixed his <i>moko </i>... <i>Ibid</i> 81 Nias also sailed around New Zealand to secure signatures or `<i>moko</i> marks' of the different Maori chiefs. <i>(1941,Numismatic History of NZ,28</i>)

>  <b>ZIMMERMAN</b> She even told me, once, that when she left the service, she thought she would have a <i>moko</i> applied to her chin, the delicate black scrollwork tattooing that one sees so often on the faces of the older women. <i>(1946,Where the People Sing,59</i>)

>  ~ The system of tattooing practised by the Maoris of New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),263</i>)

>  <b>HIL;LIARD</b> She eyed the lipstick with deep suspicion and rubbed the moko on her chin. <i>(1960,Maori Girl,36</i>)

>  <i>moko n</i> -s [Maori]<i> 1</i> : the Maori system of tattooing<i> 2</i> : a Maori tattoo consisting of pigment rubbed into spiral grooves made in the skin with a small implement resembling an adz <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1454</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> SOME MAORI WORDS ... <i>moko</i> tattoo <i>(1963,Piece of Land, A,192</i>)

>  <b>SHADBOLT</b> Some of the really old Maori women, with <i>moko</i> tattooed on their chins and shark-tooth necklaces around their wrinkled necks, didn't shake hands with me; they rubbed noses in the old Maori fashion instead. <i>(1965,Among the Cinders,24</i>)

>  <i>~ n.</i> Maori system of tattooing [Maori]. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),620</i>)

>  <i> moko ... n. <i>Maori system of tattooing [Maori]. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <i>(1968,Landfall,12/252</i>)

>  <i>(1969,Landfall,13/317</i>)

>  moko: chin tattoo, formerly worn by women. <i>(1973,Brown Man's Burden,141</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> In the dull glow of the heater she began to undress, ... `Let's go away then,' he said, and the glowing element caught the lines of his face in a pattern, a <i>moko</i> almost, of resignation. <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,39</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> moko <i>facial tattoo </i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,189</i>)

>  <b>ADCOCK</b> moko <i>facial tattoo </i> <i>(1982,Contemporary NZ Poetry,143</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> I approached Lees' General Store. The kuia no longer sit against the corrugated iron fence alongside, ... dribbling on their moko. <i>(1985,G'day Country,62</i>)

>  <i>moko n</i>. system or pattern of (esp. facial) tattoo practised by the Maoris. [M] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,484</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 315]]

>  `They had a much larger quantity of<i> amoca</i>[sic] or black stains upon their bodies and faces. They had almost universally a broad spiral on each buttock, and many had their thighs almost entirely black, small lines only being left untouched, so that they looked like striped breeches. In this particular, I mean the use of <i>amoca</i>, almost every tribe seems to have a different custom.' <i>(Nov 22, 1769,Banks Journal,203,1896 ed.</i>)

>  ... they varied very much in the quantity and parts of the body on which this <i>Amoco</i> as they call it was placed. <i>(Mar 1770,Banks Journal,2/14,1962 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> A Vocabulary of the Language of New Zealand. [...] Egooree, <i>A dog</i>. Teyka, <i>Fish. </i> Emaho, <i>Tataow. </i>.. Ekeeno[e2-grave], <i>Bad.</i> <i>(1770,Journal,126, 127</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> ... one or two Chiefs, who were distinguished by the marks (Amoko) on their face, and by the respectful deference which was shewn them by the Emokies ( i.e., the working men) that paddled the canoes ... <i>(Nov 1793,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1793 Nov King's Journal in 1908 McNab Historical Records 2/546]

>  <b>COLLINS</b> A-mo-ko, <i>The marks on their face and different parts of their bodies. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>SAV AGE</b> The operation of tattooing, which the natives call amoco, is usually performed in the following manner. <i>Ibid.</i> 77 Amoco .. Tattooing. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),46</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The tattooing (<i>moko</i>) of a man's face in late times followed a pattern almost invariable. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,257</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The tattooing (<i>moko</i>) of a man's face in late times followed a pattern almost invariable. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,257</i>)

>  <b>ROBLEY</b> The term tattoo is not known in New Zealand; and the name given to the decorative marks in question ... is in New Zealand <i>moko</i>. <i>(1896,Moko,3</i>)

>  <b>BEST</b> The native term for tattooing is <i>moko</i>, the verb being <i>ta</i>, or <i>ta mako</i>, as it is generally employed. [ta causative prefix, like <i>whaka </i>(Andersen); or<i> ta </i>`cut'; `tattoo' cf tattoo]. <i>(1924,Maori As He Was, The,2/546</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> <i>(1926,Tutira</i>) [NOTES: OED]

>  <i>~ n.</i> tattooing as practised by the Maoris. [Maori] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> The Maoris were noted for their remarkable art of `Moko', or tattooing, which was designed to clothe as well as decorate the body. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),70</i>)

>  <b>MALCOLM</b> Miraka Kino did not feel justified in giving a European girl her complete moko. <i>(1982,Where it all Began,140</i>)

>  An anonmymous $3,000 donation enabled the Moko Museum to struggle on. <i>(Aug 4, 2004,Capital Times,5</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> I was barely a man, then, the moko still fresh on my face. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,135</i>) [NOTES: (Paula Morris)]

>  Although tags beside the railway tracks in Christchurch may imitate the tagging in the Colosseum, increasingly Pacific cultural elements - moko and Samoan motifs - are emerging as a distinctive New Zealand style. <i>(Jan 20, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),51</i>)

>  Once frowned upon by pakeha missionaries and colonial governments, the proud ancient art of ta moko has now gone out into the world as part of the Maori cultural renaissance. <i>(Jul 2004,AA Directions,Spring/27</i>)

>  I met an old lady with a moko on her chin... <i>(Nov 12, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),104</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> "Let's go away then," he said, and the glowing element caught the lines of his face in a pattern, a <i>moko</i> almost, of resignation. <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,39</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> For this purpose, the ingenuity of Shunghi furnished a ready contrivance; and that chief drawing upon the deeds a complete representation of the <i>amoco</i>, or tattooing of [193] Gunnah's countenance, to which the latter set his mark, it served as the ratifying symbol of the agreement. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/329 To tattoo the skin Amoko <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),192</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The chisel was constantly dipped in a [172] liquid made of soot from a particular tree, and afterwards mixed with water, which communicates the blackness or, as they call it, the amoko (moko). I observed proud flesh rising in some part of the breech which had been cut almost one month before. The operation is too painful to bear the whole tattooing at one time. They appear to be several years before they are perfectly tattooed. <i>(1819,Letters & Journals,171,1932 ed.</i>)

>  <b>CRUISE</b> (notes) The word tattooing has been used in this journal because its meaning is generally understood; but the lines upon the faces and persons of the New Zealanders are universally designated amoco. <i>(Mar 9, 1820,Journal,209,1957 ed.</i>)

>  M|oko <i>s.</i> A tatooed, or marked face: <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,182</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i>

>  With a smile that moko'd her aged face/ and warm gentle lines she weaved her/ plaintive fingers through my hair... <i>(Nov 1973,NZ PEN Gazette,No. 91/33</i>) [NOTES: (Kona Te Auhere's poem `He Kuia')]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> abbreviation of mokopuna [see also great-moko]

>  My moko said to me 'we don't want you to die.' <i>(Oct 20, 2005,NZ Herald,-</i>)

>  We will miss you Koro, from all your mokos, Gaylene, Ngahiraka, Hiniona, Jessarney, Jason, Zhane, Kelly, James, Hakaria, Brendon and Huimai. <i>(Sep 2, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,44</i>)

>  A handsome baby brother for Nicholas and gorgeous moko of Nanny Val and Nanny Peggy. <i>(Nov 1, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,10</i>)

>  Your loving wife Peggy, Debbie, Lesley, Ronnie, Cameron, partners and mokos. <i>(Sep 16, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,46</i>)

>  I know my moko and I know he didn't kill anyone. <i>(Jun 4, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Eeeeee. Fancy mokos of our own! Remember when Kiri's Riki brought her mokos last Christmas - that little Hemi - he loved you, Matai. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,136</i>)

>  She won't kick the bucket till her favourite moko gets home, Mrs Pippin. <i>(Nov 20, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>) [NOTES: (File Mourning)]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> logo; tattoo [[Ma. <b>moko</b> logo, tattoo, lizard, caterpillar] cf. P.M. Ryan Dict. Modern Maori (1995) 144.]

>  Dr Brash has illustrated that, in his hands, the only place he would like to see the moko is in the out-house. Meanwhile Maori, through a heady mixture of sovereignty activists and academic purists appear to want to put the moko in the microwave and turn it up full bore... <i>(Feb 26, 2004,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... he stoutly denied [the theft], saying he had bought it from a store up the river, but I showed him my moko or signature on the linen, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),2/92</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> `I, Toawick, are now become Rangatera or Chief of these Southern territories', and he affixed his moko (the tattoo marks on his face) to the deed. <i>(1940,Port Molyneux,37</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Their [sc. old people's] lines and wrinkles [21] were their own and no one else's, as if, without any effort, they had grown their own personal moko. <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,20</i>)

>  <b>BLAIR</b> She sings her simple lays / Like moko on the tree. <i>(1889,Lay of the Old Identities, The,51</i>)

### moko artist
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  It is more than half-a-century since a `moko' artist plied his trade among the warriors ... <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,26</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Hauauru, a splendid old chieftain with a face deeply engraved by the <i>moko</i>-artist's chisel ... <i>(1935,Hero Stories of NZ,161</i>)

### moko skink
 
 <i>n.</i> a small skink with an unusually long tail, found in coastal areas on the mainland and many islands from Bay of Plenty northwards. [<i>Oligosoma moco</i> aka Northern brown skink - see http://www.reptilepark.co.nz/nzlizard.htm.]

>  The tiny population of moko skinks (Oligosoma moco) was found at the council-run Shakespear [sic] Regional Park on the Whangaparaoa peninsula, about 30 minutes from central Auckland. ... Moko skinks were common throughout the upper North Island before Europeans settled in New Zealand but the introduction of rats and mice saw some skink populations plummet. ... Moko skinks are mostly confined to islands such as the Hauraki Gulf's Tiritiri Matangi. <i>(Sep 12, 2005,NZ Herald,A11</i>)

>  A juicy cicada will probably last this moko skink - one of our smaller lizards - several days. (Moko is the Maori word for lizard, as well as tattoo). <i>(Jul 2000,NZ Geographic,47/71</i>) [NOTES: Date 0 / 7 / 2000]

>  Rare moko skinks, which have been wiped out on mainland NZ have discovered at Shakespear Regional Park. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.sossi.org.nz/news-and-events.html]

>  Local iwi are also concerned about the destruction of shellfish beds and the relocation of the culturally significant moko skiink. <i>(Apr 2009,North & South,58</i>)

### moko-piko
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Moko-piko, <i>Bidwill. Libocedrus Doniana. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

### moko/moki
 
 <i>n.</i> short for makomako (bellbird) (See [cf. mockie](mob-mys.md#mockie))

>  I can hear the mokis and other birds singing outside on the trees. <i>(Nov 18, 1903,Otago Witness,68</i>)

>  Melody awoke us all at 5 o'clock - tuis, mokos, kokakos, or crow.. all piping forth in the beautiful sunshine. <i>(Mar 6, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

### mokoed
 
 <i>adj.</i> [also as moko-ed, moko'd]

>  Tama Iti giggles, a crooked-toothed grin lighting up his intricately moko-ed face. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),64</i>)

>  But it was Gibbs who came through, prompting that extraordinary shot in the New Zealand Herald of the Auckland millionairess book-ended by the two moko-ed activists, Te Kaha and Tame Iti, with whom she had negotiated. <i>(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  .. a mokoed member of the Aupouri iwi ... <i>(Nov 7, 2006,NZ Herald,E3</i>)

>  A turbaned Sikh rests on a bench seat grooming his beard. There is an African woman. A mokoed Maori. <i>(Jan 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 61/70</i>)

>  <b>ST BRUNO</b> ... Mr. M'Fergus watched blankly as three heavily-built well-<i>moko'd</i> Maoris ... climbed lightly up the ship's ladder. [15] A little group apart, the <i>moko'd </i>warriors conferred ... <i>(1952,Hellbuster, The,14</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> - the moko'd elders/ Caught the message - 'Clothes are what count most' <i>(1979,Collected Poems,250,1962 ed.</i>)

### Mokohinau gecko
 
 <i>n.</i> a large species of gecko found exclusively in the Mokohinau Islands east of Northland. [<i>Hoplodactylus "Mokohinau gecko"</i>]

>  Mokohinau gecko: .. Description: Brown, grey or olive with paler bands, blotches, chevrons or stripes that may be drab or bright. <i>(2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.conservation.org.nz/conservation/native-animals/reptiles-and-frogs/species-information/atlas-of-the-amphibians-and-reptiles-of-nz/electronic-atlas/atlas-details/?SpeciesID=13247]

>  Fanal Island Mokohinau gecko, Duvaucel's gecko. An area representative of 'high lizard abundance' <i>(2007,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.sanctuariesnz.org/documents/Bell2007.pdf power point entry]

>  .. Fanal Island, Mokohinau group (80-120 covers; Mokohinau and Duvaucel's geckos present). <i>(Oct 15, 2009,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.landcareresearch.co.nz/research/wildlifeecol/herpetology/projects.asp]

### mokomokai
 
 <i>n.</i> preserved and usually tattooed and smoked heads

>  With their bright colours, airbrush strokes and puzzling images of birds, targets and moko mokai, these new paintings carry something of that surprise first seen a decade ago. <i>(Sep 23, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  Entertainer Prime, of Hawera, went to New York in March, to begin negotiations for the return of 33 tattooed Maori heads, or mokomokai... Proceeds will go to the Mokomokai Education trust, of which Sir Paul is patron... "I have an interest in what Dalvanius is trying to do, repatriating the moko-mokai [sic]... Prime has been giving talks about the mokomokai. <i>(Oct 9, 1998,Dominion,14</i>)

>  [Hdg] Dalvanius sings a mokomokai message... The Hawera entertainer went to New York in March to begin negotations for the return of 33 tattooed Maori heads, or mokomokai. <i>(Oct 9, 1998,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  The two male heads, or mokomokai, were discovered by Labour list MP Dover Samuels during a recent visit to Britain. <i>(Jun 24, 1998,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Putaranui lamented that Maori were more energetic in condemning a Texan-based entrepreneur who was using the world wide web to market reproduction mokomokai - preserved heads. <i>(Dec 1999,North & South,36</i>)

>  His use of mokomokai as a generic term for mummified heads is offensive to Maori anyway, because mokai means slave and, in fact, it was the nobility whose heads were most lovingly preserved after death. <i>(Aug 20, 2006,Sunday Star Times,C7</i>)

>  The five skulls.. arrived in New Zealand early yesterday with Moko Mokai Education Trust director Dalvanius Prime and tohunga Sonny Keepa. <i>(Apr 20, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The trade in moko mokai began in the late 1770s. At that time, it was a Maori custom to preserve the heads of kinsfolk and chiefly enemies using embalming techniques that preserved the hair, skin and moko design. <i>(Mar 10, 2001,Dominion,21</i>)

>  Strangest was the French trip, written up by the media as a mission by Gibbs and Te Kaha to repatriate a mokomokai, or shrunken head. <i>(Oct 17, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),21</i>)

>  Panekiri wears its pitted and etched rockface like a form of mokomokai, and glares down upon a number of well-hidden taonga and historical sites pertaining to the period. <i>(Oct 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

### mokomoko
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a lizard [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  The mokomoko, one of our largest lizards, used to be common throughout Central Otago, but habitat destruction and predators - mainly rats, stoats and cats - have whittled numbers down to an estimated 1000 to 3000, mostly in isolated populations around Macraes Flat and the Lindis area. <i>(Apr 2008,North & South,27</i>)

>  <b>LEE & KENDALL</b> M|oko m|oko; A small lizard. <i>(1820,Grammar & Vocabulary of the Language of NZ,182</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> <i>(1840,New Zealand  (Polack)</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 1. <i>Tiliqua Zelandica</i>. Harmless Lizard. <i>Polack</i> N.Z. i. 317 ... `Is called <i>Moko-Moko</i> by the natives of Cook's Strait, ...' <i>Dieffenbach. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/202</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/202]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Moko-moko, n</i>. ... (2) Maori name for the lizard, <i>Lygosoma ornatum</i>, Gray, or <i>Lygosoma moko</i>, Durn. and Bib. 1820 [Lee and K] <i>(1898,Austral English,299</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> I also showed him a remarkable [140] lizard called a <i>mokomoko</i> that lived in a hole in the rocks near the beach. <i>(1948,Taina,139</i>)

>  <i>moko-moko n</i> -s [Maori] : a common small lizard (<i>Lygosoma moco</i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1454</i>)

>  <i>Mo&#769;.ko-mo&#769;.ko</i> (m&omacr.&#769;.ko.-m&omacr.&#769;.ko.), <i>n</i>. [Maori.]<i> (Zoo.l.) </i>A species of lizard <i>(Lygosoma ornatum</i> or <i>L. moko</i>), formerly very abundant in New Zealand but now extinct in the South Island. <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2027</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>adj.</i> .

 <i>n.</i> grandchild [http://translator.kedri.info/translate.php]

>  <b>9</b> I cherish my mokopuna. Why can't he extend his feelings to mine? <i>(Dec 13, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  His Worship the Mayor Sir Barry Curtis, Deputy Mayor Anne Candy, Manukau City Councillors, City Manager Colin Dale, Director of City Services Wayne Goodley and council staff, extend all their aroha and awhinatia to John's whanau, especially his hoa wahine Raiha, tamariki and mokopuna. <i>(Dec 6, 2005,NZ Herald,C8</i>)

>  I've had the odd offer - do I have any mokapuna that need marrying off, because they've got spare sons and daughters? <i>(Dec 26, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),19</i>)

>  She sits in a glamorous black trouser suit, hot-pink earrings swinging wildly, intermittently answering her relentless cell phone, "kia ora, kia ora, my dear" - asks after "the baby, er the mokopuna" in her husky melodious voice. <i>(Jun 1992,Metro,99</i>)

>  She was worried about the mokopuna 'cos she was having an asthma attack. <i>(Jun 6, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),23</i>)

>  It was clear from the way she began her replies with hissed phrases like "At no time did I ... " through clenched teeth that she and Ms Stigley are not exactly knitting matinee jackets for each other's mokopuna. <i>(Aug 9, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  Loving father to all his tamariki and his Australian and New Zealand mokopuna. <i>(Feb 24, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,46</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> A grandson Mokopoona <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/335</i>)

>  <b>BURNS</b> A grandson Mokopoona <i>Ibid.</i> 144 After a lengthy manipulation she produced a photograph, with the remark, `Taku mokopuna' (My grandchild), ... <i>(1844,NZ Chief,24</i>)

>  Tai has presented the House with its second `mokopuna' also a male & descendant on his sire's side of Te Rangimatemoana the Tamanuitera clan head. <i>(May 6, 1928,Na To Hoa Aroha,1/91,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: Tai has presented the House with its second `mokopuna' also a male & descendant on his sire's side of Te Rangimatemoana the Tamanuitera clan head.]

>  One can cover four and almost five generations, if one can reach back to the Rapata Wahawaha of the eighties and watch the environment now shaping the lives of one's own mokopuna. They, poor things, may be denied all opportunities to hear the old waiata, to take part in the bowdlerised haka or to look with understanding on ceremonial. <i>(May 15, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/149,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/340</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> I haven't worked among Maori infants for so long without learning to read the unspoken word. He was telling me that he trusted me to care for his `mokopuna'. <i>(1958,Spinster,58</i>)

>  Haere mai, mokopuna, she would say. <i>(1977,Listener Short Stories,91</i>) [NOTES: Ihimaera `The Child']

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> The old women cared eagerly for their mokopuna - were upset if they were not given some to bring up. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,17</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He will love his first mokopuna from you just like he loves his first tua'kana. <i>(1974,Maori Woman,75</i>)

>  <b>KIDMAN</b> `Henare, my mokopuna,' said the old man. `My grandson.' <i>(1982,Mrs Dixon and Friend,70</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> 369 Mokopuna = grandchild <i>(1983,Bone People, The,468</i>)

>  Among words to have appeared in the past few months in this journal, [sc <i>Listener</i>] ... are <i>tupuna, mokopuna, wero, take, taonga, tuturu. </i>Only a Maori dictionary at present can cope with these. <i>(Jan 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>) [NOTES: (Deverson)]

>  <b>BURCHFIELD</b> <i>mokopuna /</i>|mckcpuna/. N.Z. [Maori.] A grandchild; a descendant. <i>(1988,Lexicographical & Linguistic Studies,194</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> Though one [of two people] may be <i>mokopuna</i>the other on the lineage count, he may also be <i>tuakana</i> [elder brother] from family descent. <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/i./342</i>)

>  <b>ASHTON-WARNER</b> He was telling me that he trusted me to look after his `mokopuna'. <i>(1958,Spinster,58</i>)

>  <b>IHIMAERA</b> Perhaps an old koroua with his mokopuna shepherds sheep to the side of the road to let the car through <i>Ibid.</i> 21 Only he and four mokopuna live at home. The mokopuna are the children of some of Miriama's nieces. <i>(1974,Whanau,2/10</i>)

### moles
 
 <i>n.</i> a shortened form of moleskins for moleskin trousers

### moleskin
 
 <i>n.</i> a) a strong, cotton fabric. b) (plural) trousers made from this fabric, formerly commonly worn by workmen. (See [moleskins](mob-mys.md#moleskins))

### moleskin squatter
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Moleskin squatter</i>.-Working man who had come to own a small sheep run. A correspondent writes: `The last time I heard it was when the Government cut up Cheviot in 1893 ... Since then <i>m.ss. </i>have become too numerous to attract attention, or perhaps the term could not survive the end of the fashion for wearing <i>ms</i>.' <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The,15</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> [quoting Acland] <i>(1941,NZ Slang,40</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Moleskin squatter</i>. `A working man who has come to own a small sheep run': New Zealand and Australian rural coll.: C. 20. B., 1941, 1942. Ex the moleskin trousers such farmers tend to wear. <i>(1949,Dictionary of Slang Addenda,1111</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>Moleskin Squatter</i>: Working man who had come to own a small sheep run. A correspondent writes: `The last time I heard it was when the Govern- ment cut up Cheviot in 1893. ... Since then moleskin squatters have become too numerous to attract attention, or perhaps the term could not survive the end of the fashion for wearing moleskin trousers.' <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs,375</i>)

>  <i>moleskin squatter n. (Obs.)</i> working man who has come to own a small sheep farm. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i> moleskin squatter </i> working man who has acquired a small sheep run; from his moleskin trousers. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small-farmer or working man who acquires land.

>  Moleskin squatter: - Working man who had come to own a small sheep run. <i>(Jan 20, 1934,Press, The  (Acland Glossary),15</i>)

>  <b>MCGILL</b> moleskin squatter: working man who has acquired a small sheep run; from his moleskin trousers. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> Among other terms we have derived from farm life and sheep stations are moleskin squatter, 'a working man who has come to own a small sheep run ' ... <i>(1941,NZ Slang,40</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> moleskin squatter: man who had saved enough of his wages to acquire a small run. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,54</i>)

### moleskin trousers
 
 <i>n.</i> cord trousers

>  Twenty years ago we lived in houses that only cost GBP10, and dressed in a blue shirt and moleskin trousers. <i>(Feb 26, 1887,Auckland Weekly News,22</i>)

>  <b>BAKER</b> ...he could make out two bodies,.. one dressed in the remnants of a print dress and the other wearinfg a corduroy shirt and moleskin trousers. <i>(2000,Kokopu Dreams,166</i>)

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> List of prices] ... flush trousers, 7s. to 10s. [per] pair; ... moleskin do., 8s. to 10s.; ... <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,375</i>)

>  <b>MONEY</b> In a place [sc. the diggings] where open shirts, moleskin trousers tucked into long boots, crimson sashes tied round the waist, and tall American wide-awakes, were the prevailing attire, ... <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,18</i>)

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> The customary dress of the settlers was a blue woollen shirt or blouse, moleskin or cord trousers, strong boots and felt hat. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,130</i>)

>  <b>DOBSON</b> During the exploring days the clothes worn were the strongest procurable, moleskin trousers, very thick and heavy, either brown or white (moleskin is a thick twilled cotton material, and when wet it is almost waterproof and like soft leather), stout flannels, and a flannel shirt. <i>(1930,Reminiscences,156</i>)

<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [sp.use] moleskin cotton trousers [customarily worn by colonial shepherds and other rural workers.]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> .. the reader has to imagine a company of young men, living on bread, mutton, wild pork, and potatoes, in a reed-hut, garbed in little else than boots, shirt and moleskins ... <i>(1921,Tutira,126</i>)

>  Common dirty men in moleskins were permitted to come in too. <i>(Feb 22, 1913,NZ Observer,5</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (informal, derogatory) a girl or woman [a derogatory term for a woman, in the 1930's and 40's not necessarily a prostitute; but, so H. Beaton p.c., not with that association here; used (ca1935+) espec. by schoolchildren for any girl (esp. if she had a reputation, for being fast).]

### mollies
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>KEITH & MAIN</b> Marbles were the currency of childhood and there were an astonishing number of names for them, some referring to the material or colour, others to value or function in the game. There were agates, aggies, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cats-eye, [sic] chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, mollies, peelies, peewees, pisswees, pretties, Scotties, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers and whities. The march of technology added ball bearings - ballies or steelies - which were unbeatable when it came to smashing an opponent's miserable glassie. World War II[roman] brought `clayeys' often imperfectly spherical they were pathetic terracotta substitutes for real marbles. <i>(1984,NZ Yesterdays,288</i>)

### mollymawk
 
 <i>n.</i> [also mollyhawk]

### MOMA
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Minister of Maori Affairs

>  It doesn't appear that "MOMA" (as the Minister of Maori Affairs if sometimes referred to) has led the charge when it comes to relationship-building, although just how deliberate this is is unclear. <i>(Jun 27, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### Momley Peg
 
 <i>n.</i> stagknife

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> <i>Stagknife</i> (K-31), a universal game of nineteenth-century childhood, was important because it was played with the most treasured play object of all boys, the pocket knife. Although known in nearly all parts of New Zealand by the name of Stagknife, the game was also known as Bites, Jackknife, Stabknife, Knifey, Momley Peg, and Throwing the Knife. ... There were generally two players ... who each sought to deflect the point of the pocket knife from certain parts of the body by a flick of the hand in such a way that the blade would stick firmly into the ground. ... Every movement had to be done three times. [172] Typical movements were: (1) Place the open pocket knife (loose) [name of movement] [sic] on the closed knuckles of the right hand, point the knife towards the thumb, then with a quick upward flick of the hand to the left, deflect the point of the knife into the ground. <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,171</i>)

[Mohoua</i> spg. from French transliteration] <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/189</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/189]

 <i>n.</i>

>  Mondayisation means that if an employee usually works Monday to Friday, then they are entitled to have the Christmas public holidays on the following Monday and Tuesday ... <i>(Dec 21, 2004,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  Monday-isation is tricky, even discriminatory. <i>(Jan 15, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### Mondayise
 
 <i>v.</i> move a public holiday to a Monday [also as Mondayising verbal noun]

>  One result of the "Mondayising" of Christmas and New Year's Day will be no "Evening Post" on Monday, December 26, and Monday, January 2. <i>(Oct 28, 1955,Evening Post,13</i>)

>  [Anzac Day] is a special day and Mondayising or Tuesdayising it would not be appropriate. <i>(Apr 22, 2004,Radio NZ / National Radio</i>)

>  Here is a list of a few familiar words with, in brackets, their equivalent in England.. Mondayise (no equivalent for this most useful and expressive word). <i>(Jul 11, 1963,NZ Herald,00</i>)

>  Because the day is "Mondayised", employees don't have to be paid penal rates or be given a day off in lieu unless it is covered in their employment contract. <i>(Sep 21, 1999,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  60 per cent do not Monday-ise public holidays, instead recognising them on the day they fall. <i>(Jan 15, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

>  So, too, is the claim that the importance of the events being marked on those days might somehow be undermined if they are Mondayised. <i>(Feb 13, 2012,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  It's a day off - with the only difference that Anzac Day is not yet Mondayised - it's off to the football or into the TAB or into the garden. <i>(Apr 25, 1993,Sunday Times (NZ),8</i>)

>  <b>TURNER</b> ... holidays are arranged to fall on Monday when possible, or are <i>Mondayized. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,155</i>)

>  N.Z. Day [heading] New Zealand Day, February 6, does not replace any existing Anniversary Day outside of North Auckland and is an additional public holiday, the Labour Department said today. The holiday must be observed on February 6 and cannot be `Mondayised'. <i>(Jan 17, 1974,Evening Star</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  Mondayising Waitangi Day I can live with. After all, it's a day of celebration, or should be, for all New Zealanders, including those who have made this country their home. But not Mondayising Anzac Day. <i>(Jul 30, 2012,Dominion Post,B2</i>)

### Mondayitis
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Mondayitis </i>the blues, depression, particularly at the prospect of having to work; from the day after the weekend, when this condition is most apparent. NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

 <i>n.</i> an ATM, cash dispenser [not in UK dicts]

### money or the bag
 
 <i>phr.</i> [with variants]

>  They would call out 'The Money or the Bag!' <i>(1979,Out of the Bag,210</i>)

>  Whaddaya want - the money or the bag? New Zealanders may once have dithered over Selwyn Toogood's good--natured gameshow poser but no more. <i>(Oct 14, 2001,Sunday Star Times,E3</i>)

>  [headline] <b>Money-or-bag CHH choice <i>(Aug 19, 2005,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

### money side
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the last side of a sheep to be shorn.

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> Then the shearer comes down the 'money side', the sheep's last unshorn side. <i>(1999,Tales from the Shearing Shed,17</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> MONEY SIDE: A shearer calls the last side of the sheep to be shorn the`money' side. It is the side on which he finished the job, and is also to collect the money for doing the job. <i>(1949,High Country Days,196</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> You'll hear a bit of barracking then; some young shearer has stolen a march on his faster mates and got onto the `money-side' first, and a bit of an ironic cheer goes up as he whips over the last hind leg and kicks his sheep out of the porthole a couple of seconds before anyone else. <i>(1951,High Country,29</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

 <i>n.</i> (See [mangemange](m-mar.md#mangemange))

### mongie
 
 ([rhymes with youngy]) <i>n.</i> a member of the Mongrel Mob

>  David Burton relives a holiday spent chasing girls and waves - and being chased by Mongies... Whereupon Pete - dear fearless, feisty, foolish Pete - picked up the billiard ball and chucked it back at the roomful of Mongies. <i>(Dec 31, 2002,Dominion Post,C1</i>)

>  <b>5</b> They are informed that she is a Mongrel Mob moll. There's only one road in and out; there are five car-loads of Mongies just outside town. <i>(Aug 11, 2000,Evening Post</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> The Black Power gang were sworn enemies of both the Heads and the Mongies. ... <i>Ibid.</i> 251 MONGIE (n) Member of the Mongrel Mob gang. <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,22</i>)
