### Mongrel
 
 <i>n.</i> a member of the Mongrel Mob (See [Mongrel Mob](mob-mys.md#mongrel-mob))

>  The link to the Mongrels is that we expected this when the Mob moved in. <i>(Feb 15, 2001,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> He said he was a mongrel. Then in the police station, according to the constable's evidence, he broke down and said he wanted to leave the [gang] <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,4</i>) [NOTES: check quote]

### mongrel
 
 <i>n.</i> 1952 Feb 16 R.Gilberd notes on Mongrel Reading `Talking of Words' (Cecil Hull) p21`We might objectively and scientifically describe him, if he were ours, as a dog of mixed breeds - ... we would not be pleased if an acquaintance calls that dog a mongrel.' In town where dogs are kept largely for appearance and display `mongrel' I think has only one meaning - that a dog is of mixed breeding (with a note of disapproval or contempt). In the country mongrel has two independent meanings. (i) A dog of mixed blood. Objective - no emotional content. Indeed `pedigreed' is acquiring something of the mongrel taint, in that if a good dog has a pedigree it is mentioned with a faint note of surprise. (ii) A `no-good' whether man or animal. `He turned out mongrel' (Rod Macaulay speaking of a pedigreed mastiff that took to sheep worrying.) `When Bill brought him home I knew he'd turn out a mongrel. Sort of a cow-y look about his head.' (Walter Hull discussing a pedigreed `Totara Park' Friesian bull which proved sterile.) Talking of dogs: An `eye-dog' is a specialised type of header which works very quietly, never taking its eyes off the sheep it is trying to pen. Sheep men generally seem to think the dog hypnotises the sheep into being penned quietly. I believe that the fixity of its gaze and its complete concentration enable it to detect and counter any rebelliousness on the part of the sheep before the sheep have quite made up their minds.



 
 <i>n.</i> [[HWO distinguish from racist connotation =halfcaste, and the Australasian offensive epithet]] (See [cf. cur; mong](../C#cur;-mong))

>  <b>BEAGLEHOLE</b> Skin colour and the mixture of blood which is responsible for it [sc. high visibility in a white world] are often commented on by one Maori to another. The terms `mongrel', `black mongrel', `dago', or `black nigger', for instance, are often used by Maoris either in referring to themselves or to other Maoris in the neighbourhood. Thus one informant, in introducing herself, remarked: `I'm only a mongrel' - meaning only a mixed-blood Maori. <i>(1946,Some Modern Maoris,299</i>)

>  <b>R. GILBERD</b> `We might objectively and scientifically describe him, if he were ours, as a dog of mixed breeds - ... we would not be pleased if an acquaintance calls that dog a mongrel.' In town where dogs are kept largely for appearance and display `mongrel' I think has only one meaning - that a dog is of mixed breeding (with a note of disapproval or contempt). In the country mongrel has two independent meanings. (i) A dog of mixed blood. Objective - no emotional content. Indeed `pedigreed' is acquiring something of the mongrel taint, in that if a good dog has a pedigree it is mentioned with a faint note of surprise. (ii) A `no-good' whether man or animal. `He turned out mongrel' (Rod Macaulay speaking of a pedigreed mastiff that took to sheep worrying.) `When Bill brought him home I knew he'd turn out a mongrel. Sort of a cow-y look about his head.' (Walter Hull discussing a pedigreed `Totara Park' Friesian bull which proved sterile.) <i>(Feb 16, 1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: 1952 p.c. Feb 16 R.Gilberd notes on Mongrel Reading `Talking of Words' (Cecil Hull) p21]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `Some mongrel's firing at me!' cried dad, justifiably on the alert, poised to take cover and defend himself - ... <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,129</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The trouble with me wasn't so much that I was poor as that I was half Maori. I was just a bloody mongrel to some of them. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 `Going off with Martha like that. I ought to sock you one. I'll clock you, you bloody mongrel!' <i>(1984,South Pacific Street,47</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> a derogatory epithet

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Apples and several stone fruits are hardy enough for high and frosty places - it's a pretty mongrel spot in New Zealand where they won't grow. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,62</i>)

>  "... And paua like mongrel spots." <i>(Sep 20, 2005,NZ Herald,A9</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (in sport, esp, rugby) hardness, fierceness

>  We're often asked by many of the broadcasters: "Where is the mongrel?" <i>(May 9, 2004,Sunday Star Times,B12</i>)

>  Cook and the crew were just as violent. They had a bit of mongrel in them which is to say they were fully human. <i>(Jul 19, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Another former All Black coach, J.J. Stewart, said the recent talk about mongrel being needed in the All Black pack was "ridiculous". <i>(Nov 4, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  Loe was amused to hear radio sports talkback callers claim the All Black forwards were too soft and "didn't have enough mongrel." <i>(Nov 4, 1999,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  Men were pine trees and they played for pride not cash. It wasn't pretty and it wasn't pure. In rugby circles they still call it "mongrel". <i>(Nov 13, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),7</i>)

>  Livewire Junior All Whites midfielder David Mulligan is urging his teammates to show "more mongrel" om tonight's final group A under-17 world soccer championship match against Poland at Albany. <i>(Nov 16, 1999,Dominion,21</i>)

>  Mongrel in sport used to be the bitser dog that strayed on to the field and held up the play. Now it's the magical quality that means the difference between winning and losing... Mongrel in its sporting sense was coined by Australians who are particularly good at coming up with pithy expressions whose meanings are clear. "Bugger off" was another of theirs we quickly borrowed. <i>(Nov 20, 1999,Dominion,64</i>)

>  They've got the same sort of mongrel in them and just as Fitzy always wanted to make his mark in the first scrum so too does Coutts always want to get in first. <i>(Feb 19, 2000,Evening Post,68</i>)

>  Respected creative writer Bill Manhire has put it another way. He suggests that New Zealand contains a touch of the mongrel; that contrary to Tourism Board advertising, we are not 100 per cent pure. <i>(Apr 13, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  Maxwell has shown how a little discipline can be applied without compromising that mongrel factor. <i>(Apr 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B5</i>)

>  But hang on, you cry, we need a bit more mongrel at the top level... Fair call, but what the Hurricanes are doing isn't "mongrel" rugby. Mongrel rugby is putting in big hits. Not taking a backward step. Sorting out opposition foul play the old-fashioned way, rather than bleating to an incompetent referee to do something. <i>(May 2, 2000,Evening Post,Flair : 28</i>)

>  It has been called the "mongrel factor", but really it's just a refusal to be physically intimated <i>(Jul 9, 2000,Sunday Star Times,B3</i>)

>  It was the FitzSimons of about 10 years ago when he was still a rugby player and got himself banished from the field in a game against the All Blacks because he showed so much mongrel he would have needed to buy boarding kennels. <i>(Sep 27, 2000,Dominion,50</i>)

>  After the 1999 World Cup fiasco, the big question facing the All Blacks is: do they have something called "a bit of mongrel"?... Contrast that with legendary All Black skipper Buck Shelford, who lost a testicle in a match against France, was stitched up, and went straight back out on the field. There's a bit of mongrel for you. <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  But what does mongrel actually mean? The old image of an enforcer, whose thuggery was committed in a noble cause?. <i>Ibid.</i> 35 Graeme Mourie was a dairy farmer, and a phenomenally successful All Black skipper. You wouldn't ever accuse him of being an old-style mongrel. <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> to hassle

>  "It was chasing the bull ... mongrelling it," Mr Tuaupiki said. <i>(Jun 4, 2004,Dominion,A9</i>)



 
<b>4</b> <i>n.</i>

>  No doubt there are some ex-TVNZ employees out there, casualties of Ralston's cost-cutting measures, who think Ralston has more than just a hint of the mongrel in him. More like a rabid rottie. <i>(Dec 27, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),28</i>)

### mongrel factor
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  When people talk about the mongrel factor, they're talking about the fierce indomitable joy that someone like Norm Maxwell gets from putting himself about all over the park... When you talk about the mongrel factor, what you want is that indomitable will. <i>(Jun 24, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

### Mongrel Mob
 
 <i>n.</i> a Maori gang

>  He robs and beats up a German tourist, attempts to rape a woman in a pub toilet (getting himself on the wrong side of the local Mongrel Mob) and reveals a considerable talent for rationalising his worthless activities. <i>(Sep 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),46</i>)

>  Home to the Mongrel Mob, Black Power and world champion barefoot water skier Stephanie Hamblyn, Wairoa is a revalation. <i>(2005,AA Directions,23</i>)

>  Isaac's dream of welding a unified Mongrel Mob adhering to a national charter like the Black Power movement evaporated. <i>(Sep 23, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),37</i>)

>  So many press releases have pulsed their way back, commenting on everything from frigates to the Mongrel Mob, that one pictures Winston not rubbing shoulders with Bush and Dukakis, or even seeing much of the sights, but hunched over the fax in his hotel room, one hand punching ISD codes, the other obsessively switching channels on the TV remote. <i>(Nov 19, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),15</i>)

>  The Mongrel Mob members tell him that this is their land through ancestry. <i>(Feb 15, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),26</i>)

>  Notes for a speech delivered by Dr John Monkhouse, associate professor of millennial studies at Mallard University, to the annual general meeting of the Mongrel Mob's Benevolent Society on June 21, 2050. <i>(Mar 27, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),35</i>)

>  He was in that game that was abandoned not so long ago when an off-field rumpus between Black Power and the Mongrel Mob escalated into some serious shooting. <i>(1995,Mana,No. 10 : 37</i>)

>  But in 1988, with the Mongrel Mob convention 10km up the road circled by railway wagons containing crushed car bodies, a Black Power meet losing the odd Molotov cocktail to police vigilance, transexuals gathering to ponder their role in society and turbocharged production saloons hitting 200km on the Wellington waterfront circuit, a bunch of nostalgic Teds rockin' through town just brought the fragrance of an artless era. <i>(Nov 26, 1988,Listener (New Zealand Listener),20</i>)

### monk
 
 <i>n.</i> spotted stargazer, monkfish

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER ... <i>Other names: Geniagnus monopterygius</i>; catfish, monk, dogfish; kourepoua (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,263</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MONKFISH </i>(a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum.</i> (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37</i>)

>  species name] stargazer .. [scientific name] <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> .. [other name(s)] monkfish, boofs, bulldogs, churchills, flathead monk, giant stargazer, monks, muddies, spotted stargazer, toebiters, whitefish, sandcod <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] monk, monkfish 1808, 1814, 1822, 1831, 1835, 1855 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### Monkey
 
 <i>n.</i> short for Brass Monkey Rally

### monkey
 
 <i>n.</i> the cover of a wool press

>  <b>CARR</b> Pressing. - When there is sufficient wool in one bin, the pressers (generally two) get to work. One gets into the `box' as the lower portion of the patent presses is called, while his mate ... hands him ... the fleeces. These are placed in tiers, and tramped down until the lower box is full. An upper box is then swung ... or [16] lowered on to the lower one, and the process repeated. When full, the `monkey' or cover is put into position, the spears (wire rope) affixed, and the two men lever the cover down until the top box is clear. <i>(1913,Country Work,15</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a light from another cigarette, short for monkey fuck

 <i>n.</i> (Sport) [Equestrian.]

 <i>n.</i>

>  Overtrou have only three uses: .. protecting your shins when bashing through monkey scrub - especially with sunburnt legs, or when the monkey scrub is frozen. <i>(Aug 2007,FMC - (Federated Mountain Clubs of NZ) Bulletin,169/46</i>)

### monkfish
 
 <i>n.</i> stargazer

>  Monkfish, which is sweetish in flavour, can be covered with a potato sauce and then dusted with cocoa for a bitter contrast... Martin is deperate one day to be allowed to buy a whole monkfish, so he can get at the liver and have a play. In Europe, chefs pay a fortune for monkfish liver, which is described as the foie gras of the sea, whereas New Zealand fishermen fillet the monkfish on board their boats and probably toss the liver overseas. <i>(Aug 29, 2004,Dominion Post,E6</i>)

>  For these particular recipes choose fillets or steaks from a large, firm-fleshed white fish. Warehou, groper, trumpeter, bluenose and monkfish are all suitable. <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),42</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER [<i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i>] ... Other names include monkfish, catfish, dogfish, and flathead. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,20</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>MONKFISH</i> (a) Scientific name: (i) <i>Geniagnus monopterygius</i> (ii) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: (both) Whitefish. (c) Other common names: (both) Monkfish, Monks, Stargazer, Flathead (also applied at times to export gurnard); Churchills, Toebiters, Bulldogs, Spotted Stargazer, Maori Chief (Dunedin); Muddies. (d) Principal localities where taken: Napier, Cook Strait, Nelson, Banks Peninsula, Bluff-Stewart Island. ... Monkfish is a general term for species of the Families Uranoscopidae and Leptoscopidiae. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,37</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> SPOTTED STARGAZER Similar to his cousin the Common Stargazer but thicker and with more distinctive, spotted markings on body and fins. ... Other names are Catfish, Monkfish and Dogfish. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,208</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> <i>GIANT STARGAZER</i> (Monkfish) <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> <i>(1982,Collins Guide,271,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> Also known as stargazers because of their upward-looking eyes, monkfish are widely distributed in New Zealand waters, especially to the south. <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,78</i>)

>  The hoki are sent down first and are by far the major species caught in this area designated G in New Zealand's exclusive economic zone. But there are also ling and school shark, headed and gutted before they are boxed, squid and silver dory, fat blue nose and silver warehou, hake and sharp-toothed southern kingfish, ugly green monkfish and slimy flathead, electric skates and others with saw toothed snouts. <i>(Aug 22, 1984,Marlborough Express,6</i>)

>  [species name] stargazer .. [scientific name] <i>Kathetostoma giganteum</i> .. [other name(s)] monkfish, boofs, bulldogs, churchills, flathead monk, giant stargazer, monks, muddies, spotted stargazer, toebiters, whitefish, sandcod <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> <i>Monkfish Kathetotoma giganteum</i> ... Also occurs in southern Australia. Known by a number of local names: giant stargazer, flathead, bulldog, boof, etc. The name monkfish is well established for this species in New Zealand, whereas overseas it is used for similarly shaped but unrelated anglerfishes. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,118</i>)

>  About 500 kilograms of monkfish sat on the Greymouth wharf yesterday as a local fisherman and the Ministry of Agriculture and Fisheries argued over its fate. <i>(Jun 24, 1987,Dominion,8</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] monk, monkfish 1808, 1814, 1822, 1831, 1835, 1855 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### monoao
 
 <i>n.</i> a scrub plant with slender, rigid leaves found especially on the North Island volcanic plateau. [Maori] Common Error: do not confuse with MANOAO.

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> The mountains here [central NI] are very lofty and covered with an ochreous soil, the manoa principally grows on them it is a sacred shrub not used as fuel for cooking said to be te matamua o nga rakau and if burnt to cause rain. <i>(Apr 28, 1847,Journal,5/51</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[racophyllum] subulatum, Hook.f.</i> ... [426] NORTH ISLAND: From Rotorua ... to ... Ruahine Mountains. 350 to 3500 ft. <i>Monoao</i> <i>Ibid.</i> 655-656 <i>crydium] Colensoi, Hook. </i>A small tree 20-40 ft. high ..., very similar in mode of growth to <i>intermedium, ..</i>56] <i>ver-pine; Monoao</i>. The wood is yellowish-white, ... very durable. [Discusses confusion with <i>Dacrydium</i> (Hooker's Podocarpus?) <i>biforme</i>, although two species different] <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,425</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> The Kapenga tableland ..., a gully-seamed broken plateau, is covered with a thick growth of <i>manuka</i> and <i>monoao</i> shrubs, <i>tutu</i> and fern, with many <i>ti</i> or cabbage trees ... <i>(1922,NZ Wars, The,2/399,1983 ed.</i>)

>  They [pumice soils] support in their natural state a cover of stunted manuka and monoao (<i>Dracophyllum subulatum</i>), ... <i>(1936,Agricultural Organization in NZ,365</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Beyond Mackenzie Pass the name of a shrub, Te Monoao, denotes the Snow River ... <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,28</i>)

>  <i>monoao</i> [s] <i>Dracophyllum subulatum</i> Hook.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> Poa tussock, danthonia tussock, stunted bracken, stunted manuka, monoao, mingimingi, tauhinu, patotara, and <i>Pimeleas</i> were the more dominant plants of the poorer areas, ... <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,10,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>POOLE & ADAMS</b> [contrast MANOAO which P and A have incorrectly named following Cheeseman: cf also Allan] <i>D[racophyllum] subulatum Hook. f. ... Monoao. Shrub reaching 2 m. Branchlets long, slender. ... Flowers in short racemes,</i> ... L[owland], M[ontane]. Shrub land; especially on so-called `frost flats' of the volcanic plateau. <i>(1963,Trees and Shrubs,160</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MONOAO (<i>Dracophyllum subulatum</i>) Native shrub resembling manuka but more spindly, rathe smaller and reddish. Common in frost pockets and other small areas on the elevated pumice land in the Taupo region, ... <i>(1982,Weeds of Roadsides,99</i>)

### Moore filter
 
 <i>n.</i> [mining]

>  <b>McARA</b> <i>Moore filters</i>: Large `baskets' of canvas-covered filter-frames weighing about 16 tons and connected to a vacuum Source. First the basket was lowered by an overhead electric-crane into a tank of thickened pulp, previously treated with cyanide solution for several days. The vacuum caused a layer or cake of slime one or two inches thick to build up on the frames, while the clean solution, now laden with gold and silver, was pumped to the precipitators. When the cake reached the required thickness the basket was lifted and placed in a tank of barren cyanide solution, which was sucked through the cake removing the residual gold-bearing solution. After this the basket was placed in a `wash water' tank to remove all the solution, and then it was lifted and placed over the discharge bay; the vacuum was then released, allowing the cake to fall off (with some encouragement from a wooden scraper) into a waste sluice. [328] This process was repeated at about two-hourly intervals, with several bays working at the same time. <i>(1978,Gold Mining at Waihi (Glossary),327</i>)

### moountain pine
 
 <i>n.</i> [kauri (cowrie) as 'mountain pine'; single citation]

We observed two species of it, but the mountain-pine, called by the natives <i>cowrie</i>, which we found growing upon the sides and summits of the hills, and rising to an enormous size, was the most remarkable. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/233</i>)

### mopoke
 
 <i>n.</i> an owl

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mopoke, n.</i> aboriginal name for an Australian bird, from its note `Mopoke.' There is emphasis on the first syllable, but much more on the second. Settlers very early attempted to give an English shape and sense to this name. The attempt took two forms, ` <i>More-pork</i>,' and <i>`Mope-hawk'</i>; both forms are more than fifty years old. The r sound, however, is not present in the note of the bird, although the form <i>More-pork</i> is perhaps even more popular than the true form <i>Mope-hawk</i> seems to have been adopted through dislike of the perhaps coarser idea attaching to `pork.' The quaint spelling <i>Mawpawk</i> seems to have been adopted for a similar reason. [1827 Tasmania] <i>(1898,Austral English,301</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> As Old Dan turned and walked towards the whare [e-acute] a `mopoke' owl that had settled on the ridge of it, and had been uttering at intervals its mournful cry, shrieked and flew away. <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,241</i>)

>  ... a flock of kakas ... broke in upon his melody. A mopoke hooted eerily from the shadows behind the tent ... <i>(Sep 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,403</i>)

>  <b>BOREHAM</b> Night after night, when the <i>mopoke</i> was shouting his strange cry down the wooded valleys, the party would paddle ashore ... <i>(1911,Selwyn,68</i>)

>  MOPOKE. - A small owl of New Zealand. <i>(1917,New Zealand At the Front,xiv</i>)

>  The moon is hid as the evening skies cloud o'er at the mopoke's plaintive shriek. <i>(Jul 11, 1921,Quick March,19</i>)

>  mopoke, morepork The aboriginal name for Australian bird, adopted in New Zealand for the native owl. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),265</i>)

>  <i>mopoke morepork n</i>. nocturnal bird, the boobook owl. [From its well-known call resembling `mo-poke,' `more pork' or `boo-book']; <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>mopoke, morepork n.</i> nocturnal bird, the boobook owl. From its well-known call resembling <i>mo-poke, more pork</i> or <i>boo-book</i>; a N.Z. owl. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <i>mopoke n. 1.</i> a small spotted owl of Australia and New Zealand. <i>... 3. Austral. & N.Z. sl</i>. a boring person. ~ Also called: <i>morepork.</i> [C19: imit. of the bird's cry] <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,732</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1986 NZPOD 487]]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Little bit of a cough, Norman's got. He doesn't grow fat. I think maybe he's hungry in the nights, 'cos when I come in he's wide awake as the <i>mopoke</i> under the sacks, but he won't sing out, will you, Norman?' a New Zealand owl. <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,273</i>)

>  [species name] ribaldo .. [scientific name] <i>Mora moro</i> .. [other name(s)] deep sea cod, googly-eyed cod, mora (or morar) <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

### morehu
 
 <i>n.</i> the dispossessed, the remnants

>  T W Ratana preached from the Bible and the Treaty, and gathered the morehu, the neglected people, into a new family who recognised his special status. <i>(Sep 11, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  "That's why the Holy Spirit came to save the Maori people" came to save the Maori people." Ratana called those who had survived "the morehu", or remnants. <i>(Jul 31, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

### morepork
 
 <i>n.</i> (figurative) a NI train

>  A North Auckland (NZ) train is (or used to be) popularly called the <i>morepork</i> because of its sad nocturnal whistle. <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,62</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>Morepork, The</i> North Auckland train, from its melancholy whistle in the night. <i>(1968,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> any of various marine fishes

>  [species name] marblefish .. [scientific name] <i>Aplodactylus arctidens</i> .. [other name(s)] granite trout, maori chief, morepork <i>(Jul 23, 1986,Codes for Species in NZ Waters</i>)

>  <b>SORENSON</b> <i>COD, ROCK </i>(a) Scientific name: <i>Lotella rhacinus</i>. (b) Suggested commercial name[s]: Rock Cod; Kelp Cod. (c) Other common names: Parrotfish; Soldiers, Kelp, Kelpies, Moreporks, Marblefish, Maori Chief, Wrasse, Bourbon, Guffy, Cloudy Bay Cod, Rasp. <i>(1970,Nomenclature of NZ Fish,18</i>)

>  NOTOTHENIA MACROCEPHALA Gunther[u-dieresis]. <i>Maori Chief.</i> Known to Wellington fishermen as `morepork.' Secured off Island Bay during the autumn. ... Distribution: South Island and southern coasts of North Island. <i>(Jun 1921,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,4/123</i>)

>  <b>FISHER</b> Even the Morepork (sometimes called the Kelpfish) thought that the only way out was to go past my ear. It was a big fish - at [38] least twelve pounds. They have a lot of meat on them and have been known to be mistaken by new chums for the Spotted Black Groper. <i>(1977,Angels Wear Black,37</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> a slowcoach or dawdler

>  <b>POTTS</b> The name at least, if not the appearance, of the morepork ... is well-known throughout the colony[OEDS country]. Australian settlers distinguish a podargus by a similar name, whence the colonial epithet (whether of Australian or New Zealand origin is uncertain) applied to a dawdling person, who is often described as `a regular old morepork' <i>(1874,On Recent Changes in the Fauna,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the owl <i>Ninox novaeseelandiae</i> (See [ruru](roa-ruv.md#ruru))

>  [Fantails] take a leading role when a mixed group of birds mobs a morepork, moving in closer than any other species and sometimes striking the morepork on the head in dive-bombing attacks from the rear. <i>(Jan 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 55/84</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> Amongst the commonest birds which frequent the forests[OEDS forest] is a small owl, generally known to the settlers by the denomination of `More pork', from a habit it has of pertinaciously reiterating this phrase for about half an hour before daylight. <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>REISCHEK</b> Only if I were to made a fire would the little `Moreporks' come, attracted by the light, ... <i>(1930,Yesterdays in Maoriland,193,1933 ed.</i>)

>  On the way south Monty and Trev are attacked by moreporks. <i>(Apr 12, 2000,Evening Post,4</i>)

>  From your bed you can hear moreporks at night. <i>(Oct 20, 2001,Evening Post,25</i>)

>  Birds were lured with a tape-recorded call before being steered into super-fine netting, using a morepork as a decoy ("the birds tend to mob any bird of prey")... <i>Ibid.</i> 69 Again, through iron patience, determination and mnountains of trial and error, he discovered what moreporks fed their chicks. <i>Ibid.</i> 70 A stunning black and white image of a morepork coming in to land, its wings fully, perfectly splayed, frozen in flight, is the logo that graces his letterhead... Of course, moreporks nearly killed him, but he couldn't hold that against them. <i>(Feb 1999,North & South,68</i>)

>  He has had a rough time of it surveying a block on the ranges up the Wai-o-hine in the Wairarapa ...[sic] Once ...[sic] they had nothing to eat but a tui and a more-pork. ... They were stewing the two birds when a kuku-pa (bush pigeon) flew up and perched. Bob shot it ... This is not exactly what the Britishers call `sport' - but there is more of nature about it than 30 gents in pink and tops ... waiting for a wretched little fox who, when he is killed, is not equal for culinary purposes to a more-pork. Once coming up the Waikato in 1851 your Uncle James got a pot-shot at a lot of parera (grey duck) ... <i>(Mar 22, 1894,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,2/597,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (C.W. Richmond)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> I never saw the native quail, the cuckoo nor the morepork in the Central [Otago, c1860s], but I saw the sparrow-hawk, the rock wren, and plenty of hawks and pihoihoi (ground larks). <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,121</i>)

>  <i>morepork</i>: New Zealand owl. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> One of his theories was that farming couldn't be made to be pay nohow, and that it would be better for him and his neighbour, ... , to cease chopping away at the bush, clearing fern and perching up at the head of the valley like a couple of moreporks, ... <i>(1959,Tales of the Mails,91</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Morepork .. .. koukou, peho, ruru .. .. <i>Ninox novaeseelandiae </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <i>231. Ninox novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae </i>(Gmelin, 1788) <i>Morepork (Ruru) </i> <i>(1970,Annotated Checklist of Birds of NZ,61,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BRASCH</b> Morepork, shrewd sentry owl,/ Watching night pass,/ Sleepless, censorial,/ <i>(1984,Collected Poems,97</i>)

>  <i>morepork n</i>. another name, esp. in New Zealand, for <i>mopoke. </i> <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,733</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 488; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 317]]

>  <i>Morepork Ninox novaeseelandiae novaeseelandiae</i> (GMELIN, 1788) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Ruru, New Zealand owl, boobook owl. </i> <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,257</i>)

>  Much remains to be learned of the private life of the Morepork. In the early spring the answering calls of "more pork; more pork; more pork" are alternated by husky crooning notes and screech-like cries of mating birds. <i>(1944,Korero (AEWS Background Bulletin),2(21) : 24</i>)

>  The morepork is lonely, it calls in the night... <i>(Mar 8, 1997,Listener (New Zealand Listener),10</i>)

>  Soon many a lonely waste, abandoned to the morepork, kaka, and tui, would be made to "blossom like the rose". <i>(Oct 18, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,7</i>)

>  He heard the boding notes of the more-pork owl on most nights. <i>(Apr 20, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  The cry of the morepork at full moon is said to be a portent of death. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,81</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> Every now and then the morepork would give his nightly call. <i>(1986,Speaking for Ourselves,00</i>)

>  "Before finishing farming I hope to be kept awake with the call of moreporks," he says. <i>(Aug 19, 2004,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  [Advt] Whether it's pork sausages, Belgians, saveloys.. or bacon, and you mention "Morepork", you've said it. <i>(Aug 12, 1916,Hawke's Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>BILLING</b> <i>Morepork</i>, said the morepork... <i>"Morepork</i>. It's too late." <i>(1965,Forbush and the Penguins,94</i>)

>  And there was this morepork in the tree, just sitting there looking at me. It wasn't till later I discovered a morepork is the traditional messenger of the Whanganui river. <i>(Oct 10, 1998,Listener (New Zealand Listener),44</i>)

>  Just then a morepork owl flew silently by. <i>(Mar 23, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,42</i>)

>  <b>MAHY</b> No one can see us except morporks.(sic) <i>(1992,Underrunners,103</i>)

>  <b>MARSHALL</b> Then he began to listen to the morepork... <i>(1979,Supper Waltz Wilson,16</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He'd sit in the clearing in the twilight and watch the moreporks come hunting for huhu grubs round rotten trees. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,278</i>)

>  Camped in the bush at Aickens to be disturbed only by whistling frogs, moreporks and occasional thundering trains. <i>(1933,Canterbury Mountaineer, The,II/32</i>)

>  Only a cow lowing, the hoot of an owl and the plaintive cry of a mopork ripped that silence. <i>(Jan 9, 1947,NZ Woman's Weekly: The First 60 Years,1</i>)

>  <b>SEWELL</b> He had sat for three hours listening for the cry of the `Morepork.' <i>(1853,Journal,1/326,1980 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> There is a large and very beautiful pigeon - and besides these I should add the `More pork', a night bird which is supposed to say these works; ... <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,45,1960 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Some [birds] are seldom seen, ... as for instance the ruru (Athene Novae Seelandiae), the `more pork' of the settlers, ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,139</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLL</b> In the evening after dark I shot an owl it is called (vulgarly) the `more pork' from its cry. <i>(Apr 16, 1864,Journal,187</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... the first awakening of the myriads of Bush-birds; ... the last cry of a very pretty little owl, called from its distinctly uttered cries [1883 words] the `more-pork.'[1883 `More-pork,' as it flitted away before dawn to the highest trees; ...] <i>(1866,Station Life in NZ,100,1883 ed.</i>) [NOTES: [check 1870]]

>  I was disturbed ... by a bird called the `More Pork' .... [Moor Perk] <i>(1868,Journal Saved from Shipwreck,26</i>)

>  Athene Novae Zelandiae. More-Pork. Heruru. Kou-kou. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/160</i>) [NOTES: (Hutton Birds Great Barrier)]

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> ... Maup hawk or owl. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>PRENTICE</b> ... Maup hawk or owl. <i>(1870,Tale of NZ,34</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> ATHENE NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. Gml. Morepork. Ruru. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,23</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> SPILOGLAUX NOVAE ZEALANDIAE. (NEW-ZEALAND OWL, OR MOREPORK.) ... <i>Native names.</i> Ruru, Koukou, and Peho; `Morepork' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,17,{1888 2nd edn 1/192} ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> During the night we heard, besides the well-known call of the Morepork (<i>Athene Novae Zelandiae</i>), that of another owl, which I had never heard before; ... <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,37</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Night-hawk or New Zealand Owl ..... Strigidae; Spiloglaux Novae Zealandiae: (Buller) The `morepork' of the colonists. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,1/305</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> As the night closed in upon us we heard all round the solemn notes of the New Zealand Owl: first, a distinct <i>kou-kou, kou-kou</i>; ... and then, in alternation, the alarm note and ever familiar cry of `more-pork'. <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,1/13</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i> Morepork, n.</i> (1) The Australian bird or birds, described under <i>Mopoke</i> (q.v.). (2) The New Zealand Owl, formerly <i>Athene novae-zelandiae</i>, Gray; now <i>Spiloglaux novae-zelandiae,</i> Kaup. <i>(1898,Austral English,303</i>)

>  <b>POWER</b> <i>(1849,Sketches in NZ,74</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [B.S. Westd] <i>The Owl or Morepork [Ninox novaezelandiae]</i> the best known is ... called the Morepork by the diggers and settlers from its peculiar cry. <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,258</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas (1957) 258.]

>  <b>WHITE</b> A small Owl flies about the garden towards night; [70] it is commonly called the `Morepork,' owing to its distinct pronunciation of those two words. <i>(1905,My NZ Garden,69</i>)

>  <b>BAUGHAN</b> <i>Morepork:</i> The New Zealand owl. <i>(1908,Shingle-Short,20</i>)

>  <b>PFAFF</b> All night the More-pokes, Wekas (Maori hens), Kiwis, and, in summer, the Cuckoos, kept up their cries ... <i>(1914,Diggers' Story, The,11</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> ... we made shift to lighten this inevitable infliction of the deep bush with yarns ... that go best to the accompaniment of ... the `kou-kou' of the wondering morepork ... <i>(1916,Bush Explorers,12</i>)

>  <i>Spiloglaux novaeseelandiae Gm. Morepork. </i> <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/84</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <i>~ n.</i> a kind of goatsucker found in Australia, the mopoke; a small owl found in New Zealand. [Imit. fr. its cry] <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> In a tree by a little dark pool sits a morepork, blinking at you out of cynical amber eyes. <i>(1934,Journalese,16</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the cry of the morepork

>  It takes the familiar cry of "morepork, morepork, morepork" to bring me back to earth. <i>(Dec 9, 2003,BLANK ENTRY,A11</i>)

>  The Rura Rura, a small owl, kept up its incessant and monotonous cry of `More Porke, More Porke'. <i>(1847,NZ Journal,7/200/219</i>)

>  <b>MANSFIELD</b> In the garden some tiny owls, perched on the branches of a lace-bark tree, called: `More pork; more pork.' And far away in the bush there sounded a harsh rapid chatter: `Ha-ha-ha ... [sic] Ha-ha-ha.' <i>(1920,Stories,232,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> The Maoris share in the almost universal superstition regarding Ruru, the `Owl', ... whose cry of `More Pork' makes them known to every New Zealander as `Moreporks'. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),104</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> We all heard the moreporks calling in the dark, `More<i> pork</i>! More <i>pork</i>!' and once the shrill <i>`Wee</i>-ka, <i>wee</i>-ka,' of our camp followers. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,74</i>)

### Moreton Bay fig
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GEE</b> He was loafing in the sun in Albert Park on a seat by the Moreton Bay fig trees. <i>(1992,Going West,212</i>)

>  <i>Moreton Bay fig</i> Australian (and NZ) tree with large glossy leaves and small fruit. [bay in Queensland] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,488</i>)

>  It's just a small park, .. redeemed by two magnificent pohutukawa and a many-trunked Moreton Bay fig whose bulging roots, thrusting through the lawn, may well represent an insidious form of Australian neocolonialism. <i>(Dec 14, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  Until recently, Moreton Bay fig trees were harmlessly barren. <i>(Oct 23, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),8</i>)

>  His initial foray into horticulture followed the purchase of an historic villa in Herne Bay with a resplendent Moreton Bay fig tree out the front. <i>(Jun 3, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D8</i>)

>  At a nearby park, the paths are painted crimson with fallen Moreton Bay figs, round and small and soft, like plums, easy to squash underfoot, and they smell like sweet coconut. Inside, the flesh is dry in the centre, sticky at the edges; and their red skins blacken in the sun. <i>(Apr 1, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a look

>  I'm just patting myself on the back for being a big-time hack and hunter when I spot a mug having a Moreton Bay fig at me. Moreton Bay fig (gig)....look <i>(May 21, 1963,NZ Truth,19</i>)

### morihana
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARSANT</b> Here we would take potatoes and matches, and then catch a small variety of carp called 'morihana'... Their Maori name, 'morihana', was a translation of "Morrison.' A captain, I believe, in the Armed Constabulary days, Morrison had introduced the fish to Taupo and Lake Rotorua <i>(1979,They Called Me Te Maari,56</i>)

>  In return, my father provided pork, lamb, beef and vegetables, as well as smoked eel and morihana. <i>(May 10, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### Moriori
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Chatham Island Morioris have joined the chorus of discontent at the Government's selection of the Treaty of Waitangi Fisheries Commission. <i>(Jun 1, 1993,Dominion,10</i>)

>  According to the traditions, when Toi arrived the country was well inhabited. ... These people are the <i>tangata whenua </i>of the traditions and they were divided into a [20] number of groups ... By an unfortunate and unjustified identification these people have become known in New Zealand by the name `Moriori' which rightly belongs only to the prehistoric inhabitants of the Chatham Islands. <i>(1961,Distance Looks Our Way,19</i>) [NOTES: (Golson)]

>  I wish to advise [him] that no such race as the Moriori ever exxisted. The Moriori con story is one of the greatest and cruellest hoaxes ever inflicted upon Maoridom. The people of Wharekauri (Chathams) are no more Moriori than I am. <i>(Jun 11, 1990,Listener (New Zealand Listener),115</i>)

>  The film's title refers to white feathers Moriori men wore in their beards to show they were peaceful after their chief Nunuku vowed never to take up arms against another human being.... He is fascinated by the fact that Moriori had tried to run a society on a philosophy of non-violence, while Maori and pakeha societies were based on violence and conquest. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  Howe alleges that "the term Moriori is now a technical [one] referring to those ancestral Maori who settled the Chatham Islands". <i>(Mar 15, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),55</i>)

>  <b>Michael King</b> Of the fact that Moriori were Polynesian and shared ancestry with Maori there was - or should have been - no doubt. <i>(2003,Penguin History of New Zealand,48</i>)

>  The remnant of the Morioris (the name given to the aboriginal inhabitants), exclusive of the few who are still maintained in slaver, is settled at Ohangi, on the south-eastern side of the island. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/00</i>) [NOTES: Check page and year.]

>  Scattered amongst the fallen stones from the Maori ovens, an odd fragment of two and an occasional leg bone showed the remnants of some Moriori whose body had been interred in the sand there. <i>(Feb 12, 1924,Otago Witness,7</i>)

>  Some Moriori were staked to the ground and left to die slowly as "a warning to the others"; the surviving Moriori were taken as slaves. In the film one of the Moriori slave characters tells how she could be killed at any moment and "cut up for the oven or cut up for the dogs". <i>(Jul 30, 2000,Sunday Star Times,F8</i>)

>  The story is told in the film <i>The Feathers of Peace</i>, named for the white feathers Moriori men wore in their beards to denote peaceful intentions. <i>(Jul 19, 2000,Capital Times,4</i>)

>  The wherefrom of the Moriori is so clouded with legends of magic and miracle and nebulous with contradictory versions that they fail in weight as history. <i>(Jul 22, 1992,Metro,18</i>)

### Moriori flax
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment)

>  <b>HOLMES</b> <i>Astelia chathamica </i>- .. It has long silvery sword-like leaves with flower heads like the cabbage tree and in the autumn berries are produced. (Locally called Moriori flax.) <i>(1984,Chatham Islands,93</i>)

>  Kakaha or Moriori flax (<i>Astelia chathamica</i>) has also doubled in these areas ... <i>(Nov 2006,Footnotes,26 / 7</i>)

### Moriori myth
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SORRENSON</b> Then in 1937 he published an essay called `The Maruiwi myth'. This was concerned largely with the first part of the legend of the coming of the Maori - `that the first Polynesian settlers who arrived with Toi ... found the North Island - possibly both islands - inhabited by a non-Polynesian race, known as Maruiwi or Mouriuri, whom they subsequently exterminated; a small remnant alone escaping to colonize the Chatham Islands'. <i>(1979,Maori Origins and Migrations,53</i>) [NOTES: 1979 Sorrenson Maori Origins and Migrations 53 quoting 1937 H.W.Williams J.P.S. 46/105-122]

>  <b>WISNIOWSKI</b> This passage embodies an early version of what has become known as the `Moriori myth'. Except for the name Moriori, which was borrowed from the Chatham Islands and applied by 1880 to supposed pre-Maori inhabitants of New Zealand, the theory is all here in a book published in 1877, based on material collected in the mid-1860s. A black-skinned, curly-headed people with timid dispositions, presumably negroid or Melanesian, were supposed to have been found in possession of New Zealand by the first Polynesian settlers and exterminated by them except for a few with whom they intermarried, so that their racial traits appeared from time to time among the Maoris. <i>(1877,Tikera,301</i>) [NOTES: 1972 ed. note in 1877 WISNIOWSKI Tikera (trans. Podstolski 1972) 301]

 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> My name Frederick they had Moriorized into Pererika, and by that name they ever afterwards addressed me. <i>(1866,Chatham Islands,33</i>)

### morning glory
 
 <i>n.</i> = bindweed; convolvulus

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CONVOLVULUS [BINDWEED] (<i>Convolvulus arvensis</i>), is also called bindweed and black bindweed, cornbind, and morning glory. it is an introduced weed found in fields, gardens and waste places in many localities. It has a perennial creeping underground stem, trailing or twining [aerial] stems with leaves 1 to 3 inches long, shaped like a spear head, and handsome bell-shaped flowers an inch or more in diameter- ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,135</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> CONVOLVULUS [BINDWEED] (<i>Convolvulus arvensis</i>), is also called bindweed and black bindweed, cornbind, and morning glory. it is an introduced weed found in fields, gardens and waste places in many localities. It has a perennial creeping underground stem, trailing or twining [aerial] stems with leaves 1 to 3 inches long, shaped like a spear head, and handsome bell-shaped flowers an inch or more in diameter- ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,135</i>) [NOTES: 1926 headword <i>convolvulus</i>; 1967 headword <i>bindweed</i>]]

>  <i>morning glory [=] blue morning glory: field bindweed greater bindweed [s] </i> <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

>  <b>ADAMS</b> Various bindweeds collectively known as `convolvulus' are vigorous twiners with white or pink flowers. The most beautiful is the royal-blue morning glory that covers shrubs ..Botanical name: <i>Ipomaea learii</i>, Tropical America. Family: Covolvulaceae. <i>(1980,Wild Flowers,28</i>)

### morning star
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Tawera spissa</i>, a small bivalve abundant on sandy beaches of northern New Zealand.

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> MORNING STAR (<i>Tawera spissa</i>). This fish has a small venus shell; it is very abundant on most open sandy beaches. It is easily recognised by its colour pattern of brown zigzag markings. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,22</i>)

### morning tea

>  [It is a yellow-bird, and always likes some tea in the morning.] <i>(Jul 17, 1890,Otago Witness,39</i>)

### morning wood
 
 <i>n.</i> kindling set aside to start domestic fires. More common in NZ than <i>kindling</i>, but perhaps now (1984) old-fashioned ["As a child it was one of my chores to cut and bring in the "morning wood". I guess it was so named because in the days of coal ranges, if you didn't have the "morning wood" to light the fire there would be no cuppa', no porridge or whitebait fritters in saseon! and no water for a bath." (Valerie Young); ca1920's (R. Mason) Kindling for lighting the fire in the morning. ca1983 Sept Canvastown : `the morningwood's too wet' - said of difficulty in lighting the Trout Hotel open fire at 5.00 pm.]

>  Dad used to split thin wood and place it up the chimney to dry for morning's wood and often that wood caught fire. <i>(1959,Greymouth Evening Star</i>) [NOTES: 1959 Mary Anderson `The Women of Westland' in Greymouth Star in 1974 Heinz Bright Fine Gold 16]

### mossbank
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) mounds of partly decayed vegetable matter lying on the soil surface to a depth of up to ten feet or more, formed in much the same way as peat and common in former forestland.

>  The Catlins District Mossbanks: In the Catlins district, South Otago, there are large tracts of country more or less covered with what is known as "mossbank." These mossbanks are mounds or heaps of partly decayed vegetable matter lying on the surface of the soil and varying in depth from 1 ft to 10 ft or more. The acidity of mossbanks is very marked .. Under all mossbanks is to be found a hard pan at varying depths, and generally of undulating formation. <i>(Sep 20, 1916,NZ Journal of Agriculture,198</i>)

### moth plant
 
 <i>n.</i> an evergreen invasive climber, <i>Araujia sericifera</i>, recently declared a pest plant in Wellington. [Jim Cameron writes: Also known as <i>kapok plant</i> from the capsules. Neither name is in any dictionary I have seen, and they may be NZ terms. See pamphlet <i>Invasive Creepers and Climbers</i>, Wellington Region Council (nd. - 1999?). Some other plant names in this pamphlet might also be specific to NZ).]

>  Moth Plant <i>Araujia sericifera</i>. Also known as Kapok plant... Shade tolerant evergreen up to 5m or more from rhizomes. Stems produce a sticky white substance. <i>(1999,Invasive Creepers and Climbers,00</i>)

>  <i>moth plant</i> [s] <i>Araujia hortorum</i> Fourn. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

>  <i>moth plant</i> [s] <i>Araujia hortorum</i> Fourn. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

### mother
 
 <i>v.</i> to match a newborn lamb with its mother; find a mother for a newborn lamb. [occas. mother up] [cf. mismother]

>  <b>OVERTON</b> Lambs mother up quicker and are more easily handled in moderate sized mobs. <i>(1949,Fifty Years Sheep Farming,98</i>)

>  He pretended to give me every opportunity of `mothering' (as it is called) my missing lambs, but for fifteen ewes I could find but four. Again, next year, he played me a similar trick, and ... curtly told me to keep my crawlers on my own grass ... <i>(1889,Zealandia,1/30</i>) [NOTES: (Reeves)]

>  <i>(Jul 1941,NZ Farmers' Weekly</i>) [NOTES: (RM)]



 
 <i>n.</i> a term of address to early missionary wives (cf Marsden Letters etc)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Her own [sc. Mrs Williams] friendly natives would rush in with any story they heard. "Oh, Mother, there has been a fight - the Williams is dead." <i>(1961,Reminiscences,14</i>) [NOTES: 1842 in 1961]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> They might have said, as an old Maori woman long afterwards said to me, `Mother, my heart is like an old kete [e1-circumflex] (<i>i.e.</i>, a coarsely-woven basket). The words go in, but they fall through.' <i>(1884,Our Maoris,44</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) [of young stock] to find their mothers.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It would be necessary for someone to remain behind and hold the mob together for an hour or so while the lambs mothered and the head shepherd elected that young Wallace should stop and assist him. <i>(1949,High Country Days,24</i>)

>  Do not move the ewes until all the lambs have mothered. <i>(Sep 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,202</i>)

### Mother Bishop
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SELWYN</b> The Maoris gave us a hearty welcome, "Haere mai, Haere mai Mata Pihopa" - "Come here, come here, Mother Bishop". <i>(1961,Reminiscences,17</i>) [NOTES: 1842 in 1961]

>  <b>SELWYN</b> "Now then, Mother Bishop," said they, "you must show us how to cut him [sc. a pig] up." I should think that herein I occupied a unique position as it would hardly come under the sphere of a "Mother Bishop's" duties at home. <i>(1961,Reminiscences,27</i>) [NOTES: 1844 in 1961]

### Mother Cameron's weed
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) St John's Wort or Hypericum perforatum, a plant with toxic effects for some farm animals. [also Mother-of-Cameron weed]

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Mother-of-Cameron weed, perhaps better known as St Johns Wort, was troublesome on both the Aurum and The Branches stations in our time. <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,159</i>)

>  <i>Hypericum perforatum </i> <i>(Aug 1964,Bulletin of the Wgtn Botanical Soc.,14/8</i>) [NOTES: [R. Mason notes: Central Otago, common name according teacher at Secondary School Science Teachers' refresher course. [get quot]]]

>  <i>Mother Cameron's weed [=] St. John's wort</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Back in the early days Mrs Cameron planted this beautiful yellow flower at Glen Finnan and it spread all over the countryside. It was known to the people in the area as Mother Cameron's Weed. <i>(1976,High Country Sheep Station,42</i>)

>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Another plant which used to make cattle throw violent fits when crossing water was St John's wort. Back in the early days Mrs Cameron planted this beautiful yellow flower in her garden at Glenfinnan and it spread all over the countryside and was locally known as Mother Cameron's weed. [Mother-of-Cameron weed] <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,160</i>)

### Mother Carey's chicken
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> It had a yellowish beak & <i>Capt Cook</i> says Mr <i>Banks</i> has seen it near <i>Cape Horn</i> in the high southen latitudes, & called it <i>Mother Cary's Goose</i>. [[ed. fn] Giant Petrel <i>Macronectes giganteus </i>(Gmelin, 1789).] It must be observed that the Sailors call all the <i>Procellarias, Mother Cary's Chickens fowls,</i> ducks, pigeons, Geese etc. [sic] in proportion to their Size. <i>(Dec 21, 1772,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/203</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> As it came on to rain we were obliged to haul to the wind, and stood off till towards morning. Mother Kary's Chickens playing about all day. <i>(Apr 6, 1828,Early Journals of Henry Williams, The,119,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> I found ... that seamen make no distinction between this species of Storm-Petrel [<i>Thalassidroma melanogaster</i>, Black-bellied Storm-petrel], and its congeners, calling them all `Mother Carey's Chickens', and resenting as a positive sin any attempt to shoot or capture these `spirits of departed sailors', as they facetiously term them, to whom they profess to commit the destinies of the voyage. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,230</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water tupoupou tchu[macron]poup' pied blue billie reoreo ro[macron]ro[macron] Mother Carys chicken turiwati tchu[macron]riwat' dottrel [sic] kuaka ku[macron]k' snipe <i>(1928,Bishop Museum Memoirs,IX No. 5 /358</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]

### mother country
 
 <i>n.</i> England; later, Britain, the UK. Used as a sentimental expatriates' term from almost the beginning of settlement, and as an indication of the nature of the ties of loyalty to Britain. Since WW2 would, I think, be generally regarded as too sentimental for born-Kiwi use, and more suited to recent, unassimilated immigrants. Cf. Home

>  ... mother-country <i>(1843,Letters from Settlers,38</i>) [NOTES: 1842 R.Stokes Letters from Settlers (1843) 38]

>  Messrs. Beamish ... have secured the right to an unlimited quantity of prime pasturage, and from their experience in the management of stock, both in the mother country and the colonies, they trust, with their united efforts, to give general satisfaction. <i>(Jan 11, 1851,Lyttelton Times,1</i>)

>  'Tis not that ... my mother-country's soil / Will not support her child; <i>(May 10, 1851,Lyttelton Times,7</i>)

>  ... New Zealand must be regarded, not only as owning no dependence on the Mother-Country ... <i>(Sep 26, 1864,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Sep 26 Cardwell to Grey in 1949 Rutherford ed. Select Documents 152]

>  ...to send Jimmy Duncan to the Mother Country as a coach to the New Zealand team. <i>(Jun 24, 1905,NZ Truth,1/7/</i>)

>  <b>WAITE</b> The pioneer settlers of New Zealand left the Mother Country for many reasons, but primarily because they wished for a freer existence. <i>(1919,New Zealanders at Gallipoli,1</i>)

>  <b>McCRAE</b> ... inducements to a Great Nation ... to establish a Colony ... more likely to repay the fostering care of the Mother Country <i>(1820,Journal,10,1928 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> I can have no hesitation in asserting, that thousands of tons of this valuable article of commerce may be shipped off annually from New Zealand to the mother-country; ... <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,18,1979 ed.</i>)

>  New Zealand was a different land when Mr Hunt was elected in 1966... The country still "lived off the sheep's back" and was outwardly very much still a white child of "the mother country". <i>(Sep 20, 2003,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### mother hair
 
 <i>n.</i> halo hair

### Mother Hucks
 
 <i>n.</i> Theatre Royal Cafe, Christchurch [1954 (R. Mason) Apparently after a former proprietress.]

### mother of all budgets
 
 <i>phr.</i> [Popularised by Suddam Hussein's "the mother of all battles has begun" 17/1/1991 which ws picked up in Ruth Richardson's "mother of all budgets".]

>  Not so easy to understand is a government that has forgotten the lessons of 1991, when Ruth Richardson's ill-conceived "mother of all budgets" tipped an already shaky economy into a deep recession. <i>(Oct 12, 1998,Dominion,12</i>)

>  In 1991, the Ruth Richardson-Bill Birch "Mother of All Budgets" savagely slashed welfare benefits... Indeed, the clear consequence of the "Mother of All Budgets" was to see the economy contract. <i>(May 7, 2002,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  Dismantling either [proposal] would mean taking out some people's pockets - echoes of Ruth Richardson's 'mother of all budgets' <i>(Jan 24, 2006,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

### Mother of the Nation
 
 <i>n.</i> nickname of former One News presenter Judy Bailey

>  there's only one mother of the nation and she's just lost her job. <i>(Nov 16, 2005,Radio NZ / National Radio,Nine to Noon</i>)

>  Will the mother of the nation become the grandmother of the nation while we watch? <i>(Mar 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),p26</i>)

>  This is a place where they think that being beastly to the Mother of the Nation will have the ratings rocketing. <i>(Oct 22, 2005,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

### mother on
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to match a lambless ewe with a motherless lamb.

>  We usually mother-on in the lambing houses in the back yards if possible, rather than tying the sheep to a muddy fence-line. <i>(Jul 22, 1971,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> It is best that the lamb is hungry when mothered on, as there is then more chance of commencing the association with a suckling which can satisfy both the ewe and the lamb. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,51</i>)

>  <b>MILLS</b> .. one of any pair of twins should always be mothered on to a good sound ewe for whom no 'stray' is immediately available. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,94</i>)

>  Lambing was good fun for a couple of weeks, and I was dead keen to mother on every possible stray. [mother-on] <i>(Aug 2002,Growing Today,42</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a matched non-birth ewe and lamb unit.

>  In fact, in the house paddock where all the mother ons are released, we tailed over 100%. <i>(Nov 27, 1975,NZ Farmer,28</i>)

### mother seed producing area
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an area where a new strain of seed is introduced and grown.

>  <b>COULSON</b> .. the Department of Agriculture had stated that whilst it had in view a scheme for the certification of Akaroa seed, it was not yet prepared to declare Banks Peninsula a 'Mother Seed' producing area. 77 Banks Peninsula was ideally suited for the purpose of certification, and the whole district would be treated as a Mother Seed producing area with suggested boundaries from Little River to Mount Herbert and all east of that line. <i>(1979,Golden Harvest,74</i>)

### mother up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to match motherless lambs with lambless ewes

>  Wherever ewes are lambing there will always be lambs to mother up. <i>(Jun 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,296</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> Whenever he brings a lamb in, he insists on mothering it up to some other ewe as soon as he can. <i>(1953,Breakfast at Six,146</i>)

>  I mother up more than 100 lambs each year and rarely lose more than six this way. I put the ewe and lamb in a catching pen for a day or so to start with, and in difficult cases tether the ewe to a fence for a while. <i>(May 15, 1945,NZ Journal of Agriculture,502</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [ewes and lambs] to reunite after being moved or after a docking muster.

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> ..after getting them [ewes and lambs] over, be sure to wait a while to give them a chance to mother-up and string quietly away. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,85</i>)

>  I was to keep them [sc. sheep] there until dark to "mother-up". <i>(Feb 8, 1956,Weekly News,45</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> This reduced number is less difficult to provide with shelter during a storm, is easier to mother up and present fewer difficulties in shedding the ewes and lambs on to saved pasture or green feed, while tailing mobs can be built up with ease. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,48</i>)

>  <b>DU FAUR</b> Some farmers allow the ewes with the undrafted lambs to mother-up in a holding paddock near the yards. <i>(1966,Sheep Farming for Profit,109</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [cows and calves] to reunite after separation.

>  <b>DAVIS</b> Once the calves had mothered up again a few days later, they were quietly ridden out of the mob and sent to Waihora. <i>(1994,Lab Coats to Gumboots,8</i>)

### mother whitebait
 
 <i>n.</i> (?translation of a Maori term) smelt

>  <b>MANNERING</b> Another Maori name for the smelt is `rangiriri'. I have witnessed the autumn migration of these fish to the sea, both in Hawke's Bay and Taranaki, and seen Maoris filling sugar bags with them ... They usually call them `Mother Whitebait'. <i>(1943,Eighty Years,158</i>)

### mother-marking
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  ... nor do I believe in the necessity for the great precautions taken by the late Mr. Macombie [the breeder of black Angus] to have high paling fences erected, so as to prevent his pregnant cows from seeing other beasts passing ..., and thereby bring about cases of mother-marking. <i>(1901,TrNZI,33/191</i>)

### Mother-of-Cameron weed
 
 <i>n.</i> variant of Mother Cameron's weed

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Mother-of-Cameron weed, perhaps better known as St [sic] John's Wort, was troublesome on both the Aurum and The Branches stations in our time. <i>(1965,Skippers Road,159</i>)

### mother-of-pearl
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LYSNAR</b> The eyes of the tiki were filled with rings cut from the parva, or mother-of-pearl, but recently this has been quite superseded by the charm of red sealing-wax. <i>(1924,New Zealand (various authors/titles),62</i>)

### mother-of-thousands
 
 <i>n.</i> black nightshade

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> BLACK NIGHTSHADE (<i>Solanum nigrum</i>), is called also white nightshade, mother-of-thousands, black tomato, black potato, wonder berry, and by a serious confusion deadly nightshade and bittersweet. Increasingly common from Auckland southward to Otago. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,152,1967, p.  152 ed.</i>)

### mothering
 
 <i>n.</i> fostering

>  <i>Mothering</i>: Same as fostering. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,13</i>)

### mothering box
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I love watching the ewes accept the little orphaned lambs. I wonder if there is anything in the mothering box at the moment? <i>(Sep 10, 1980,Evening Post,40</i>) [NOTES: (Footrot Flats)]

### mothering on
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>STEWART</b> None of the recognised mothering on techniques of sheep husbandry would work with goats and we had a lot of orphans. <i>(1985,Gumboots and Goalposts,91</i>)

### mothering paddock
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a lambing paddock [where ewes and young lambs from larger station blocks are kept.] (See [maternity paddock](mar-moa.md#maternity-paddock))

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> As every ewe block had a set of yards and a mothering paddock, ewes and lambs were mustered in sections, with very early starts to avoid the summer heat. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,21</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Entrances to the mothering paddocks were a chain wide ... <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,22</i>)

### mothering pen
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a small sheep pen used for matching a motherless lamb with a lambless ewe, or a lamb with a reluctant ewe mother.

>  <b>MOORE</b> Picking up the orphan [lamb] I locate the bereaved mother [sheep], and, collecting her dead lamb, work her quietly into a mothering pen in the corner of the block. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,69</i>)

>  <b>PERRY</b> It is an excellent practice to erect mothering pens in the lambing paddocks. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,76</i>)

>  All that was left over from the previous day was a ewe and lamb in the Bush Paddock mothering pen. <i>(Aug 1959,NZ Dairy Exporter,115</i>)

>  A careful survey should be taken of the lambing fields, to pick out the best spots to place the mothering pens. <i>(Jul 2, 1923,NZ Farmer,922</i>)

>  Ewes and lambs are then transported to mothering-up pens. In West Otago it is common to see these mothering-up pens studded around the lambing paddocks. [mothering-up pen] <i>(Jun 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,595</i>)

### mothering up
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a matching or reunion of natural ewe and lamb in a large mob of sheep and lambs [especially after a muster, docking.] (See [re-mothering](../R#re-mothering))

>  <b>PERRY</b> Working in an open paddock also allows the subsequent mothering 'up' to be carried out more expeditiously. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,79</i>)

>  These [yards] will be of great value for mothering up or for sick animals. <i>(Sep 1, 1937,Weekly News,80</i>)

>  Good shepherding naturally leads to less mothering up being necessary. <i>(Jun 15, 1956,NZ Journal of Agriculture,593</i>)

>  <b>STEVENS</b> Throughout tailing, the dog has been actively but quietly engaged holding the ewes and lambs handy to allow quick mothering-up. <i>(1958,Sheep.  Part 1, Sheep husbandry,59</i>)

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> When mis-mothering takes place no serious attempt at 'reunions' should be made until the natural mothering-up process is almost completed. [mothering-up] <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,99</i>)

### mothering-on
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) the matching of motherless lamb and lambless ewe by enclosing both in a small pen [often with the dead lamb's skin over the adoptee].

>  <b>MILLS ET AL</b> Mechanised inspection also permits several ewes and lambs to be transported to the home environs where the job of mothering-on may receive closer attention. Another mothering-on method where fences are at a premium is to screw or drive auger-shaped fence standards into the ground to which the ewe may be tied. <i>(1964,Practical Guide to Handling Dogs and Stock,94</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> For the old shepherds, the best method of mothering on was to skin the dead lamb and fit this coat over the new one. <i>(1987,People, Places & Paddocks,58</i>)

### mothering-up
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>PERRY</b> Working in an open paddock also allows the subsequent mothering `up' to be carried out more expeditiously. <i>(1922,Sheep Farming in NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>MOORE</b> This `mothering-up' of odd lambs [to ewes who have lost their lambs] takes quite a lot of the shepherd's time. <i>(1969,From Forest to Farm,80</i>)

>  <b>TAYL;OR</b> `New carpet in your room, too, and at long last a dryer for the clothes. Bliss at lambing time.' `Don't tell me you use it for mothering-up. ...' <i>(1971,Plekhov Place, The,15</i>)

### mothering-up pen
 
 <i>n.</i>

### mottled kauri
 
 <i>n.</i> a valued variety of kauri or totara timber that presents a mottled appearance. -->1882 Hay

>  <b>HAY</b> Mottled totara is as much esteemed for cabinet work as mottled kauri. Some of the largest and soundest kauri timber has richly mottled shading, which appears to be an abnormal growth, due to the bark being entangled in the ligneous growth, causing shaded parts, broad and narrow, according as the timber is cut relative to their planes. This makes a rich and valuable furniture wood, and in the market is known as `mottled kauri'. <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/189</i>)

>  <b>MANDER</b> Besides this, Peter picked out as a gift mottled kauri for the front room and the ceiling and some fine bits of bird's-eye totara for the doors and cupboards. [also <i>mottling</i>] <i>(1925,Allen Adair,61,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARLOW</b> Mottled Kauri trees are usually found in rocky situations. <i>(1888,Kaipara and Waitemata Echo,113</i>)

>  <i>On Mottled Kauri</i>. by James Stewart, C.E. <i>(1875,TrNZI,7/376</i>)

### mottled moray
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>AYLING</b> Mottled Moray (spotted moray, saw-toothed moray).. Common around the offshore islands from the Bay of Islands north... The only species of moray that is aggressive enough to stand its ground when approached by a diver and if provoked will make a lightning fast bite before retreating. <i>(1982,Sea Fishes of NZ,92</i>)

>  <b>PAUL</b> Mottled Moray <i>Gymnothorax prionodon</i>. Brown with a mottled arrangement of light spots, variably spaced. <i>(1986,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,47</i>)

### motu
 
 <i>n.</i> island [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <i>motu n,</i> pl<i> motu</i>or <i>motus</i> [Maori, Tahitian, Tuamotuan, Marquesan, Samoan, & Tongan] : a Polynesian reef islet with vegetation <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1476</i>)

>  She also goes along to Kura Reo Maori immersion classes around the motu. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,5,Summer ed.</i>)

>  As the daughter of one of the claimants, the late John Hippolite of Ngati Koata, I am accutely aware how much this claim means to Maori throughout the motu. <i>(Jul 12, 2011,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Reward yourself during breaks from homework and mahi with the gossip from talented role-models from around the motu. <i>(2003,Te Mana  Taiohi,1,Winter ed.</i>)

>  .. news and gossip from talented role models from around the motu. <i>(2005,Te Mana  Taiohi,1,Autumn ed.</i>)

### Motuekan
 
 <i>n.</i> a resident of Motueka

>  Now there is a big Motuekan kick over the frightful expenditure, ... [of building a 2 (pounds) platform for a memorial service for Seddon]. <i>(Jul 14, 1906,NZ Truth,56/7/4</i>)

### Motunau coal
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Motunau Coal, Canterbury Plains, resembles that of Massacre Bay in its general character, but ignites more readily, and burns with less smoke; it contains a great quantity of sulphur but no traces of bituminous matter; its residue is composed of hard clinkers of brownish colour, and of powdery charcoal-like substance. <i>(Mar 15, 1851,Lyttelton Times,6</i>)

### mould up
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to ridge or raise the soil around plants in a crop. (See [ridger](../R#ridger))

>  Keep ground well stirred while plant [chou moellier] is growing and as it increases in size, it should be moulded up. <i>(Sep 15, 1913,NZ Journal of Agriculture,198</i>)

>  <i>Moulding, stuck (solid moulding)</i>- Formed or stuck on the solid, not planted. <i>(1979,SANZ Glossary Building Terminology,76</i>)

### Mount Cook buttercup
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) <i>Ranunculus lyallii</i>, an alpine plant [also known as giant buttercup]

>  The giant buttercup <i>R. lyallii</i> grows from Kahurangi National Park south to Stewart Island... [Alan Mark]described the 1973 distribution of <i>Ranunculus lyallii</i> - the world's largest buttercup - as follows: 'Introduced game animals have caused its decline to that large plants now tend to be restricted to inaccessible bluffs.' <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/2</i>)

>  <i>Ranunculus Lyallii</i> giant white buttercup <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Ranunculus Lyallii</i> white buttercup <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Ranunculus lyallii</i> MOUNTAIN LILY ... Mount Cook lily ... Great white buttercup <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,126</i>)

>  The giant buttercup <i>R. lyallii</i> grows from Kahurangi National Park south to Stewart island. <i>(Nov 2002,Forest & Bird,306/2</i>)

>  During Victorian times Ranunculus lyalli, the Mt Cook or Mountain Buttercup, was known in England as the "Rockwood Lily."... The exact connection between the "Rockwood Lily" and a Canterbury sheep station presents an interesting puzzle... Rockwood Station, owned by Henry Phillips, was one of the earliest established homesteads... However, the precise connection with Rockwood.. has yet to be discovered. <i>(Feb 11, 1988,Press, The,10</i>)

>  Most of the alpine plants, like the Mount Cook buttercup ( <i> Ranunculus lyallii </i> ) and vegetable sheep ( <i> Raoulia eximia </i> ) failed the test of easy "growability". <i>(Dec 2006,Growing Today,31</i>)

 <i>n.</i> alpine weta q.v.

### Mount Cook lily
 
 <i>n.</i> Ranunculus lyalli (See [great mountain buttercup](../G#great-mountain-buttercup),[shepherd's lily](sa-sho.md#shepherds-lily))

>  <b>PINNEY</b> In 1879 he sent down to Campbell at Otekaieke `a few roots and seeds of the Mount Cook Lily'. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,33</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> yet the whole place is relieved by a growth of Alpine flora; daisy, ranunculus, the Mount Cook Lily, with its plate-like leaves, and a great variety of Veronica. <i>(Oct 15, 1866,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,96</i>)

>  <b>GREEN</b> Of the smaller plants the great <i> Ranunculus Lyallii</i>, or Mount Cook lily as it is called by the colonists, was especially interesting, ... <i>(1883,High Alps of NZ,173</i>)

>  <b>HARRIS</b> (And)<i> Ranunculus Lyallii</i> Mount Cook lily <i>(1890,NZ Flowers</i>)

>  The Mount Cook, or Shepherds' Lily - no lily at all, but a buttercup. <i>(1895,Pictorial NZ,242</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>[R. Lyallii, Hook.f.]</i> It has received many local names, as the `mountain lily', `shepherd's lily', `Mount Cook lily', &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,10</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Ranunculus Lyallii (Lyall's Ranunculus)</i> ... The plant is known to colonists as the Mountain, Shepherd's, or Mount Cook Lily. The name is [167] most inappropriate, as the plant is not a lily, but a large white buttercup. However, any large-leaved herbaceous native plant is called by the colonists a lily, especially if it has white flowers. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,166,1940, p. 175 ed.</i>)

>  Mount Cook lily A popular name for the great mountain buttercup of New Zealand. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),266,8th ed.</i>)

>  <b>SEVERINSEN</b> Noteworthy of the Upper Branch [Canterbury] is a profusion of the lovely Mt. Cook Lily. <i>(1970,Hunt the Far Mountain,20</i>)

>  <i>Mount Cook Lily</i> South Island alpine flower <i>(1985,Collins Compact Dictionary,319</i>) [NOTES: nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; nf. 1986 NZPOD]

>  Earlier, while crossing Whitcombe Pass, I had drunk from the cup-shaped leaves of a Mount Cook lily. As broad as dinner plates, the leaves capture precious droplets of dew, rain and mist. Actually not a lily at all, <i>Ranunculus lyalli</i> is the world's largest buttercup... <i>(Sep 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 53/36</i>)

>  Twenty diamond rivulets go dancing down from the everlasting snow-fields.. now spraying the cup-like leaves of the Mount Cook lily, now laving the root of a rich broadleaf, or nourishing the blue koromiko blossom. <i>(Nov 11, 1897,Otago Witness,49</i>)

### mount the Peter
 
 <i>phr.</i> to get into the witness box

>  `I'll mount the Peter in the morning and give evidence about it,' she said, shrilly. His Worship: What does `mount the Peter' mean? - To come here, your Worship. <i>(Dec 31, 1910,NZ Truth,288/6/2</i>)

### Mount, the
 
 <i>n.</i> (informal) short for Mount Maunganui (Mauao).

>  You want to lounge about ...; to swim in the warm waters below the Mount; to sunbathe on Ocean Beach ... <i>(1948,Our Own Country,31</i>)

>  Her father looked towards the hump of the Mount in the distance. 'I think it's time to go,' he said... <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,64</i>)

### mountain
 
 <i>adj.</i> as a distinguishing epithet with names of plants often merely translates the specific <i>alpinus, montana</i>, etc. rather than originating in a general observation expressing itself in a given vernacular descriptive term: e.g. <i>Phyllocladus alpinus,</i> mountain toa toa -->a. chiefly botanical (rather than popular) uses include MOUNTAIN -BUTTERCUP, -COTTONWOOD, -FOXGLOVE, -HOLLY, -LANCEWOOD, -PALM, -PIRIPIRI, -TREE-DAISY, -TWITCH, WINEBERRY; b. popular (often local) uses distinguishing alpine or sub-alpine species include: (though often the vernacular name may have originally been botanically given.) e.g. MOUNTAIN-BEECH, MOUNTAIN-PINE, MOUNTAIN-RIBBONWOOD. (see Andersen Popular Names of NZ Plants Tr NZI; and 1933 Acland.) -->for animals see DUCK, KIWI, MINNOW, PARROT, TROUT -->as a distinguishing epithet in the names of plants see AKEAKE, ASTELIA, ASTER, BEECH; BEECH, SOUTHERN; BIRCH, BLADDERWORT, BUTTERCUP; BUTTERCUP, YELLOW; CABBAGE-TREE, CASSINIA, CEDAR; PINE, CELERY; CLUB-MOSS, COTTONWOOD, COTULA, CURRANT, DAISY, DANTHONIA, EVERLASTING; FERN, FILMY; FERN, SHIELDFERN; FERN TREE-FERN; FERN UMBRELLA; FLAX, FORGET-ME-NOT, FOXGLOVE, GENTIAN, GRASS, OAT-GRASS; GRASS, TWITCH; HOLLY, HYPERICUM, IVY-TREE, KOROKIO, LANCEWOOD, LILY, MARGUERITE, MOP, MUSK, MYRRH, PALM, PANAX, PINE; PINE, CELERY; PIRIPIRI, PRIMULA, RATA, RIBBONWOOD, RIMU, SEDGE, SNOWBERRY; TAUHINU, TAWHEOWHEO, TOATOA, TOETOE, TOTARA, TREE-DAISY; TUSSOCK-GRASS; TUTU UNCINIA, VIOLET, WILLOW-HERB, WINEBERRY
