### mountain beech
 
 <i>n.</i> Nothofagus solandri (var. cliffortioides), a beech common in drier subalpine forest.

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  Cockayne Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: (And) [N. clif]]

>  <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: (And) [N. clif] Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <i>Nothofagus cliffortioides</i>, TAWHAI-RAURIKI (cf BIRCH) perhaps mainly a scientific or trade term <i>(1925,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: also in 1917 edition (And) [N. cliff],]

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>N. Cliffortioides (The Cliffortia-like Beech)</i> ... The <i>Mountain beech. </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,140</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> The track, for most of the way, leads along the crest of a main ridge, and passes, for several miles, through an area of native mountain beech forest. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,77</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> [N. cliff] <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Fagus cliffortioides.</i> - This tree much resembles <i>F. Solandri</i>, but it is found generally only at high altitudes; it is unlikely to be of much value as a timber, though it is of great use to retard soil-erosion. The most suitable name for it is `mountain-beech.' <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/308</i>) [NOTES: (E. Phillips Turner)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F. Cliffortioides, Hook.f</i>. ... A small tree, usually from 20 ft. to 40 ft. high ... Mountain districts ... <i>Tawhai-rauriki; Mountain Beech or Birch. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,643</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>(1916,TrNZI,48</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> Usually it is silver beech with small toothed leaves but in the drier areas, especially Canterbury, eastern Nelson and Marlborough in the South Island and on the highly porous soils of the volcanic plateau in the North Island, silver beech [<i>Nothofagus solandri </i>var. <i>cliffortioides</i>] is replaced by mountain beech with small, smoothed-edged leaves. <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,10,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Five species of <i>Nothofagus</i> were formerly recognised in New Zealand, but more recently two of these have been combined as varieties of one species. ... <i>Nothofagus solandri</i> var. <i>cliffortioides:</i> mountain beech (grows mainly at higher altitudes than other beeches). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,121</i>)

>  <b>DENDY</b> As we ascend the character of the vegetation changes, the mountain beech [fn <i>Fagus cliffortioides.</i>] replaces that of lower altitudes, and the undergrowth consists largely of the alpine celery pine [fn <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i>.], an almost unique member of the order <i>Coniferae</i>, with the [188] customary needle-like leaves replaced by flattened leaf-like branches, ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,186, 188</i>)

>  [Settlers' name] Mountain beech .. [Family] Cupuliferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>KIRK</b> [N. cliff] <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: And)]

>  English Name Native name Botanical Name Mountain Beech .. Fagus Cliffortioides <i>(1889,Catalogue, Canterbury Court Exhibition,35</i>)

>  <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,9</i>)

>  Mr. Enys' house ... is 2,500 feet above the sea, and is beautifully situated in a clump of Mountain Beech (<i>Fagus cliffortioides</i>) ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/202</i>)

>  <b>HUDSON</b> [N. cliff] <i>(1898,NZ Moths & Butterflies</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Beech </i>... In New Zealand, there are six species of true beeches, which according to Kirk are as follows - ...MOUNTAIN B. <i>F. Cliffortioides</i>, Hook. f. ... All these, however, are commonly called <i>Birches.</i> <i>(1898,Austral English,24</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ...Bush or forest in the high country [of Canterbury] consists almost entirely of <i>Nothofagus cliffortioides</i>, the mountain beech. ...high country men usually call them `white birch'. <i>(1970,Glorious Morning,254</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>NOTHOFAGUS SOLANDRI </i>variety <i>CLIFFORTIOIDES. Mountain beech, widespread from East Cape southwards, is particularly abundant on poor mountain soils ... </i> <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,80</i>)

>  <b>DENDY</b> <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,186, 188</i>)

>  <i>mountain beech n, Austral & NewZeal </i>: a tree of the genus <i>Nothofagus </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1477</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Beech ... Mountain .. tawhairauriki <i>Nothofagus cliffortioides </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/710</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> Mountain beech (<i>N. solandri var. cliffortioides</i>) descends to river terraces ... <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,159</i>)

>  Lithograph of mountain beech foliage. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,648</i>)

>  [Ruahines] [We] take the track into splendid kaikawaka (pahautea) and mountain beech bush <i>(1978,Manawatu Tramping Club Jubilee,99</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>Crowe</b> Although mountain beech is capable of growing in the most severe conditions, high in the mountains, in the south it also grows right down to sea level. <i>(2009,Which Native Tree?,43</i>)

### mountain birch
 
 <i>n.</i> kamahi (mistakenly applied)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Letter to Strauchan] mountain birch or Kaimai <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,175</i>) [NOTES: 1892]



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Fagus cliffortioides</i> Hook. f. .. Mountain beech or birch.<i> [Fagus] fusca</i> Hook. f. .. Tawhai, black-birch.<i> [Fagus] Menziesii</i> Hook. f. .. Silver or red birch. <i>[Fagus]Solandri</i> Hook. f. .. White-birch. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> <i>Mountain Birch: Common misnomer of mountain beech [in Canterbury]. </i> <i>(1972,Mountain World,310</i>)

>  The house nestles under a forest of Mountain Birch, so-called, but really a beech, with very small leaves, ... <i>(Jul 20, 1861,Letter</i>) [NOTES: The house nestles under a forest of Mountain Birch, so-called, but really a beech, with very small leaves, ...]

>  <i> [Notho]fagus Cliffortioides</i>, Hook. f. ... Mountain beech. Black Birch of portions of Wellington, Canterbury, Otago and Southland. White Birch of Nelson, and in part of Otago and Southland. Barely distiguished by the woodman from Entire-leaved beech. The mountain beech bears a close resemblance to the entire-leaved beech in general appearance, although it never attains the extreme dimensions of that species.] <i>(1884,TrNZI,17/298</i>) [NOTES: (Kirk, NZ Beeches)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>F. Cliffortioides, Hook.f.</i> ... A small tree, usually from 20 ft. to 40 ft. high ... Mountain districts ... <i>Tawhai-rauriki; Mountain Beech</i> or <i>Birch. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,643</i>)

### mountain cabbage tree
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cordyline indivisa</i>

>  <b>TURNER</b> (And) <i>Cordyline indivisa </i> mountain cabbage-tree <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Turner Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <b>McLINTOCK3/705-712</b> Mountain broad-leafed cabbage tree .. toi, .<i>Cordyline indivisa </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An</i>)

>  Simpson says of our fifth species, C. indivisa, or mountain cabbage tree, that it "has pushed the concept of a tropical Gondwanaland tree lily to its limit". <i>(Jun 2, 2001,Dominion Post,14</i>)

>  C indivisa, the mountain cabbage tree, has wide drooping leaves and far outshines its imported lookalikes, the ubiquitous agaves. <i>(Sep 15, 2001,Dominion Weekend Magazine,10</i>)

### mountain celery pine
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka alpine celery pine, mountain toa toa; <i>Phylocladus alpinus</i>]

### mountain dog

### mountain duck
 
 <i>n.</i> = blue (mountain) duck.

>  <b>BUTLER</b> I have seen the rarest kind of mountain duck swimming within five yards of me ... as though I had no soul for roast duck seasoning. There are several ducks - the paradise duck is commonest. ... Then there are the grey duck and the mountain duck - rarer, less striking in their personal appearance, and better eating. <i>(Jun 1860,Forest Creek MS,44,1960 ed.</i>)

>  HYMENOLAIMUS MALACORHYNCHUS, Gml. Whio. Blue Duck, Mountain Duck. <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/73</i>) [NOTES: (Potts Birds)]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 101. HYMENOLAIMUS MALACORHYNCHUS. Gml. Blue, or Mountain Duck. Whio. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> In the smaller water-courses were numbers of Mountain ducks (<i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i>) which with Paradise ducks (<i>Casarca variegata</i>), Scaups (<i>Fuligula Novae Zealandiae</i>), ... formed a welcome addition to our stock of provisions. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,37</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Blue or mountain ducks (<i>Nestor rotabilis</i> [sic - Harper is shaky on scientific names], are not now found in any numbers except in the upper parts of hitherto unvisited rivers, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,42</i>)

>  <i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i> Gm. Blue Duck; Mountain Duck. This beautiful and characteristic species maintains itself wherever the mountain-creeks which form its home are undisturbed by the too near approach of settlement. <i>(1923,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,6/83</i>) [NOTES: Myers Endemic Birds]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> The geographical formation of Tutira - conglomerate and limestone superposed [check ??superimposed] on `papa' - is well suited to the `Blue,' or `Mountain Duck.' <i>(1927,Birds of New Zealand,41</i>)

>  <b>15</b> <i>Mountain</i> ... As adjective, often used as part of the name of a species found in the back country, mostly smaller than their down-country relatives, e.g. m. pine, totara, minnow, duck (blue duck), daisy, lily (Mount Cook lily). (C.) [M. musk, used for flavouring tobacco, is Celmisia novae-zelandiae A.W.] <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The</i>) [NOTES: (Acland Gloss.)]

>  <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,160</i>) [NOTES: (OEDS)]

>  <b>VON HAAST</b> In the smaller water-courses [in Southern Alps] were numbers of Mountain Ducks (<i>Hymenolaimus malacorhynchus</i>) which with Paradise ducks (<i>Casarca variegata<i/>), and Scaups (<i>Fuligula Novae Zealandiae</i>) and others obtained ...formed a welcome addition to our ... provisions. <i>(1948,Julius Von Haast,216</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1951,Early Canterbury Runs</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Mountain - Duck, several species of Anatidae - and in New Zealand apparently applied colloquially to <i>Tadorna tadornoides</i> (Sheld-drake). [?Is this supposed to be the paradise duck] <i>(1894,Dictionary of Birds,600</i>)

### mountain five-finger
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Pseudopanax colensoi</i>

>  <b>DAWSON</b> Mountain five-finger (<i>Pseudopanax colensoi</i>) also has a wide range, but is absent north of 36[degrees]S and from Stewart Island. I have observed it growing as an epiphyte on kaikawaka or mountain cedar (<i>Libocedrus bidwillii</i>) on Mt. Taranaki ... <i>Ibid.</i> 166 Some of the other shrub species with relatively large thick leaves are <i>Griselinia littoralis</i> (broadleaf), <i>Pseudopanax colensoi</i> (mountain five-finger) and <i>Olearia colensoi</i> (leatherwood). <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,82</i>)

### mountain flax
 
 <i>n.</i> mountain(-)flax <i>Phormium colensoi</i>

>  There are two species: Phorium tenax, also know as harakeke, whararahi or swamp flax; and phorium cookianum, also known as wharariki or mountain flax. <i>(Apr 22, 2006,Dominion Post,Indulgence (supplement)/ 20</i>)

>  One of the features will be plantings of mountain flax, a broad-leafed cabbage tree that grows around about [Ohakune]. <i>(Oct 3, 1998,Dominion,13</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>P. Colensoi</i>, a smaller species, is found on dry hillsides.) <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,111</i>)

>  <i>mountain flax [s] Phormium colensoi</i> Hook.f. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,50</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> Notable here [among flowering plants] are the several alpine species of snow tussock (<i>Chionochloa</i>) and speargrass (<i>Aciphylla</i>), also the mountain flax (<i>Phormium cookianum</i>). <i>(1973,NZ Alpine Plants,17,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Phormium colensoi</i> mountain-flax <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

>  <b>TURNER</b> (And) <i>Phormium colensoi</i> mountain-flax <i>(1909,AJHR,C-11</i>) [NOTES: Botanical Examination Higher Waimarino]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Phormium colensoi</i> mountain-flax <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>LEVY</b> [Table 1] Mountain flax <i>(1951,Grasslands of NZ,88,1970 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> As he aged, Hermit's fleece grew very long and dingy looking, making it hard to distinguish him from one of the many dead mountain flax bushes that dotted the slopes about. <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,125</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> He loved to scramble through the maze of angled rock in a mountain gorge ... that unrestrained world of rock and snow grass and astelia and mountain flax, the shingle screes and the outcrop spurs. <i>(1968,Pagan Game, The,120</i>)

>  <b>BROOKER et al.</b> <i>Phormium cookianum</i> Le Jolis Common name: <i>Mountain flax</i> Maori name: <i>Wharariki ... </i> Many authors have used <i>P. colensoi</i> but this name is antedated by <i>P. cookianum. </i> <i>(1981,NZ Medicinal Plants,35</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Phormium cookianum </i>MOUNTAIN FLAX ... Wharariki (= <i>P. colensoi</i>). <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,300</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> <i>(1940,Te Waimate,246,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SUTHERLAND</b> Under the lee of a rock outcrop, sheltered on two sides by clumps of mountain flax, we set up house; a ten by [200] ten feet tent under a twelve by twelve feet fly with a ten by ten feet fly extended from the tent as a firefly, to give some shelter to the chef at his cooking pots. <i>(1953,Golden Bush, The,199,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>11/273</b> The first two are of importance to the flax-miller, while in the third is included the mountain-flax (<i>P. Cookianum</i>). <i>(1930,NZ Journal of Science & Technology</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> On the more sheltered aspects, so-called mountain flax (<i>Phormium cookianum</i>) may form a close cover <i>Ibid.</i> 181 The term `flax' for the genus <i>Phormium</i>, although firmly established, is yet another inappropriate common name as <i>Phormium</i> and true linen flax (<i>Linum</i>) have in common only the possession of useful fibres. Mountain flax is <i>Phormium cookianum</i> and its tufts of narrow, leathery leaves may be conspicuous in poorly drained places in tussock herbfield and shrubland. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,156</i>)

### mountain foxglove
 
 <i>n.</i> Ourisia spp.

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story,2nd ed. ed.</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> In the spring a number of ourisias flower on the Paparoa tops, including the mountain foxgloves <i>O. macrocarpa</i> and<i> O. macrophylla </i>(the latter of which also grows in the lowlands). <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,165</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> The Ourisias are sometimes called mountain foxgloves. This is a common name with at least some validity since foxgloves and <i>Ourisia</i> belong to the same family. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,178</i>)

### mountain gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a mustering gang. (See [flying gang](fin-fly.md#flying-gang),[hill gang](hea-hom.md#hill-gang),[hill team](hea-hom.md#hill-team))

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> Young Bill McNutt from Wanaka was my mate in the mountain gang. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,20</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> And a hillman never mustered with the mountain gang again. <i>(1981,Tales of the High Country,41</i>)

### mountain goat
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who climbs hills, or steep places, as well as a mountain goat (esp. trampers' use)

>  ...like a lot of mountain goats. <i>(1952,Tararua,36</i>)

>  ...of streets that only a mountain goat or a resident of Hataitai could love. <i>(Jul 1, 1952,Evening Post,11</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> Bedford vehicle used on Mt Ruapehu (and elsewhere) to take skiers up the mountain before skilifts

>  <b>GAJHADAR</b> The name given to the four-wheel drive vehicle at the Chateau Tongariro, used to take skiers by a narrow often snowbound road up to the skiing grounds on Mount Ruapehu. <i>(Jul 1952,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: (Local Ruapehu, Chatean Hotel)]

### mountain ivy-tree
 
 <i>n.</i> Nothopanax Colensoi =three-finger

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i> Nothopanax Colensoi </i> mountain ivy-tree <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Next we have the FIVE-FINGER, the THREE-FINGER, the HAUMAKAROA, and the RAUKAWA, all species of <i> Nothopanax </i>... the three-finger or MOUNTAIN IVY-TREE has as a rule three sessile leaflets. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,183,1961 ed.</i>)

### mountain kiokio
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Blechnum montanum</i>

>  Only the alpine shield fern (<i>Polystichum cystostegia</i>), bladder fern (<i>Cystopteris tasmanica</i>), mountain kiokio (<i>Blechnum montanum</i>), thousand-leaved fern (<i>Hypolepis millefolium</i>), finger ferns (<i>Grammitis poeppigiana</i> and <i>G. givenii</i>), one filmy fern (<i>Hymenophyllum villosum</i>) and two clubmosses (<i>Huperzia australiana</i> and <i>Lycopodium fastigiatum</i>) are found primarily in this habitat <i>(Jan 2001,NZ Geographic,No. 49/78</i>)

### mountain lily
 
 <i>n.</i> Ranunculus lyalli = Mount Cook lily

>  <b>DENDY</b> mountain(-)lily, Ranunculus lyallii; =Mount Cook lily. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,6</i>)

>  <i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i> mountain lily <i>(1880,TrNZI,12 :</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i> Ranunculus lyallii </i> MOUNTAIN LILY ... Mount Cook lily ... Great white buttercup <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,126</i>)

>  <b>PRINCE</b> The Saddle itself is ... studded with Ranunculus Lyalli (mountain lily), several forms of Celmesia (mountain daisy), and with Edelweiss plants ... <i>(1889,Diary of a Trip,9</i>)

>  mountain lily : large white buttercup. <i>(1947,Reeds' School Dictionary: NZ Suppl.,175</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> All this alpine meadow was beautiful with flowers: giant celmisias with satiny-white petals like enormous daisies, mingled with snow-white gentians, and the wonder of the Alps - the mountain lily. <i>(1911,Through South Westland,6</i>)

>  Just picture to yourself acres and acres of Ranunculus Lyallii (the so-called mountain lily) in full bloom. <i>(Jan 8, 1924,Otago Witness,47</i>)

>  <b>HETLEY</b> (And)<i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i> mountain lily <i>(1888,Native Flowers of NZ</i>)

>  <b>FEATON</b> RANUNCULUS LYALLI ...The `SHEPHERDS' OR MOUNTAIN LILY'. ... It is indigenous to the Middle Island ... at an elevation of 2000 to 3000 feet ... It has been named by the shepherds of Otago the `Water Lily' on account of its fancied resemblance to that plant. <i> Ranunculus godleyanus </i> ... The most graphic description would fail to give any adequate idea ... of the purity and charm of these Alpine Flowers [sc the white <i> R. Lyalli </i>] growing ... side by side with the yellow Mountain Lily (<i> R. Godleyanus </i>), and interspersed with Alpine marygolds (<i> Senecios </i>) and the little star-like <i> Montias </i>.... the shepherds or Mountain Lily ... <i>Ranunculus Lyalli.</i> <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,6</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> .. (And) <i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i> mountain lily <i>(1890,Descriptive & Priced List</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> [Letter to Strauchan] ...mountain Lilly ... [sic] <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,176</i>) [NOTES: 1892 (1957) Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas]

>  (And) <i>Ranunculus Lyallii</i> mountain lily <i>(1890,Zealandia</i>) [NOTES: July 1889- June 1890]

>  <b>HARPER</b> The most beautiful is the now well-known Mountain Lily (<i> Ranunculus Lyalli </i>) ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,265</i>)

>  On both sides [of the Southern Alps] grows the sub-alpine vegetation in all its beauty. You may walk knee-deep in Mountain Lilies, you may pick Edelweiss [fn] by the handfull [sic]; the air at a certain season of the year is full of the fragrance of the Alpine Broom [fn 2]. [fn] The Mountain Lily is ... a buttercup, and the Edelweiss does not belong to the same genus as its European namesake. [fn 2] <i> Carmichaelia grandiflora </i>, var.<i> alba </i>. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,203</i>) [NOTES: L.Cockayne]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> [<i> R. Lyallii, Hook.f.</i>] It has received many local names, as the `mountain lily', `shepherd's lily', `Mount Cook lily', &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,10</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i> Ranunculus Lyallii (Lyall's Ranunculus)</i> ... The plant is known to colonists as the Mountain, Shepherd's, or Mount Cook Lily. The name is [167] most inappropriate, as the plant is not a lily, but a large white buttercup. However, any large-leaved herbaceous native plant is called by the colonists a lily, especially if it has white flowers. <i>(1907,Plants of NZ,166,1940 p. 175 ed.</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> The mountain-lily (<i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i>) is their [sc. buttercups'] queen. <i>(1910,NZ Plants,93</i>)

>  <b>NAIRN & SON</b> (And) <i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i> mountain lily <i>(1912,Catalogue (Nairn & Son)</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i> mountain-lily <i>(1919,NZ Plants</i>)

>  <b>TANNOCK</b> The mountain lily, when well grown, is very beautiful, and it thrives in some gardens. <i>(1921,Manual of Gardening,142</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i> Ranunculus Lyallii </i> mountain-lily <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>)

>  <b>COWAN</b> Most abundant are the giant alpine buttercup (Ranunculus Lyalli), popularly called `mountain lily', and the celmisia or mountain daisy, called by the Maoris <i> ti-kumu.</i> <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,Vol II South Island) 70</i>)

>  As adjective, often used as part of the name of a species found in the back country, mostly smaller than their down-country relatives, e.g. <i> m.</i> pine, totara, minnow, duck (blue duck), daisy, lily (Mount Cook lily). (C.) [M. musk, used for flavouring tobacco, is Celmisia novae-zelandiae A.W.] <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MYERS</b> `We have the Edelweiss, just like Switzerland. It's a little, flannel-like flower, found on the steep crags of our Alps. And in the meadows [160] grow mountain lilies ... Here, also are mountain daisies called cotton plants. ...' <i>(1941,Valiant Love,159</i>)

>  <b>SOLJAK</b> Of these [sc. alpine flowers] the most common are the edelweiss, ... the white or yellow mountain daisy, of which there are no fewer than sixty varieties, and the mountain lily, which is native to the lower slopes of the Southern Alps. <i>(1946,New Zealand (various authors/titles),12</i>)

>  <b>HUNT</b> It has in the erroneously called ... Mountain Lily (<i> Ranunculus lyalli </i>) the largest buttercup in the world. <i>(1951,Confessions,136</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Then they both went on, leaving me sitting among the mountain lilies, and with my boots off and but one thought in my head - to bathe my blistered feet. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,4</i>)

>  <i> mountain lily n 2 </i> : a showy white-flowered buttercup (<i> Ranunculus lyallii </i>) of New Zealand <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1477</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> English names of animals and plants are sometimes mistakenly applied. the great buttercup of the alps, <i> Ranunculus lyalli </i>, is called the mountain lily; the indigenous black nightshade, <i> Solanum nigrum </i> is often called deadly nightshade which is a very different species. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/680</i>)

>  <b>SEDDON</b> Daisy-like celmisias grew in drifts and between rocks near the series of little lakes at the top of the pass, and fields of mountain lilies which older people called ranunculas opened their white petals and nodded to the passer-by, ... <i>(1968,Seddons, The,57</i>)

>  <b>BRASCH</b> Higher than birds climb from their marble forest/ The mountain lily nests/ In shade among scrawled rocks and snow-grass plumes,/ <i>(1968,Collected Poems,87</i>)

### mountain maire
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Native name] Maire .. [Settlers' name] Mountain maire .. [Family] Jasmineae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,121</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 121]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Rororo, ... Olea montana, mountain olive. </i>[so 1957/348] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,405</i>)

### mountain mop
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dracophyllum traversii</i>, neinei

>  R. Mason notes: f. tufts of leaves at end of bare branches. In use amongst Canterbury Mountaineers. I first heard it 1943, at first aquaintance with a Canterbury mountaineer and the plant in the field together. <i>(Aug 1946,Bulletin of the Wgtn Botanical Soc.,14/8</i>)

>  It is unnecessary to write at length in general of New Zealand plant names, but mention may be made of the <i>Tararua dishmop</i> and the <i>mountain mop</i>. Apparently the first is given to <i>Raoulia tenuicaulis</i>, one of the scabweeds, by Heretaunga Tramping Club. No doubt plenty of Tararuas have used it with sand or ashes to clean out a billy, little thinking that their actions might inspire a new plant name, given to it by Canterbury mountaineers apparently, from the tufts of leaves at the ends of the branches. <i>(Sep 1958,Tararua,30</i>) [NOTES: Ruth Mason `Track and Trail]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> This might be described as a miniature of the MOUNTAIN MOP or NEI-NEI (D. traversii). <i>(1961,Flora NZ,227,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>MARTIN</b> This might be described as a miniature of the MOUNTAIN MOP or NEI-NEI (D. traversii). <i>(1961,Flora NZ,227,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mountain mop Dracophyllum traversii </i>plants used by trampers in Canterbury area as cleaner for pots and billies. (Cf Tararua dishmop.) <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

### mountain mule
 
 <i>n.</i> a pack

>  I was astonished at what he carried [in his old Mountain Mule Pack] - the contents of a hardware store, grocery shop ... and always willing to share it. <i>(Feb 18, 1999,Evening Post,5</i>)

### mountain musk
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Did not want of space forbid I could tell ... of grassy glades carpeted with Celmisia, whose aromatic scent [209] fills the air. This plant the shepherds call the Mountain Musk [fn], and smoke it mingled with tobacco for the fragrance it affords. [fn] <i>Celmisia discolor, Cel.incana </i>and <i>Cel. Sinclairii. </i> <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,208</i>) [NOTES: (L. Cockayne)]

>  <i>Celmisia incana</i> mountain musk <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: Cockayne Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park(And)]

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Celmisia Sinclairii </i> ... <i>Celmisia incana</i> white mountain musk <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  M[ountain] musk, used for flavouring tobacco, is Celmisia novae-zelandiae. - A[rnold] W[all]. <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>HARPER</b> This shelter [on the Karangarua River, Westland] we named `Musk Camp', because here our only firewood was mountain musk, as it is generally called. It is a shrub of the myrtle species, of a sage green colour, and grows to a height of four feet [only above 2500 ft level]. <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,218</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> South Island: sub-alpine scrub ... Mountain musk, Incense-plant. ... <i>Olearia moschata (The Musky Olearia)</i> ... Mountain musk, Incense-plant. <i>O. nummularifolia</i> is another species of the sub-alpine scrub. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,446</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a plant used to enhance the smell of tobacco by shepherds.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The scent of mountain musk clings to the high basins ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,33</i>)

>  <b>HOLGATE</b> This plant the shepherds call the Mountain Musk, and smoke it mingled with tobacco for the fragrance it affords. <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,210</i>)

>  <b>WATERS</b> There is also a mountain musk which I often took home to mix with my tobacco. <i>(1976,Mostly a Farmer,89</i>)

### mountain mutton
 
 <i>n.</i> venison (in the days when espec. commercial shooting or use was a punishable offence)

>  <b>ELVY</b> It pulled up at the Pelorus Hotel, Canvastown, and ... the travellers went to the dining room, where mine host, Tom Dillon, presided at the head of a long table and carved the joints. `What'll you have, beef or mountain mutton?' he asked. ... The young man gave his order ... and was served with slices of juicy, tender venison. In those days deer were protected, to be shot only in season, and under licence, so what the travellers ate was poached. It was a feature of the hotel, under mountain mutton. <i>(1900,Reminiscences</i>) [NOTES: [ca1900] W.J. Elvy Reminiscences in 1953 Millar High Noon (1965) 88]

### mountain myrrh
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Eight genera have but a single species, the native carrot (<i>Daucus brachiatus</i>) and the mountain myrrh (<i>Oreomyrrhis andicola</i>) being two with no very attractive features. <i>(1961,Flora NZ,288</i>)

>  Sometimes mountain [beech] and <i>Dracophyllum traversii</i> (mountain neinei) are also present. <i>(1990,Tramping in South Island Forest Parks,62</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Dracophyllum Traversii</i>, mountain mop.

>  The low canopy is dominated by mountain neinei, the Dr Seuss-like Dracophyllum traversil. <i>(Mar 13, 2004,Dominion Post,E17</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Dracophyllum Traversii</i> mountain-neinei <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Dracophyllum Traversii </i> <i>(1910,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The,? 1919 ed.</i>)

>  The Mountain Neinei (D. Traversii) bears aloft its tufts of long, wide, grass like leaves. <i>(1936,Hills and Valleys,38</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> [Neinei, D. latifolium] is replaced in the mountainous districts of the South Island by the closely allied D. Traversii.) <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,351</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>DRACOPHYLLUM TRAVERSII</i>. Mountain neinei is an example of the larger mop-headed species ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,114</i>)

### mountain oysters
 
 <i>n.</i> (usu. pl.) sheep's or lamb's testicles [In OED from 1890 Cent. Dict. s.v. Oyster, *Mountain-]

>  And the crew ends up with a dish that uses lamb tails and also sneaks in lamb "mountain oysters". <i>(May 10, 2005,Dominion Post TV Week,15</i>)

>  There's the big-crowd puller of whitebait, slugs, ostrich meat, pickled ponga, worms, octopus, eel, kangaroo, mutton bird, truffles, paua, crayfish and mountain oysters (sheep testicles). <i>(Mar 8, 2001,Evening Post,3</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i> mountain oyster</i>: Unlike the beautiful Bluff oysters, these do not come in shells. They are the GONADS of RAMS. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,68</i>)

>  <i>mountain oysters NZ</i> lamb's testicles as food. <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,491</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>mountain oysters</i> lamb testicles, for some a gourmet delight. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

>  Ata was born on a piece of bloodied sacking, sliding out warm and screaming, surrounded by scattered lambs' tails and beside a bucket of mountain oysters. <i>(2003,Huia 5: Contemp. Maori Fiction,158</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) lamb testicles [boiled or roasted over fire out-of-doors along with lambs tails and regarded as a great delicacy at the completion of a docking day.] [occ. used for other animals.]

>  <b>MCGILL</b> mountain oysters: lamb testicles, for some a gourmet delight. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,73</i>)

>  <b>PLUMMER</b> We also had a frying pan and when we knocked off for lunch, as well as boiling the billy, we would fill the frying pan up with lambs balls, which the best people called 'mountain oysters.' The mountain oysters went down very well with most of us .. Jim Case took a poor view of those mountain oysters. <i>(1994,From Drover to Director,35</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> mountain oysters: testicles of young lambs, saved at tailing time. Rural gourmets consider them not unlike an oyster in flavour. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,54</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Some bush wag, years ago, named these delicacies mountain oysters. <i>(1993,Station Country I,35</i>)

>  <b>BEACH</b> John, who was in the middle of gutting it [a wild pig] said. 'You can have the mountain oysters,' (the bits between the back legs). <i>(1993,Over 30 Years Pig Hunting,16</i>)

### mountain palm
 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) Cordyline indivisa

>  New Zealand flax is also spoken well of for shelter, together with Chatham Island ake-ake, taupata, and karo, whilst cabbage trees stand the blizzard, and so also does the mountain palm. <i>(Jul 14, 1900,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  <i>Cordyline indivisa </i> <i>(1916,NZ Association of Nurserymen, Trade Register</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Cordyline indivisa </i> <i>(1917,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  There are mountain palms or <i>titoi </i>(cordyline indivisa), and <i>mamaku</i> fern-trees ... <i>(1926,Travel in NZ,(Vol II South Island) 148</i>)

>  The rare and handsome Toi, or mountain palm, grows on most of the lower slopes. <i>(May 6, 1891,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  A waterproof cloak, made by a Waitara native from the mountain palm (cordyline indivisa), from leaves grown in Mr. Thompson's garden, was inspected by many... <i>(Mar 18, 1898,Hawera & Normanby Star,2</i>)

>  There is on view to-day at Messrs Laird and Sons' seed shop a splendid flower head of the Cordyline Indivisa, better known perhaps as the "Mountain Palm", and by the natives as "Toi". <i>(Oct 9, 1909,Wanganui Herald,4</i>)

### mountain parrot
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> indigenous parrot, the kea [aka kea <i>Nestor notabilis</i>]

>  A Gazette notice states that hawks of all species, the kea or mountain parrot, and the black shag, white-throated shag and sea-shag, all species of birds indigenous to New Zealand, are not to be deemed protected. <i>(Dec 16, 1920,Poverty Bay Herald,4</i>)

>  <b>Harper</b> Also the New Zealand mountain parrot, the Kea, ... likewise tame and impudent, ... <i>(Jul 10, 1868,Letters from Canterbury, NZ,1914</i>)

>  The bird, which is very rare, is a mountain parrot, a dweller in the Alpine heights, and was probably driven down by the unusual inclemency of the weather to Mr. Tripp's station, at Mount Peel, where it was caught. <i>(Sep 28, 1861,Nelson Examiner and New Zealand Chronicle,3</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 52. NESTOR NOTABILIS. Gould. Mountain Parrot. Kea. <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,47</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> NESTOR NOTABILIS. (KEA PARROT.) ... <i>Native names</i>. `Kea' of the Maoris; \{Kea and Keha;\} `Mountain-Parrot' of the colonists. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,52,{1888 2nd edn 1/166} ed.</i>)

>  A number of Keas (the green mountain parrot) ... broke the stillness of nature with their plaintive notes. <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,117</i>)

>  52. <i>Nestor notabilis</i>, Gould. Mountain or Green parrot, Sheep-killer, Kea, Kia, Keha, Keo, Kaieo. - By all these names is this troublesome bird known by the highland shepherds and sheep musterers in alpine districts from Lakes Wanaka and Hawea to the river Rangitata. <i>(Jul 1885,NZ Journal of Science,481</i>) [NOTES: (T.H. Potts)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kea</i> (i), <i>keha</i>, n. <i>Nestor notabilis, mountain parrot</i>; probably so called from its screech. [so 1957/112] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,131</i>)

>  <b>BAXTER</b> kea <i>mountain parrot</i> <i>(1982,Selected Poems,188</i>)

>  Polly is a kea, or mountain parrot. <i>(Sep 8, 1938,NZ Observer,10</i>)

>  MESSRS. W. H. MORRAH AND CO. will sell at their rooms as above - .. pair stuffed huias, mountain parrot ... <i>(May 29, 1902,Evening Post,8</i>)

### mountain pine
 
 <i>n.</i> [<i>Halocarpus bidwillii</i> previously <i>Dacrydium bidwillii</i> aka bog pine]

>  <b>Kirk</b> Dacrydium Bidwillii mountain pine <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  <b>Howard</b> From about 900 feet upward the forest merges into a manuka scrub ... and mountain pine (Dacrydium Bidwillii ) up to ... about 1,500 feet. <i>(1940,Rakiura,xviii</i>)

>  Away across the carpets of moss we make for a small belt of mountain pine on the right of the plateau, and into which runs the track. <i>(Apr 17, 1886,Otago Witness,1</i>)

>  Pine, mountain (tarwood) .. Dacrydium Bidwillii .. Timber hard, and used for firewood Ibid. 17 81 Mountain pine slab. <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,13</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> occasional name for the yellow silver pine or yellow pine [<i>Lepidothamnus intermedius</i>, previously <i>Dacrydium intermedium</i> yellow silver pine or yellow pine but sometimes dubbed mountain pine. see http://www.nzetc.org/tm/scholarly/tei-Bio04Tuat03-t1-body-d4.html]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Dacrydium intermedium</i> mountain pine <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>D[acrydium] intermedium, T.Kirk ... Mountain-pine; Yellow Silver-pine.</i> Wood reddish-yellow, highly resinous ...; largely used in Westland (together with <i>D. Colensoi</i> [Silver-pine, Manoao]) for railway sleepers, ... &c. <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,655</i>)

### mountain primrose
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

### mountain rata
 
 <i>n.</i> Metrosideros umbellata (formerly M.lucida) (cf RATA) [nb 1827 unique reference to black rata]

>  <b>MATGTHEWS</b> (And) <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i> M[etrosideros] lucida , A.Rich. ... Mountain-rata. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,163</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> It grows in masses on the slopes of the Southern Alps.) <i> Metrosideros lucida </i> <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,294</i>)

>  The lower portions [of the Auckland Islands] are usually covered with bush, which consists mostly of mountain rata (<i>Metrosideros lucida</i>), which grows to a height of 30ft. ... <i>(1889,TrNZI,21/382</i>)

### mountain ribbonwood
 
 <i>n.</i> [aka mountain lacebark; found in SI mountain forests <i>Hoheria glabrata</i> mostly in the west, and <i>H. lyallii</i> mostly in the east, with overlap in Central Otago.] (See [houhere](hom-hyp.md#houhere))

>  (And) <i>Plagianthus Lyallii</i> ribbon wood <i>(1896,Dunedin Field Club Plant Catalogues</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>Hoheria Lyallii</i> (The Mountain Ribbonwood). ... South Island, sub-alpine. ... (<i>Gaya Lyallii</i>) <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,268</i>)

>  [caption] The mountain ribbonwood, <i>Hoheria lyalli</i>i, was discovered at Dusky Sound by Forster on Cook's second voyage ... <i>(1971,New Zealand's Heritage,1/114</i>)

>  <b>DAWSON</b> <i>Hoheria glabrata</i> or mountain ribbonwood colonises young, deep, moist, weel-drained and often stony soils such as those provided by slips, talus slopes and alluvial fans. <i>(1988,Forest Vines to Snow Tussocks,165</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> (And) <i>Gaya ribifolia</i> mountain ribbonwood ... (And) <i>Plagianthus Lyallii </i> <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> John was to climb up on our right and continue around through the high snow tussock until he was above the ribbonwoods, giving him an opportunity of getting any wapiti that Roy and I might drive out .. <i>(1952,Deer Hunter: The Experiences of a NZ Stalker,183</i>)

### mountain run
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country pastoral property.

>  <b>CAIRD</b> .. where they [keas] are numerous they are said to attack the sheep .. on some mountain runs, therefore, regular crusades are made against them. <i>(1874,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,17</i>)

>  A sub-lease of part of the mountain run also affords Mr Revie scope for a small hill flock. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,35</i>)

>  <b>MCMILLAN</b> Twenty years back on mountain runs in many a sweaty shed we earned our cheques. <i>(1975,More From the High Country,23</i>)

### mountain scrub
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shrubby tree that grows at high altitudes.

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Plastered with snow and loking like immense snowballs, the poor little brutes [hoggets] went rolling down to fetch up against a big snow grass bush or mountain scrub far below. <i>(1974,Kingdom in the Hills,143</i>)

### mountain sheep
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) Raoulia lutescens, a plant growing on high hill and mountain faces in white, cushion-like masses, with a distant appearance of sheep. (See [vegetable sheep](../V#vegetable-sheep))

>  Masses of mountain-sheep (Raoulia lutescens) occupying the former site of tussock pasture. <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,12</i>)

>  The most notable plant to appear is one of the mountain-sheep (Raoulia lutescens) which forms large, rounded, cushion-like masses, and is commonly known as "scabweed." <i>(Jun 15, 1910,NZ Journal of Agriculture,14</i>)

### mountain station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high country holding or run.

>  In Otago also the front country has suffered severely, but some of the mountain stations in the interior must likewise have lost heavily. <i>(Aug 22, 1903,Farmers' Union Advocate,5</i>)

### mountain toatoa
 
 <i>n.</i> indigenous pine of mostly alpine habitat [<i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> a variety of toatoa, of North Island hill-forest, celery pine]

>  <b>Martin</b> The name CELERY PINE in common usage is almost restricted to Phyllocladus alpinus, the others being called tanekaha and toatoa, ... This celery pine or MOUNTAIN TOATOA is an abundant plant in subalpine beech forests ... <i>(1961,Flora NZ,171,1929 ed.</i>)

>  [Native name] Toatoa .. [Settlers' name] Mountain toatoa .. [Family] Coniferae <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,122</i>) [NOTES: 1886 T.Kirk in 1902 Settler's Handbook of NZ 122]

>  <b>KIRK</b> (And) <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> mountain toatoa <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>)

>  Toatoa, mountain .. Phyllocladus glauca .. Timber white, straight grained, and tough; ... <i>(1890,PWD Catalogue of Timbers,15</i>)

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>P[hyllocladus] alpinus, Hook.f. </i>... A shrub or small tree, usually from 8 ft. to 25 ft. high, ... <i>Mountain Toatoa. </i> <i>(1906,Manual NZ Flora,659</i>)

>  (And) <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> mountain-toatoa <i>(1908,AJHR</i>) [NOTES: 1908 Cockayne Botanical Survey Tongariro National Park]

>  <b>CHEESEMAN</b> <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> mountain toatoa <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> mountain-toatoa <i>(1925,Duncan & Davies' Catalogue</i>) [NOTES: (And) (mis-spelt totoa)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> This celery pine [<i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i>] or MOUNTAIN TOATOA is an abundant plant in subalpine forests ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,171,1929 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i> Hook. f .. Mountain-toatoa. <i>(1918,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,1/144</i>)

>  <b>MARK & ADAMS</b> These shrubs, often no higher than the tussocks, vary somewhat from place to place but snow totara <i>(Podocarpus nivalis)</i>, mountain toatoa (<i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i>) and some of the mid-sized species of <i>Dracophyllum (D. uniflorum) Hebe (H.odora)</i> and <i>Coprosma (C. pseudocuneata) </i>are widespread. <i>(1873,NZ Alpine Plants,18</i>)

>  <b>DENNIS</b> In less fertile places stunted rimu is regularly joined by ... silver pine (<i>Dacrydium colensoi</i>), yellow silver pine (<i>D. intermedium</i>), and mountain toatoa (<i>Phyllocladus alpinus</i>). <i>(1981,Paparoas Guide,159</i>)

>  Woody scrub, including bog pine and mountain toatoa, grows around the edge of the clearing. <i>(1983,Land of the Mist,66</i>)

### mountain toetoe
 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> a sub-alpine variety of toetoe

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Beside the tall snow grass and the larger MOUNTAIN TOETOE<i> (D[anthonia] cunninghamii) </i>other smaller species of <i>Danthonia</i> occupy extensive areas of the subalpine grasslands of South Island. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,130,1961 ed.</i>)

### mountain totara
 
 <i>n.</i> (Environment) a subalpine variety of totara, <i>Podocarpus nivalis</i> (See [also alpine totara](all-ari.md#alpine-totara))

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> Grows at an altitude of from 2,000 to 4,000 feet. The specific name has reference to its sub-alpine habitat. <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,68</i>)

>  A small stand of Hall's totara (<i>Podocarpus hallii</i> or mountain totara) has also survived. <i>(Jul 2005,Open Space,No 64</i>)

>  But after following this spur probably two or three miles, through a bush consisting of light or mountain totara timber, it will be found to turn nearly south, and leaving, as before, the Pelorus gorge on the east side of it. <i>(Apr 19, 1864,Colonist (Nelson)</i>)

>  Of the mountain Totara - the most durable of its family - fine specimens are shown. <i>(Dec 24, 1872,Star, The,2</i>)

>  <b>Santa</b> Test item for database upgrade <i>(Dec 25, 2011,Star, The,1</i>) [NOTES: Happy Christmas]

>  Returning to Cronin Creek, we noticed good specimens of Senecio Bidwillii, Olearia elaegnifolius, Coprosmas, and the Mountain Totara, a species of the yew family, which would flourish even in Siberia. <i>(Jul 23, 1887,Te Aroha News,4</i>)

>  For a rugged groundcover shrub, Eadie recommends the mountain totara, Podocarpus nivalis, which - despite its lofty-sounding name - rarely reaches more than one metre tall. <i>(Sep 16, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Podocarpus hallii</i> (See [totara](ten-tot.md#totara))

 <i>n.</i> <i>Cyathea colensoi</i>

### mountain trout
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Galaxias </i>spp., kokopu, rock-trout [kokopu (cf cockabully) (see Morris for distribution of species: not confined to N.Z.)] (See [kokopu](kiw-kwi.md#kokopu))

>  The correct name of the Mountain Trout or Black Kokopu is <i>Galaxias argenteus</i> Gmelin and the Barred Trout or Kokopu is apparently the young, with conspicuous markings. <i>(1939,TrRSNZ,69/230</i>) [NOTES: (Whitley and Phillipps)]

>  ... mountain-trout <i>(1840,NZ Journal,1/16/205</i>)

>  Looking into the deep, clear stream beneath, hundreds of mountain trout are to be seen sporting in its waters. <i>(Sep 24, 1908,Marlborough Express,3</i>)

>  ".. And I have seen the kingfisher dart into the creek and take up a small mountain trout time and again." <i>(Jan 29, 1910,Evening Post,14</i>)

>  <b>GILLINGHAM</b> The town [of New Plymouth] is situated between two small rivers, both of which abound with mountain trout and eels, and their waters are as good as any I have ever tasted <i>(Mar 2, 1842,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Mar 2 Stephen Gillingham Letter in 1940 Establishment of New Plymouth 203]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mountain-Trout, n.</i> species of <i>Galaxias</i>, small cylindrical fishes inhabiting the colder rivers of Australasia, Southern Chile, Magellan Straits and the Falkland Islands. On account of the distribution of these fish and of other forms of animals, it has beeen suggested that in a remote geological period the area of land above the level of the sea in the antarctic regions must have been sufficiently extended to admit of some kind of continuity across the whole width of the Pacific between the southern extremities of South America and Australia. <i>(1898,Austral English,304</i>)

>  <i>Galaxias kokopu</i> mountain-trout. Generally so called in Westland. W3 2. an Aust upland minnow (<i>galaxias coxi</i>). <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/88</i>) [NOTES: (Clarke)]

>  <i>(1899,TrNZI,31/86</i>) [NOTES: [check quot]]

>  <i>Galaxias fasciatus:</i>[European Name] Native trout or mountain trout. [Maori Name] Kokopu. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  The correct name of the Mountain Trout or Black Kokopu is <i>Galaxias argenteus</i> Gmelin and the Barred Trout or Kokopu is apparently the young, with conspicuous markings. <i>(1939,TrRSNZ,69/230</i>) [NOTES: (Whitley and Phillipps)]

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> Koukoupara - is a fish like the kokopu, but differently marked, and following the example of one of his informants, the translator [sc Beattie] has translated this as `mountain trout'. Koukoupara is the spelling adopted in the lists, but the [Maori] compilers have varied this with kopara, koupara, and kokaupara. Never once do they use the southern form of kokopara, which the southerners pronounced kokobala, which the early settlers soon turned into `cockabully'. <i>(1945,Maori Place-Names of Canterbury,63</i>)

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> Mountain Trout or Giant Galaxias <i>Galaxias argenteus</i> (Gmelin) (Syn. <i>G. alepidotus</i> Bloch and Schneider, <i>G. forsteri</i> Cuv. and Val. <i>G. grandis</i> Clarke, preocc., and <i>G. kokopu</i> Clarke) <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,399</i>) [NOTES: 1955 Whitley Namelist in 1956 Graham NZ Fishes 399]

>  <b>GRAHAM</b> KOKOPU <i>Galaxias fasciatus</i> Gray. It is often called Mountain [115] Trout but it is also found practically at sea level, just above high- tide mark, as I have often caught specimens in small creeks near the sea at Portobello, in Otago Harbour. <i>(1956,NZ Fishes: An Identification Guide,114</i>)

>  I am informed that the streams there abound in mountain trout, eels, &c. <i>(Jun 20, 1874,Taranaki Herald,2</i>)

>  They offered to kill a pig, but I preferred the lake fish, of which there is but one sort, being a description of small speckled mountain trout, similar to that met with in the streams of Van Dieman's Land; about eight inches long, of a very delicate flavour, and making most excellent soup. <i>(Sep 9, 1842,New Zealand Colonist & Port Nicholson Advertiser,4</i>)

>  A splendid specimen of the <i>salmo fario</i>, or mountain trout, was killed yesterday in Sawyer's Creek. <i>(Oct 26, 1881,Grey River Argus,2</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Galaxias robinsonii </i> [see OED, AND; Some NZ specificity.]

### mountain tutu
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a high-altitude tutu species, Coriaria angustissima or C. plumosa.

>  <b>MCCONACHIE</b> There were too a number of plants of mountain daisy, mountain tutu, gentian, and a pleasing array of that most beautiful alpine flower the Ranunculus lyalli. <i>(1966,You'll Learn No Harm from the Hills,67</i>)

### mountain wash
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McKAY (1897)</b> At higher levels, under and surrounding the upper township, there is what is called the "mountain-wash," a thick deposit of coarse gravels evidently derived from the eastern slopes of.. the Carrick Range. <i>(1894,Report on the Older Auriferous Drifts of C.Otago,28</i>)

### mountain wind
 
 <i>n.</i>

In a few hours all the dead trees and stumps in the neighbourhood were on fire. <i>(Feb 24, 1854,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/141,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J. Maria Richmond)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Subalpine shrubs worthy of note include the ROCK MAHOE (<i>Hymenanthera alpina</i>), the MOUNTAIN WINE-[231] BERRY (<i>Aristotelia fruticosa</i> ), the ALPINE BLUEBERRY (<i>Suttonia nummularia</i>), and an Exocarpus. <i>(1929,Flora NZ,230, 232,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Aristotelia fruticosa</i> MOUNTAIN WINEBERRY ... Shrubby [sc <i>fruticosa</i>] wineberry ... Shrub up to 1 m. ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,61</i>)

### mountain wren
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HARPER</b> The canaries (<i> Orthonyx ochrocephala </i>) and the little mountain wrens (<i> Xenicus filviventris </i>) [sic] are useful as foretellers of the weather, ... <i>(1896,Pioneer Work in the Alps of NZ,39</i>)

### mountainsides
 
 <i>n.</i> a fish

### mounted police
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The sight of `mounted police', - men whose business it was, in the penal colonies to apprehend escaped convicts and hunt down `bush-rangers' - patrolling the beach [at Wellington] with fetters in their hands, for the intimidation of the inhabitants, served but to prepare them [the Company settlers] for the still harsher usage they were to meet from local government. <i>(1842,Narrative of a Residence in  Various Parts of NZ,9</i>)

>  <b>COLENSO</b> ... brushing by the [mounted] police who, in uniform, were keeping guard before the door. <i>(1890,Treaty of Waitangi,13,1940 ed.</i>)

### Mounteds, the
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  THE MOUNTED RIFLEMAN [title] New Zealanders in these regions are popularly referred to, in their Homeland, as `The Mounteds'; and as being indicative of their pecularities in method of warfare, the term has its value. In origin, the Mounted Riflemen date back to the 'sixties, when that fine soldier, Major Sir Harry Atkinson, formed the settler-riflemen into a mounted force of a nature demanded by the topographical and tactical conditions of the fiercely-contested Maori wars. <i>(Aug 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,Second Series 2/16</i>)

### mouse-ear chickweed
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cerastium glomeratum</i>

>  [On Molesworth there is] some Scotch thistle; chickweed; and the larger mouse-ear; ... <i>(1919,NZ Journal of Agriculture</i>) [NOTES: c1919 Cockayne Journal of Agriculture in 1969 McCaskill Molesworth 154]

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> ... others like Mouse-ear chickweed (<i>Cerastium glomeratum</i>), Silene (<i>Silene gallica</i>), and the native Houtawai (<i>Acaena australis</i>) had ... fastened their seeds to the legs and fleeces of sheep. <i>(1926,Tutira,166</i>)

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> MOUSE-EARED CHICKWEED (<i>Cerastium [glomeratum]</i>) [which is sometimes] often called mouse-ear for short, is found in cultivated fields and thin pastures throughout both islands. ... The leaves are downy, [pale yellowing-green] and oval (whence the name `mouse ear'), ... <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,77,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> MOUSE-EAR CHICKWEEDS (<i>Cerastium</i> spp.) ... This introduced cosmopolitan weed is frequent in clover which is sown under cereals,in lucerne in winter and spring, and in thin pastures in the drier districts, especially on light land. <i>(1981,Weeds of Lawns,72</i>)

### mouth
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>DARE</b> They were `mouthing the dry sheep' - whatever that meant - ... I would be able to tell the grannies by looking at their teeth ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a grazing animal.

>  Retaining surplus cattle progeny means in times of drought the Aitkens are able to quit a lot of mouths before having to sell capital stock <i>(May 19, 1993,NZ Farmer,22</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to estimate the age of sheep by assessing teeth numbers and condition [ in order to assess whether sheep should be retained for breeding or sold.]

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> We began mouthing sheep. <i>(1863,Chudleigh Diary,105</i>) [NOTES: Diary (1950)]

>  <b>DARE</b> They were 'mouthing the dry sheep' - whatever that meant ... <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,8</i>)

>  <b>STAPLES</b> The Boss mouthed the sheep checking the condition of their teeth. <i>(1964,Cooks and Shepherds Come Away,24</i>)

>  <b>MAF</b> Hence [by examining teeth] you can 'mouth' or age a sheep and estimate its age. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,49</i>)

### mouth power
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the control of pastoral growth by stock.

>  Do you need the extra ewes on for mouth power? <i>(Feb 17, 2001,Fencepost.com,ashleycole</i>)

### mouther
 
 <i>n.</i> one who tells a sheep's age from its mouth

>  [We] began mouthing sheep. Polhill who has bought the run and the sheep took down the mouths on paper as the sheep are to be paid for according to their ages. ... You catch a sheep in your arms, carry it to the gate where the mouther stands, hold the mouth open for him to look at and you at the same time call out two, four, six, full or whatever it may be. ... The mouther has to be very quick and catch the mouth at once and put a dot under whatever age it may come on paper. So you know the number age sex etc. of your whole mob. <i>(Oct 1, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,105,1950 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a person in a drafting team who inspects the mouths of sheep [ to assess whether they should be retained for breeding or sold. ]

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> You catch a sheep in your arms, carry it to the gate where the mouther stands, hold the mouth open for him ... <i>(1863,Chudleigh Diary,105</i>) [NOTES: Diary (1950)]

### mouthfeel
 
 <i>n.</i> [?an introduced term]

>  While still providing the smooth mouthfeel that only fats and oils can bring to food, they also bring distinctive flavours, which take time and experience to appreciate and use with discernment. <i>(May 3, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),43</i>)

>  The "body" or "weight" of a wine is mainly determined by its alcohol content. Alcohol adds a sweetness and roundness to wine which you not only taste but also feel in the mouth (wine tasters call this "mouthfeel"). <i>(Dec 17, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D7</i>)

>  Though there are hints of lemon scent on the nose, mostly this $20 wine's about mouthfeel: a fleshy mixture of musk and oil and flowers and ... hang on, am I describing a massage or a wine? <i>(Jan 26, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,5</i>)

>  The tiny jelly-like balls of sago contribute a lovely mouthfeel. <i>(Mar 27, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),41</i>)

>  Amazing mouthfeel, a fat smoky , multi-layered mid-palate, beautifully balanced, linear, elegant and stunningly styled. <i>(Feb 2, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,5</i>)

>  "Mouthfeel" is one recent example that has made the cut into <i>The Oxford Dictionary</i>". <i>(Nov 9, 2002,Dominion Post,F7</i>)

>  One of grapedom's most aromatic varieties, 2001 turned sauvignon on its head, making it more about mouthfeel and flavour. <i>(Sep 1, 2001,Dominion,7</i>)

>  Then, just to take the oily mouth-feel one startling step further, there are slivers of briefly seared salmon. <i>(Nov 14, 2002,Dominion Post,D4</i>)

### mouthing
 
 <i>n.</i> checking the mouths of sheep for age etc. [1985 citation belongs with the verb to mouth]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Jack was constantly on the farm, `mouthing ewes in the [136] yards', stooking with the children in the oats paddock, `foot-rotting the cull half-bred ewes', or `shifting sheep'. <i>(1985,Coldstream,135</i>) [NOTES: 1913 diary in Studholme Coldstream (1985) 135-136]

>  <i>Mouthing.</i> Determining the age of sheep by examining their teeth. <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an examining of a sheep's teeth to assess its age.

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> mouthing: the annual task of determining the age of each sheep by examining its front teeth. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,55</i>)

>  The photograph shows part of the preparation of a breeding flock for the season, which entails mouthing, drafting for wool, age and other factors. <i>(Feb 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,122</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> What with mustering, drafting, and mouthing on the farm; then the 11-mile drive to the saleyards, then more drafting and sorting into age-groups ... <i>(1989,Pioneer's Life, A,84</i>)

### mud flounder
 
 <i>n.</i> = black flounder

>  <b>WILSON</b> FLOUNDER, BLACK More sole-like in shape than his cousin flounders this fellow also has small, brick-red spots on his back and has the ability - unlike the other flatfish - of being able to live in pure, fresh water. Other names are `River Flounder' and `Mud or Freshwater Flounder'. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,72</i>)

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER ... Other names: <iRhombosolea retiaria</i>; mud flounder, river flounder, estuary flounder; patiki-mahoao (Maori). <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,215</i>)

>  <b>MORELAND</b> BLACK FLOUNDER [<i>Rhombosolea retiaria</i>] ... River flounder and mud flounder are widely used alternative names. <i>(1967,Marine Fishes,32</i>)

>  <b>AYLING</b> BLACK FLOUNDER (Mud flounder, patiki-mahoao) <i> Rhombosolea retiaria </i>... Coloration is distinctive; the back is usually dark grey-green with darker mottling and a variable number of brick red spots. <i>(1982,Collins Guide,310,1984 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PAUL et al.</b> [index to common names 283-293] flounder, mud 2287 <i>(1987,Fishes: A Bibliography</i>)

### mud grubber/mud-grubber gurnard
 
 <i>n.</i> = stargazer, river gurnard

>  <b>DOOGUE & MORELAND</b> STARGAZER ... <i>Other names: Leptoscopus macropygus</i>; river gurnard, mud gurnard, flat-head gurnard, mud grubber. <i>(1966,Sea Anglers' Guide,262</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> Most gurnards are brownish-red but larger specimens are often bright red on top and white below. The Stargazer is sometimes known as the Mud Gurnad, River Gurnard or Mud-Grubber Gurnard but is in fact no relation. Similarly the Sea Perch is sometimes called a Rock Gurnard - ... <i>Ibid.</i> 208 STARGAZER A distinctive estuarine fish which spends most of its life buried in mud up to the eyes. ... Comes in two varieties - the Common Stargazer and his tubbier cousin the Spotted Stargazer. ... Other names are River Gurnard, Mud Gurnard, Flathead Gurnard and Mud Grubber. <i>(1981,Fisherman's Bible,118</i>)

### mudfish
 
 <i>n.</i> the general name for the varieties of New Zealand fish which can live in mud or damp surroundings

>  The fish is the New Zealand mud-fish (Neochanna apoda). It is usually found in clay, in places which are overflowed by rivers when they are in flood. <i>(Sep 25, 1912,Otago Witness,76</i>)

>  <b>HECTOR</b> A most curious fish, allied to the [<i>Galaxias</i>], is the Mud Fish (<i>Neochanna apoda</i>), ... <i>(1872,Edible Fishes,130</i>)

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 97. NEOCHANNA APODA. Gunth. Mud Fish. <i>(1872,Fishes,61</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... he produced a dish of water containing some `mudfish' which [the Bittern] instantly ... devoured. This singular fish (named by Dr. Gu[dieresis]nther <i>Neochanna apoda</i>) is very common in the Hokitika district, being found in all the creeks and surface-ponds in the woods ...On the pools becoming dry these mudfish burrow into the moist soil or clay, ... remaining there for an indefinite time, or until the return of the rainy weather rendered their pools habitable again. <i>(1873,Birds of New Zealand,237</i>)

>  <b>SHERRIN</b> <i>Neochanna apoda</i> Gunther Mud-fish <i>(1886,Handbook Fishes of NZ,305</i>)

>  <b>PRESHAW</b> a species of mudfish, Neochanna apoda, which seems to burrow underground <i>(1888,Banking Under Difficulties,175</i>) [NOTES: (Griffiths Collection)]

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mud-fish, n.</i> a fish of Westland, New Zealand, <i>Neochanna apoda</i>. Gunth. Gunther says <i>Neochanna</i> is a `degraded form of Galaxias [see <i>Mountain-Trout</i>], from which it differs by the absence of ventral fins. This fish has hitherto been found only in burrows which it excavates in clay or consolidated mud, at a distance from water.' <i>(1898,Austral English,305</i>)

>  <b>DRUMMOND & HUTTON</b> The mudfish is found in clay or mud, several feet below the surface,... <i>(1902,Nature in NZ,76</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... several species of Galaxies, a mud-fish ( <i>Neochanna apoda</i>), only found in the west coast rivers of both islands ... <i>(1922,Naturalisation of Animals and Plants,185</i>)

>  <i>Neochanna apoda</i> Guenther. MUDFISH; HAUHAU or WAIKAKA. <i>(1927,Bibliography of NZ Fishes,14,1971 ed.</i>)

>  <i>Neochanna apoda</i>: [European Name] Mudfish [Maori Name] Hauhau or waikaka. <i>(1936,Handbook for New Zealand, The,73</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPPS</b> THE MUD-FISH <i>Neochanna apoda</i> Gunther ... The mud-fish is also a member of the family Galaxiidae; but differs from members of the genus Galaxias in that it has no ventral fin. <i>(1940,Fishes NZ,41</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Hauhau </i>(i) n Neochanna apoda, <i>mud-fish = waikaka. </i> <i>(1957,Dictionary of the Maori Language,39</i>)

>  <i>mudfish n c</i> : a New Zealand fish ( <i>Neochanna apoda</i>) of the family Galaxiidae that lives in burrows like a crayfish <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1482</i>)

>  <i>mud fish n</i>. N.Z. fish that burrows in mud, distant from the water. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <b>707</b> <i>(1979,Heinemann NZ Dictionary</i>)

>  <i>mudfish n. NZ</i> fish that burrows in mud <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,493</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 739]]

>  <i>mud fish n</i> New Zealand fish that burrows in mud, distant from the water. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  The swamp is a breeding ground for native fish, including mudfish, kokopu and inanga. <i>(Nov 2003,NZ Geographic,No. 66/96</i>)

### mug
 
 <i>v.</i> (to make) preliminary love-play; pet [ca 1940 at least Wellington (The term <i>mug</i> etc is now obsolescent (perhaps obsolete) except in the occas. slang use <i>muck up</i>. It was most popular in the begining of the 20c up to, say, the 1930's. The common sense now (1984) is `to rob with violence'.)]



 
 <i>v.</i> to pet, to smooge, to kiss [(also vbl n mugging)]

### muka
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a strip of flax fibre. [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>TAIT</b> The dressing of the flax (muka) was continued when Mr Flay took up the land. <i>(1956,Farms & Stations of NZ Vol I,65</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> Plaited whips, complete with handle, leather thong and muka lash. Muka is the Maori name for the flax fibre after the green has been scraped from the fibrous skeleton <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,143</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> edible part of nikau

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> . . .at the bottom of the leafs [of the nikau], a fruit or white substance is found, out of which grows the leafs. This substance is called by the natives `Pito' (pronounced peetaw [pitau]). The middle is in seperate [sic] layers called `Muka' from its resemblance to the flax, eaten by the natives, ... <i>(1844,NZ Journal,108,1956 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> the prepared fibre of New Zealand flax [ORIGIN: Maori]

>  <b>POLACK</b> The flax (<i>korari</i>,) forming the dresses is of two or [176] three kinds; the most coarse, called muka, is employed for working the common garment (<i>kakaou</i>[a1-acute] <i>maori</i>[a-acute].) This flax is also made use of for laying up ropes, lines, &c. The superior dress called Kaitaka[a2-a3-acute], is made from silken flax, <i>(hunga hunga</i>[as-acute].) <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/175</i>)

>  Muka of the natives, as the dressed fibre of the Harakeke, or Flax Plant) <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/48</i>) [NOTES: (Colenso Botany North Isd)]

>  The Korari is the name of the flower stalk, often, but wrongly, used as the name of the plant. The edible root is the Aruhe. The word `Muka' is commonly applied to the dressed fibre; it is the correct name for the inner leaves. Cleared bush-land is suitable for the cultivation of the flax plant, and even swamp-land is improved by drainage. In the various flax swamps near nelson ...the finest plants grow upon the soil that has been thrown out of the ditch ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/117</i>) [NOTES: (Heaphy NZ Flax)]

>  The renovation of `Rangiatea' is a much bigger undertaking. ... The wall tukutuku are about 16 feet high while in the gables they run up to 30 feet. The old fellows must have put in a lot of thought to piece together such lengths against a real kakaho backing, with muka [ed fn: Prepared flax] binding to keep the whole in position. To keep the horizontals from sagging with the sheer weight of the material the muka binding was passed through holes bored into the edges of the poupou - an adaption of the rauawa technique. <i>(Mar 17, 1931,Na To Hoa Aroha,2/128,1987 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Ngata)]

>  Aesthetically beautiful and entirely natural, the stunning Hapene range is exclusively designed and produced in Fielding, and uses a patented method to release the muka, or fibre, within the leaf. <i>(Sep 2005,Air New Zealand inflight magazine,12</i>) [NOTES: -]

>  <i>muka</i>, prepared fibre of flax (harakeke). <i>(1946,Journal of the Polynesian Society,55/154</i>) [NOTES: Andersen Maori Words in English]

>  <b>REED</b> [Mrs Brown recounts:] At other times we would sit down amongst some flax bushes, and make `muka' for whip-lashes. <i>(1953,Story of the Kauri, The,141</i>)

>  <b>BOSWELL</b> ... beautiful, compactly plaited whips complete with handle, leather thong and muka lash. Muka is the Maori name for the flax fibre after the green has been scraped from the fibrous skeleton ... <i>(1960,Ernie and the Rest of Us,143</i>)

>  <b>HULME</b> I was playing, making knots in the muka in the sun/ and I dropped the tangle, forgot it/ <i>(1982,Moeraki Conversations,16</i>) [NOTES: Hulme The Silences Between]

>  <b>KEENE</b> <i>muka</i>: prepared fibre of flax. <i>(1963,O Te Raki,193</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> Mooca .. Thread, cord, rope. <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),76</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> Flax Moka <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/336</i>)

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> You will send the <i>Active</i> full of moca (muka), potatoes, limes, mats, fish, and nets. <i>(Mar 9, 1814,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1814 Mar 9 Marsden in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 58]

>  <b>MARSDEN</b> The women employ part of their time in making kakkahows (kakahu), <i>mats, moka</i> (muka), <i>etc.,</i> ... <i>(Jun 25, 1814,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1814 July 25 Kendall in Elder Marsden's Lieutenants (1934) 67]

>  <b>CRAIK</b> In this prepared state it is ... called mooka ... <i>(1830,New Zealanders, The,181</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Muka - flax prepared <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/374</i>)

>  a mat of muka flax is then made. It is then filled with berries and is placed in a hangi (oven) underground on heated stones. <i>(1844,NZ Journal,99,1956 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HOOKER</b> Muka, <i>W.Mantell.</i> Dressed fibre of <i>Phormium tenax. </i> <i>(1867,Handbook NZ Flora (List of Names),766</i>)

>  <b>BAINES</b> He said that the natives all the country round about would bring me for sale a great amount of `muka' scraped flax - ... and wheat without <i>(1874,Edward Crewe,116</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The fibre (<i>muka</i>) was then steeped for three or four days in running water, taken out and pounded with a stone beater. <i>Ibid.</i> 258 Sometimes the chisel was not dipped into the pigment but a wad of scraped flax (<i>muka</i>) with the pigment smeared thereon was drawn over the wound ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,222</i>)

>  <b>PINFOLD</b> As the blood oozes from the wound, it is dexterously wiped away with the `muka', a small bunch of finely prepared flax. <i>(1930,Fifty Years In Maoriland,25</i>)

>  <b>BUCK</b> <i>(1949,Coming of the Maori, The,3/351</i>)

>  <b>COLLINS</b> E-mu-ka, <i>The flax plant when dressed. </i> <i>(1804,English Colony in NSW,561</i>)

>  <b>LINDSAY</b> In Otago the general name of the [flax] plant, as a whole, is `Korari,' - or as metamorphosed by the settler, pronounced and spelt `Kouradi,' [a-macron] or `Coradi' [a-macron]. The same term is applied in the North Island equally to the whole plant, and to the dried flower-stem when cut for rafts or other purposes; or exclusively to the flower-stalk. In the North [83] Island, the Flax-plant is also known as `Harakeke,' or `Herekiakia,' - its usual name there. ... The lower part of the flower-stem is `Kaikaha;' the outer part of the leaf `Parakoka;' [a1, o-macron] the refuse of the leaf `Hunga-hunga;' the flax, dressed or not, or scraped fibre, manufactured from the leaf, `Whitau,' or `Muka.' <i>(1868,Contributions to NZ Botany,82</i>)

>  <b>Ihimaera</b> 'You will need stitches,' Paraiti says. She makes a thread of muka and uses a wooden needle to sew the wound. <i>(2007,Ask the Posts,252</i>)

### Muldoonery
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  We were planning by the dying glow of Muldoonery. <i>(Nov 1995,North & South,128</i>)

>  Are we then to presume that in sensing imminent economic disaster, plagues of Rogernomics and a famine from Muldoonery, the bay's good people have turned to more mighty beings than the present government. <i>(Sep 12, 1986,National Business Review,10</i>)

>  Not only that, the Burmese have abandoned `Muldoonery' after decades of controls. <i>(Jul 29, 1988,Contact (Wgtn),7</i>)

>  Do we really want Helen Clark deciding Judy Bailey's salary? That's pure Muldoonery. <i>(Jul 19, 2005,Radio NZ / National Radio,Nine to Noon</i>)

>  Between $8 billion and $11 billion(depending how you do the sums) had to be written off as a result of misguided Muldoonery. <i>(Aug 31, 2004,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  It seems a lifetime since David Lange was swept into office on a tide of assurances that, after nine turbulent years of Muldoonery, the country would be restored to a nirvana-like state of stability, oneness and certainty. <i>(Jun 17, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  Despite the party's protests to the contrary, that's essentially more "Muldoonery" at a time when New Zealand already has abundant infrastructure. <i>(Nov 29, 1992,Sunday Times (NZ),19</i>)

### Muldoonese
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  "Send the people who took part to Australia," cried Langwell, appropriately dropping into classic Muldoonese. <i>(Aug 29, 1992,Listener (New Zealand Listener),72</i>)

### Muldoonian
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  [He] is displaying the worst type of residual Muldoonian thinking. <i>(Feb 1992,Metro,8</i>)

### Muldoonism
 
 <i>n.</i> (Politics) the political (and economic) style of the National Government Prime Minister (Sir) Robert Mulddon (19 - to 1984) [ORIGIN: eponym]

>  Muldoonism is in the language and in the history, but there is a touch of tragedy listening to a man who refused to back down. <i>(Sep 16, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),63</i>)

>  Kerr said the PM.s call for his sacking "reminds one of Muldoonism." <i>(Dec 17, 2000,Sunday Star Times,C3</i>)

>  The epithet `Muldoonism', used by politicians, schoolchildren and businessmen alike, has become a familiar word in the Kiwi vocabulary. <i>(Feb 14, 1973,Dominion,7/6</i>)

>  They [cool TV newsreaders] were symbols of a benign paternalism that paraded the daily doses of murder, mayhem and Muldoonism past us and wordlessly reassured us: `There, there, it won't really hurt you. ...' Whatever did happen to that old comfortable world; has it all been Rogernomicised out of existence? Happily no. What we used to call social justice and now term wimpishness still lives. <i>(Nov 7, 1986,National Business Review,11</i>)

>  Under his tutelage New Zealand learned to live beyond its means as it had never done before, to such an extent that nobody seems to know quite how large its total foreign debt has become, except that it is certainly huge and may be a per capita world record. Its welfare state, which has often been held up as an example to the world, was allowed to swell as its traditional and all-important agricultural markets shrank. As the budget deficit widened, Sir Robert became an apparently immovable fixture, the political Mr Micawber of the southern hemisphere. Now the voters have awoken to the unreal nature of what long since became known to them as `Muldoonism', its creator and chief exponent could not bring himself to accept his stinging defeat, like many another unseated authoritarian before him. <i>(Jul 22, 1984,Guardian Weekly, The</i>)

>  <i>The Rose</i> was topical, they said, because it dealt with Muldoonism at its height. ... The play was just as much a comment on the political reactions of New Zealanders as it was about Muldoonism. For once, [Roger] Hall was creating and taking sides. <i>(Aug 9, 1986,Listener (New Zealand Listener),39</i>)

>  Laidlaw says he was a traitor - to Muldoonism, not to his country. <i>(Dec 4, 1989,Listener (New Zealand Listener),5</i>)

>  In the meantime the word `strong' needs urgently to be redefined. For the majority of men - and increasing number of women - it is at present simply Muldoonism brought into the sphere of personal and professional relations. Deny your most [119] tender feelings, destroy those who reveal themselves as vulnerable or sensitive, put down the opposition like dogs, polarise everybody in sight, create plausible scapegoats, ... and hate ... with all the venom you can muster. <i>(Jan 1987,Metro,118</i>)

>  Meurant simply seized on probably the only appealing aspect of Muldoonism these days for the born-again Nats, the former prime minister's cheery ability to throw ... allegations of treason ... <i>(Oct 8, 1987,National Business Review,10</i>)

>  Remember the Belgian millionaire who arrived here ... when the country was firmly in the grip of Muldoonism? <i>(Nov 13, 1988,Sunday Star Times,A15</i>)

>  On Tuesday he likened former leader Nee Wyn's socialism as a `classic case of Muldoonism'. Those policies collapsed the country, leaving it one of the least developed countries in Asia. <i>(Jul 31, 1988,Sunday Star (Auckland),A4</i>)

>  But there they are complaining bitterly about the way the still waters of Government and the dribblings of Muldoonism are wetting their feet. <i>(Jul 24, 1970,Dominion,10d</i>)

>  Muldoonism, to its followers and its critic, meant Think Big and Intervention. <i>(Aug 5, 1992,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  Mr Quigley's sacking did much to coalesce the forces building up against "Muldoonism". <i>(Aug 6, 1992,Dominion,11</i>)

>  It was a long and strange journey for New Zealand into Muldoonism. <i>(Aug 9, 1992,Dominion Sunday Times,10</i>)

>  Such an article (Allan, 1952) of especial interest in this era of "Muldoonism" - a time when the role of the University in New Zealand and the subjects taught threin is [sic] under scrutiny - appeared in "Canta", the newspaper of the Students' Association of Canterbury University College (as it then was called), a response to a questionnaire sent to heads of departments to ascertain their views on the role of the particular subjects in the university curriculum and as a guidance to students trying to "justify their presence". <i>(May 1971,Geological Society of NZ Newsletter,30/11</i>)

>  Critics of Finance Minister Michael Cullen's moves to block the foreign takeover of Auckland Airport have accused him of Muldoonism .. At times like this, would a dash of Muldoonism be so bad? <i>(Mar 12, 2008,NZ Herald,A7</i>)

>  Used badly, it could trigger incipient Muldoonism among Labour's leaders. <i>(Jun 16, 2002,Sunday Star Times,E2</i>)

### Muldoonist
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  At last it came - a flash of neo-Muldoonist shin-kicking in defence of the economy. <i>(Oct 21, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  The Business Roundtable has attacked the Government for its big-spending "Muldoonist" policies, warning that they will harm the economy and push interest rates higher. <i>(Mar 23, 2000,Dominion,2</i>)

>  The combative Winston Peters and former prime minister Sir Robert Muldoon took it as a compliment when Mr Caygill scoffed at them as being `the Batman and Robin of failed Muldoonist policies'. <i>(Apr 15, 1989,Dominion,2</i>)
