### mullein
 
 <i>n.</i> also called Aaron's rod, flannel-leaf, tobacco plant, tobacco weed; any of several plants of dry soils and riverbeds, having a rod-like stem rising from a rosette of woolly, flannel-like leaves and ending in a shaft of yellow flowers.

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FLANNEL LEAF (<i>Verbascum thapsus</i>) is commonly called mullein in England, and is frequently so called here as well. Its commonest local name is probably tobacco plant, or native tobacco, a name for which there is no justification in either appearance or use. Velvet mullein woolly mullein, golden rod, and Aaron's rod are other appellations, but flannel leaf, one of the common sheep-station names is both descriptive and distinctive. The plant is found in dry soil and river beds ... in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,155,1967 ed.</i>)

>  <i> mullein [=] moth mullein: white mullein: woolly mullein</i> [s] <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,51</i>)

>  <b>DOWLING</b> By the dusty roads are blues and yellows and reds/ Of bugloss and furred mullein, stonecrop and centaury,/ <i>(1973,Unreturning Native</i>) [NOTES: (`By Lake Wanaka')]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>Cretan mullein</i> [s] <i>Celsia cretica</i> L. <i>Ibid.</i> 51 <i>mullein, Cretan</i> [s] <i>Celsia cretica</i> L. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,18</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> flannel leaf

>  <b>HILGENDORF</b> FLANNEL LEAF (<i>Verbascum thapsus</i>) is commonly called mullein in England, and is frequently so called here as well. Its commonest local name is probably tobacco plant, or native tobacco, a name for which there is no justification in either appearance or use. Velvet mullein woolly mullein, golden rod, and Aaron's rod are other appellations, but flannel leaf, one of the common sheep-station names is both descriptive and distinctive. The plant is found in dry soil and river beds ... in both islands. <i>(1926,Weeds of New Zealand,155,1967 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <i>mullein, white</i> [s] <i>Verbascum blattaria </i>L. f. <i>crubescens</i> Brugg. <i>Ibid.</i> 85 <i>white mullein</i> [s] <i>Verbascum blattaria</i> L.f.<i> erubescens</i> Brugg. <i>(1969,Standard Common Names for Weeds,51</i>)

### mullet boat
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Mullet boats don't have keels, only centreplates that can be raised and lowered, like dinghies. <i>(Feb 1992,Metro,90</i>)

>  <b>CARTER</b> The name of mullet-boats dates back to the very early days of New Zealand's colonization, and although there is no record for the first boat which was built in Auckland, there is sufficient evidence to say the type was evolved somewhere about the year 1880. The boats were designed for a specific purpose, the netting of mullet which abound in the shallow tidal reaches, creeks and estuaries of the Hauraki Gulf, and its environs. <i>(1944,Little Ships: NZ's Yachting Fleet,101</i>) [NOTES: Pagination from 1948 edition]

>  <b>MANDER & O'NEILL</b> Our own mullet boats raced on length, too, from twenty to twenty-eight feet, which would appear.. to have been the ideal length for boats fishing out of Auckland in an earlier age. <i>(1964,Give a Man a Boat,19</i>)

 <i>n.</i> (informal) a mullet boat q.v.

>  In earlier notes reference was made to three twenty-six foot mulleties to be built.. [at] Tauranga. <i>(Dec 1964,Sea Spray,85</i>)

>  <b>HOLMES</b> Perhaps the most popular type of mullety has been the twenty-two-footers <i>(1971,Century of Sail,153</i>)

>  In the summer I crewed on a mullety. The mulleties were over-canvassed in my cautious opinion, but a real thrill to sail... Mulleties were wide-beamed but had a shallow draft with a centreboard... A classic description of a weekend away in a mullety was: "It's like spending the weekend under the kitchen table with an 18-gallon keg." <i>(Aug 25, 2001,Evening Post,10</i>)

>  <b>GEE</b> 'He's got a yacht.' 'That's just a mullety. It wouldn't get him past Barretts Reef.' <i>(2001,Ellie and the Shadow Man,37</i>)

### mullibar
 
 <i>n.</i> psg molly-bar, mully-bar cf mulabar

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> Marbles had a language of its own. There were names for the marbles, the games, and all the various aspects of the play. There were terms for marbles such as `taw' or `shooter', referring to the marbles used to fire with; `dubs', `dates', `stakes', `dukes', or `changers', referring to the marbles put down to be fired at. Then there were the terms referring to particular kinds of marbles: for example, agates, aggies, aggotties, American alleys, blood alleys, bomb-squashers, bonies, bonsers, bottlies, bull's eyes, bum-squashers, cat's eyes, chinkies, connies, duckies, eggies, glassies, Greeks, milkies, molly-bars, mulley-bars [mullibars: large marbles], peelies, peewees, piss-wees, pretties, Scotties, Scottish alleys, smokies, stinkies, stonies, stripies, tom-bobblers, white alleys, and in more recent years ball-bearings, chippies, plastics, and woodies <i>(1972,Folkgames of Children,174</i>)

### mullock
 
 <i>v.</i> [1861 NZ Goldfields 1861 (1976) 4 Oct. 36 It is very probable that many portions of Gabriel's Gully will pay for reworking, for the place has been terribly `mullocked' by the original workers. mulloched sic in citation below?]

>  Whether the flat will pay to resluice at present, from its being so much worked and "mulloched," and also rather deep is doubtful. <i>(Jul 12, 1862,Otago Witness,8</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> mining debris

>  <b>BURGESS</b> 'No b----y fear; I should know it was a lot of mullock they are telling, for you are not like this Jew.' <i>(1866,Life of Richard Burgess,46</i>)

>  You may wash out what you wish, / Puddling mullock's knocked into a "mish". <i>(1875,NZ Songster,5/64</i>)

>  [The mine] contains a number of blocks which vary in width, and are generally short. They wedge out very quickly, passing into mullock or `formation'. <i>(1909,TrNZI,41/93</i>)

>  Mullock : Waste rock not containing minerals worth processing. <i>(Sep 1999,Ohinemuri Regional History Journal,43 : 14</i>)

>  On looking around he saw the mullock falling from the hanging-wall on deceased. <i>(Jul 16, 1900,NZ Mines Record,496</i>)

>  He.. got a job, where he shovelled mullock for many months. <i>(Aug 20, 1904,NZ Observer,5</i>)

>  The majority of the corpses wear a peaceful appearance, and were only disfigured by mud and mullock. <i>(Mar 1, 1879,Auckland Weekly News,14</i>)

>  <b>WILKINS</b> Brad, you heap of mullock, you're giving me a bad case of the mulligrabs. <i>(1990,Veteran Perils,110</i>)

>  The little hillocks all along its course contained a mullock which, when spread on the road in its puddly state, dried, and set like concrete... A gentleman in Wanganui told me that he drove over this road when it was being made.. and nearly got stuck in the mullock, when it was first spread, before it had dried. <i>(Nov 3, 1883,Auckland Weekly News,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> rubbishy food; muck

>  <b>TRAIL</b> I remember we had `bubble and squeak' for breakfast that morning. `Mullock' my father called it. I'd often heard the term used by the miners and I imagined they meant rubbish or muck when they spoke of it, but I understood that my father was only teasing when he referred to our favourite breakfast as `mullock'. <i>(1984,Child of the Arrow,51</i>)

>  <b>NEWBOLD</b> `What do you reckon about this stale bread, Greg?' ... `Fucking mullock.' <i>(1982,Big Huey, The,153</i>)

### mullock at
 
 <i>v.</i> to poke at

>  <i> mullock at, to poke v.</i> to ridicule, make fun of <i>(Sep 1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <i> mullock at, to poke v.</i> to ridicule, make fun of <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <i>mullock n. NZ & Austral.</i> rock which does not contain gold or from which gold has been extracted; <i>poke mullock at sl</i>. ridicule. [Emg. dial.] <i>(1968,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,494</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 740]]

### mullock band
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The miner took the naming of rocks into his own hands, and pronounced the softer country "sandstone," where this had not to be called "puddingstone," "mullock-bands," or hard-bars." <i>(Mar 16, 1900,NZ Mines Record,305</i>)

### mullock heap
 
 <i>n.</i> [also mullock dump]

>  <b>GRACE</b> Across the scarred, disfigured valley, over the mullock heaps... bearded men.. collect in Canvas Town's one ramshackle street. <i>(1914,Tale of a Timber Town,152</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Somewhere under the pastures must be the long-rotted sawdust heaps that littered the valley like mullock dumps, garnet red and acid yellow. <i>(1992,Fence Around the Cuckoo,224</i>)

### mullock over
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mullock over, n.</i> Shearing slang. See quotation. <i>(1898,Austral English,307</i>)

>  `I affirm as a practical shearer, that no man could shear 321 sheep in eight hours, although I will admit he might do what we shearers call `mullock over' that number; and what is more, no manager or overseer who knows his work would allow a shearer to do that number of sheep or lambs in one day.' <i>(Sep 23, 1893,Age, The,14/4</i>)

>  <i>v. (slang).</i> to shear sheep roughly and hurriedly. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>mullock over v. (Sl.)</i> shear sheep roughly and hurriedly. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### mullock rocks
 
 <i>n.</i>

It is not common in general New Zealand English, <i>but</i> mullock rocks, <i>`waste material from quartz mines', occurs in a West Coast context. </i> <i>(1966,English Language in Aust. & NZ, The,152</i>)

### mullocked
 
 <i>adj.</i> drunk

>  By nine o'clock everybody is absolutely mullocked. The combination of the alcohol, the soothing paint, the aggressive bar staff who get the adrenalin going everytime you go up to the bar and the boring conversation is enough to make any person a babbling drooling uncontrolled mess. <i>(Apr 28, 1986,Salient,5</i>)

>  Alvin's letter to Chaff (Alvin being the Massey student who wrote "firetrucks are cool"), was not as harmless as it appeared. "Your 'happy' letter writer Alvin was not referring to the big red firetrucks one sees driving round town," she wrote. "Firetrucking", male student style, is the practice.. of getting so hideously mullocked that control of the bladder is lost. A wet patch on the front and legs of the trousers ensues. This is the signal that a firetruck has occurred." <i>(Sep 2000,Off Campus,5(3) : 5</i>)

>  <i>mullocker n.</i> inexpert, careless, clumsy person. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

 <i>n.</i> [aka multiple-unit] (See [unit](und-up.md#unit))

[166] On electrified sections near Wellington multiple units, not steam trains, handle suburban passenger traffic. <i>(Oct 1951,NZ Geographic,160</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

### multiplier farm
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a satellite site from which stud stock are bred.

>  Meat from the AgResearch-developed breed, known as the GrowBulk, has been in demand since the first sheep went to multiplier farms five years ago ... <i>(Sep 20, 2002,Dominion Post,C6</i>)

>  GrowBulk 2-tooth ewes on a multiplier farm. <i>(2002,Wool Grower,Spring 4</i>)

### multy
 
 <i>adj.</i> no good, very bad

>  <b>WHITWORTH</b> I was down there with Old Ashmore's lot, doing the trampoline, and so on; but things was very quizby. Everything went crooked somehow, and the gorger had a hawful cramp in his kick. Well, ramp was the word, and vamose we did, the lot on us; some here, some there. I was reg'lar beached, and I tell you when a cove 'as to spout his smish it's about time to look out. However, I got a job at a livery stable, but although that kep' me in grub, and I slep' in a spare stall, the billet was very multy, and I kep' on the kivvy for something better. [fn] 1. [trampoline] Vaulter. 2. [quizby] Bad. 3. [crooked] Wrong. 4. [cramp in his kick] Emptiness in his pockets. 5. [ramp] Clear out. 6. [vamose] Go. 7. [beached] Hard up. 8. [smish] Shirt. 9. [multy] No good. 10. [kivvy] Look out (qui vive.) <i>(1879,Spangles and Sawdust,5</i>)

>  This was the multy show that was so roughly dealt with in `Truth' some months ago. <i>(Sep 21, 1907,NZ Truth,118/5/1</i>)

### Mum
 
 <i>n.</i> (rarely <i>mother</i>) a boarding-school nickname for a fussy, maternal, and, usually kind hearted, boy who takes an interest in smaller or younger boys [ca 1941 St Patrick's College, Silverstream]



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> the missus, the old woman, the wife, espec. in hotel bars (`No more for me; I'm going home to mum'), and sometimes used as a general name and form of address for a publican's wife, especially in country pubs (ca 1920 at least: J.Columbus; ca 1940-50, Marlborough) (These uses are not of course related to the English servant's, etc, use of mum as a vulgar variant of <i>ma'am</i> (P: 19-20c) see Barker

>  <b>BARKER</b> There ain't not a drop of yeast about, anywhere, mum.) <i>(1873,Station Amusements in NZ,124</i>)

### munga
 
 <i>n.</i> food, a meal [ca 1961 F.M. O'Brien `Heard in Oamaru and Wellington as `food'.']

>  We argued quite a lot until munga time. <i>(Oct 25, 1943,NZEF Times,III/122 : 11</i>)

>  After registering in at a pub and scooting downstairs to the restaurant for some munga, the chief character decides a telegram to "New Jork (sic)" is the next item on the agenda. <i>(Jan 31, 1944,NZEF Times,III/136 : 7</i>)

>  <b>THE SARGE</b> "Let's look for some munga."<i>Ibid.</i> Glossary <i>Munga</i>: (Kiwi- ism) `food' <i>(1950,Excuse my Feet!,47</i>)

>  F. stands for F-deck, where munga queues wait. <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,15</i>)

>  <b>DAVIN</b> [The cook] swore he'd give them some good manga to go off with. <i>Ibid.</i> 344 We'd been topping our liquior off with a bit of manga, a bloody good feed of steak and three eggs each. <i>(1947,For the Rest of Our Lives,75</i>)

>  [`Diggers out of Uniform'] `Munga,' said the Grim Dig. `Tucker. All the best moaners work on the munga. ... <i>Ibid.</i> What was your specialty? `Munga,' said the Grim Dig. `Tucker. All the best moaners work on the munga. . .' <i>(Jun 4, 1955,Upper Hutt Leader,5</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> Then he posted a picket and we sat in the stable resting up and waiting for the <i>munga</i> party to come up. ... I was in the Front Line [in Italy]. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,55</i>)

>  <i>munga n. NZ & Austral. sl.</i> food, a meal. [ult. f. F <i>manger</i> eat] <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,494</i>) [NOTES: [nf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict.; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 321]]

>  Munga .... and/or chow, - dinner-kai, as you like said the bloke <i>(1945,Victory Voyager Transport Magazines</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> [[The word was adopted during WW2, and survived a few years in NZ after, say, 1950, but is now (1984) Italian<i>mangiare</i> etc; perhaps from, or reinforced by, French <i>manger</i> (cf WW1 <i>mungey</i> `food', probably from French). It is unlikely that Maori <i>maanaga</i>, the remains of food (after a meal) influenced the Army use, except perhaps, the sense I given by Partridge, which I have not recorded from elsewhere. It may, however, helped to give the NZ use `a Maori shape', as Partridge says; but I am not aware of Maori <i>maanga</i>ing any currently in NZ English in spite of Davins spelling.]]

### munt

>  It is the friend you share crap holidays with, the one you can eat like a munt in front of, the male mate who won't yell when you hire a chick flick that you're sure he'll enjoy. <i>(Sep 30, 2001,Sunday Star Times,D3</i>)

>  Parker tells Laura's mother Dianne, who is leading dalmatian Shilo, that 'the technical term is "munted". There's mega-munt, medium-munt and minor-munt.' 'This is mega-munt.' <i>(Mar 6, 2011,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>)

>  The interviews with the wasted teens were indicative of far deeper social problems than Mum and Dad having an after-dinner munt. <i>(Nov 2, 2000,City Voice,15</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (informal) to break, ruin [cf. munted adjective]

### munted
 
 <i>adj.</i> (informal) drunk

>  It won't stop their parents taking the view that getting munted is a rite of Kiwi passage. <i>(Aug 28, 2011,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Such was the criticism that emanated from my dinner guests when I first floated the idea of contesting a civic election... One, that I was pissed, and two, that I was seriously munted. <i>(Oct 10, 2004,Sunday  Star Times: Escape,C10</i>)

>  <b>Geary</b> We were doing fine, totally munted, dancing around with roses between our teeth, winking at flowergirls. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,32</i>)

>  I tried to enjoy Placebo, but they were ruined by a wave of munted moshers. <i>(Feb 3, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53</i>)

>  Sophie hits Tyrone over the head with her backpack and Tyrone is fully munted (See 1Aa)... Georgia's brother Brooklyn is telling everyone in the train he got fully munted at the weekend... Colin, pride fully munted, returns to his seat and eats his chips. [Fn] 1AAa - Fully munted: Stuffed, history. <i>(Jul 5, 2001,Evening Post,6</i>)

>  [Hdg] Munted... Drinking alcohol inevitably makes you drunk, but does it cause you to drive like Bruce Willis? <i>(Aug 21, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),34</i>)

>  <i>Strawpeople</i>.. has given the lie to any impression that Kiwi rock was just a bunch of sweaty guys in provincial pubs grinding out beer-drinking anthems for the young and munted. <i>(May 20, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),62</i>)

>  It's like, they've tuned into some alien network because they're so munted. <i>(Sep 8, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),74</i>)



 
 <i>adj.</i> broken, damaged

>  Parker tells Laura's mother Dianne, who is leading dalmatian Shilo, that 'the technical term is "munted". There's mega-munt, medium-munt and minor-munt.' <i>(Mar 6, 2011,Sunday Star Times,A3</i>) [NOTES: word in very common use to describe damage and destruction caused by Christchurch's earthquake in February 2011]

>  [Hdg] Scary thrills in the Munted Manor... Munted Manor is aimed at 15 to 25-year-olds... The Munted Manor is a labyrinth of corridors that weaves through a series of spooky scenes. <i>(Nov 3, 2004,Dominion Post,A7</i>)

>  It is also claims the network on my street is "munted" and that this is a reflection of my less than satisfactory 28 kpbs to 32kbps [sic] dialup internet connection. <i>(Jul 16, 2005,NZ Herald,C7</i>)

>  "Our resident AA expert quickly spotted that most panels had been repainted recently, including both bumpers, of which the rear had taken a heavy knock at some stage. The jacking point on the right front side had been munted - not unusual as the recommended jacking points are easy to miss." <i>(Sep 2002,AA Autocar Quarterly Car Guide,30</i>)

>  "1990 Audi 90. Has hit a median barrier, flipped three times, landed on roof. Totally munted. Bought for parts for $250 last year. No rego, no warrant, numbers don't match. But you'd never know." <i>(Sep 2002,AA Directions,78</i>)

>  The Left must accept that, in a country where two credit agencies have downgraded us, where the sharemarket seems munted, where a recent survey showed we have some of the worst managers in the world, a corporate welfare safety net is essential. <i>(Oct 10, 2011,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

>  <b>Jane Bowron</b> Without power and water for months and sitting alone in a munted riverside house, with no decisions having been made about his home, he's had a gutsful. <i>(Apr 25, 2011,Dominion Post,B5</i>)

>  Another was proud of how having children meant he was always on top of kidspeak, like "munted" (for broken), "random" (for crazy), and "gay" (for unfashionable). <i>(Nov 26, 2005,Dominion Post,B4</i>)

### munter
 
 <i>n.</i> contemptible person; fool etc.

>  Munter: An ugly person, sometimes differentiated by gender. Also someone affected by excessive intake of external substances, ie, drunk or drugged. <i>(Jul 31, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

>  As for munters, creeps, weirdos, bitches, goons, assorted maniacs - they, too, go on to hold down steady jobs. <i>(Apr 22, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Dads, chaps, bogans, rugby-heads, brutes, munters and little tykes with velcro wallets ... They shuffled silently in droves from pavilion to pavilion. <i>(Dec 2, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),24</i>)

>  We all know those faces: their owners are variously known as hoons, bogans, munters, and wasters, the youth of today, the faces of the masses we hear so much about, good New Zealand faces. <i>(Jun 12, 1999,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Munters are hoisted aloft their mates' shoulders only to have their T-shirts ripped off in a peculiar display of homoeroticism. <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),53 ed.</i>)

>  <i>munter</i>: idiot <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  ...the usual suspects. Munters, dickweeds, tools. <i>(May 8, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  About a dozen munters were roped in to wear amazing op-shop clothes and deliver gormless dialogue in Stapp's two-part film. <i>(Jan 27, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),56</i>)

>  <b>RUD WARD</b> Munter: Most commonly male. Below average intelligence. Buffoon. Little regard for what others think of him. Liable to do stupid things at regular intervals. Finds these acts humorous ... Munter means to me: male, generally has a mullet [type of haircut] and drives around in a classic car, sometimes hangs off rafters at certain Ponsonby bars, has also been known to go jet-skiing in the early morning munted [=pissed]... Usually sells weed [<i>cannabis indica</i>, as I'm sure you'll know!]. Ex-painters are automatically classified as munters. [God knows why!!] Will have a strong preference for Fords or Holdens. [Again God knows why!] <i>(May 19, 2003,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Collected by his 26-year old son from his contemporaries]

>  We've got a sophisticated audience ... without the munters. <i>(Jul 7, 2001,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  "Not really [happy to see the race in Wellington]... it would probably attract lots of munters and petrol heads". <i>(Dec 7, 2004,Independent Herald (Wellington),p2</i>)

>  Thus we're seeing far too much of Elliot and Jen, who are already prats, and hardly anything of those still waiting to achieve complete munter status. <i>(May 13, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),70</i>)

 <i>adj.</i> big, hard, bad (and the like)?? see Google (also one hit for go munty on someone) [Gattung (below) went on to say she had actually said munted: (Agenda transcript) SIMON You see them, and I'm quoting directly here because we loved this in the office, we think they are our friendly cousins and they think we are their - and I'm quoting directly - munty next door neighbours. THERESA: That's not quite right I said munted actually. SIMON: Munted next door neighbours, sorry. For the benefit of you what's the difference between munty and munted. ### murder house
 
 <i>n.</i> the school dental clinic; more recently, the dentist; the central Dental Clinic in Wellington

>  I suppose that there are still people out there who carry fearful memories of the school dental nurse and, indeed, comments about the "murder-house" and the treadle drill are part of our national psyche. <i>(Jul 3, 2004,Listener (New Zealand Listener),9</i>)

>  A survey of Hamilton schoolchildren suggests a visit to the dental clinic is no longer considered a trip to the "murder house". <i>(Nov 6, 1993,Dominion,11</i>)

>  <b>ELDRED-GRIGG</b> "The murder house.' That's what the kids in the school playground called the dental clinic, just like kids in school playgrounds called dental clinics when I was a kid myself .. I was turning into my mother, the murderess in the murder house. <i>(1995,Mum,234</i>)

>  Plans to extend and develop five new apartments in the old "murder house" on Willis Street are currently under negotiation. <i>(May 28, 2003,Capital Times,3</i>)

>  Years ago every primary school had a 'murder house', and when the kid turned two, mum and kids went to the dental nurse. <i>(Oct 14, 1998,Dominion,11</i>)

>  [Hdg] 'Murder house' goes on the road .. The clinic - commonly known as the murder house - has treated the teeth of thousands of young Wellingtonians during its 60-year history. <i>(Oct 28, 1999,Evening Post,1</i>)

>  [Hdg] Clinic replaced by mobile 'murder house'. There will be no more visits to Wellington's old 'murder house" - from now on dental treatment for Wellington City schoolchildren will be on wheels. <i>(Apr 8, 2000,Evening Post,7</i>)

>  `Missed writing to-day. Had to go to the Murder House instead.' `The Murder House?' My hand falters to my throat. `You mean the Headmaster's Study?' `No, the Dental Clinic. See?' He bares his gum, and through the crumbs I see two bleeding holes which lately housed two obstinate baby teeth. <i>(1964,Book of New Zealand, A (J.C. Reid),240</i>) [NOTES: Marie Bullock]

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>murder house</i>: At most large schools, and for each collection of small ones, there is a small prefabricated building set slightly apart in which you can find one or more School Dental Nurses who do all of the preventive, and most of the corrective dental work (free) for the children in their schools. The children, unlike you and I?, consider a visit to the SCHOOL DENTAL CLINIC an unpleasant necessity, at best. Hence the name. <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,68</i>)

>  [heading] Murderhouse: `One kid was drilled so long she [sc the dental nurse] got through to the wooden headrest.' <i>(May 7, 1989,Dominion Sunday Times,14</i>)

>  A couple of weeks ago the new mobile dental units were launched. An operation begun 80 years ago under Mickey Savage had come to an end. The official Murder House was no more. <i>(May 4, 2000,Contact (Wgtn),7</i>)

>  The nickname "murder house" is unwarranted, says lecturer Christine Fogden. It is a ghost from the bad old days when children weren't given pain relief, even for extractions. <i>(Jan 29, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),30</i>)

>  "Appointment at the murder house" .. brought back memories, not of "murder", but of love. Of a gorgeous dental nurse. Of my hormone-ravaged young body reclining on her chair, my head at - er - chest height. You get the picture? <i>(Feb 26, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),6</i>)

>  A recent article on the sale of the Willis St Dental Clinic .. carried the misleading headline: Murder House Goes On The Market. <i>(Mar 4, 2002,Evening Post,8</i>)

>  To generations of Wellingtonians it was known as "the murder house" and last week the old Dental School building on Willis St extracted a price understood to be just over $2.5 million. <i>(Jul 23, 2002,Dominion Post,C9</i>)

>  I still flinch at the thought of visits to the "murder house". <i>(Jul 12, 2005,NZ Herald,A8</i>)

>  <b>KEITH</b> She had an appointment that day at the school's "Murder House" - the dental clinic, with its treadle drill ably wielded by the dental nurse. <i>(1991,Lovely Day Tomorrow, A,24</i>)

>  They called out your name in class when [the dental nurse] was ready to see you, and the chant went up: "The murder house! The Murder house!" <i>(Nov 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),94</i>)

>  Visiting 'the murder house' is now more affordable for students, thanks to a renewed contract between the Wellington People's Centre and M@WSA. <i>(Jul 31, 2000,Magneto,4</i>)

>  New dentistry technology is making visits to the "murder house" fewer and cheaper in the long term for some patients. <i>(Dec 17, 2001,Evening Post,9</i>)

### Murrumbidgee
 
 <i>n.</i> a gambling game

The person receiving the marble with the highest number was paid the pool less banker's percentage .... The game, or rather gamble, was called Murrumbidgee which probably indicates, that it was originated, or at least was played, in that part of Australia [ca 1928]. <i>(1939,Outside the Law,28</i>)

### muru
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Take muru. That was where if you'd offended, the victim and their whanau could take whatever it was they thought appropriate from you. <i>(Oct 1998,Mana,No. 24: 31</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> (Introd.) Altogether the year was a very troubled one, with many rummours of the death of Hongi, who had been wounded at Whangaroa, and consequent threats of <i>muru. </i> <i>(1961,Journal,20</i>) [NOTES: 1961 (ed) Rodgers Journal H. Williams 20]

>  <b>BUDDLE</b> Now there is to be no `muru', <i>i.e</i>. no plundering to obtain <i>utu</i> for insult or wrong ... <i>(1860,Maori King Movement,24</i>)

>  <b>MANING</b> There were in the old times two great institutions, which reigned with iron rod in Maori land - the <i>Tapu</i> and the <i>Muru</i>. Pakehas who knew no better called the <i>muru</i> simply `robbery', because the word <i>Muru</i>, in its common signification, means to plunder. But I speak of the regular legalized and established system of plundering as penalty for offences, which in a rough way resembled our law by which a man is obliged to pay `damages'. <i>(1863,Old New Zealand,(vii) 105</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> ... I heard that a <i>muru</i>, or robbery, had been perpetrated. <i>(1878,Forty Years in NZ,85</i>)

>  <b>KNOX</b> Another curious custom of the Maoris was the <i>muru</i>; the word means `plunder' - and some folks might call it robbery, ... <i>(1888,Boy Travellers,205</i>)

>  Death was not always the punishment that followed a violation of the law of tapu, sometimes a violation was visited by temporal punishment, in accordance with the institution of muru ... [The sick or unfortunate were] plundered of their worldly goods ... <i>(1900,Canterbury Old & New,151</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> The plantations were far apart, for fear that either an enemy's war party or a friendly plundering (<i>muru</i>) party might ruin the results ... <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,103</i>)

>  <b>TREGEAR</b> A remarkable custom was that of `plunder' (<i>muru</i>). ... a method by which an offence was expiated. It consisted of a band of persons <i>taua</i> visiting the offender and stripping him of all his movable property, ... If a man allowed one of his boy children to get hurt, the tribe would <i>muru</i> the father for the [140] loss ... since the boy probably would have been a future warrior. ... <i>Ibid.</i> 141 Perhaps `damages' rather than `plunder' is the best translation of <i>muru</i>. <i>(1904,Maori Race, The,139</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Muru - forgive, spoil, rob, bruise; <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/374</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> <i>`If you give her what she deserves, her family </i>come and <i>muru</i> you.' ... `They would come and seize everything you had, ... and leave you stripped and destitute, and all for not looking after your wife better.' ... `I have been twice <i>murued</i> by my wife's relations ...' <i>Ibid.</i> 177 He lingered on for two days and died. His father was <i>murued</i>. The <i>muru</i> party assembled at Rangi's house and awaited the signal. <i>(1948,Taina,33</i>)



 
<b>3</b> <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He lingered on for two days and died. His father was <i>murued</i>. The <i>muru</i> party assembled at Rangi's house and awaited the signal. <i>(1948,Taina,177</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `But,' I hear someone ask, `What is a `muru raid'?' <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,29</i>)

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> ... I should first sneak away such valuables as I could conveniently hide, and stolidly awaiting the muru party, console my dejection with the comfort that the fates had not forgotten me, had singled me ... out for the distinguished honour of a muru raid! <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,29</i>)

### muscle
 
 <i>n.</i> mussel

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called<i> para</i>[as-acute] <i>paraua</i>[a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus); black whale, <i>mungu nui</i> (black physeter); right whale, <i>tohora</i> (balaena mysticetus); finback, (balaena physalis); sun-fish (diodon mola); large-lipped whale (balaena mysticetus); large-lipped whale (balaena musculus); hump-back (balaena gibbosa); shark, <i>mango</i> (squalus); hammer-head (zygaena); pilots, (scomber); flying-fish (exocaetus volitans); and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

### mushy
 
 <i>adj.</i> of wool lacking character

>  <i>Mushy</i>: Wool which is lacking character. Usually a sign of low fleece weight. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,57</i>)

>  Mushy wool - wool that is irregular in staple and is from sheep in a bad season. <i>(1919,Wool in NZ,21</i>)

>  <b>MACDONALD</b> Mushy wool: shows no pronounced staple; from badly-bred or old sheep. <i>(1920,Australian & NZ Sheep Farming,142</i>)

### Musket Wars
 
 <i>n.</i> the intra-Maori wars of 1818-1835

>  In <i>Taua</i>, historian Angela Ballara has taken up the debate. but resiles from the terminology of "musket wars". Ballara concludes that musket-armed rangatira waged war no differently than they did before the musket arrived. <i>(Nov 8, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),54</i>)

>  These [influences] include the north-south frictions ("utu debit accounts") that led to the one-sided slaughter of the more southern iwis in the musket wars(1818-1835). <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,7</i>)

>  Something akin to the musket-wars broke out, with Kim Hill in full flight and Tuku Morgan in full ... well in full something. <i>(Mar 2, 2002,Listener (New Zealand Listener),65</i>)

>  <b>KING</b> ... when [the northern tribes] move out of the [Northland] area as a group, as they did during the musket wars, they are known generically as Ngapuhi. <i>(1983,Whina,18</i>)

>  <b>BELICH</b> From 1818. parts of the North Island were convulsed by increased tribal warfare. These conflicts continued in full force until about 1833, and are known as the Musket Wars. <i>(1986,NZ Wars, The,20</i>)

>  In war, the Waipapa was a natural moat along two side, the musket wars raged along it and the Ngati Paoa were massacred on its bank in the early 19th century. <i>(Feb 22, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener),32</i>)

>  In the "musket wars" of the 1810s and 1820s (which destroyed far more human lives than later wars between Maori and pakeha), Hongi descended on the Waikato, and on what is now Auckland, and slaughtered whole pas. <i>(Sep 21, 2003,NZ Catholic,16</i>)

>  It has been estimated that perhaps a quarter of the Maori race were killed during the Musket Wars. <i>(Dec 3, 2003,Capital Times,2</i>)

>  More ink has been spilled over the New Zealand Wars than blood. They still overshadow the vastly bloodier Musket Wars in our historiography, and they have even been called New Zealand's civil war buy the silly. <i>(Sep 28, 2002,Dominion Post,F9</i>)

### muso
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Smiler haven't been together all that long, but the members are individually well-known musos. <i>(Nov 1977,Rip It Up (Auckland),2</i>)

>  We are both musos from way back. <i>(Sep 8, 1999,Capital Times,4</i>)

>  Laughton, who used to live in Wellington, is also an actor, but "when you break it down I'm a muso", he laughs. <i>(Oct 14, 2005,NZ Herald,B5</i>)

>  ... another poor tortured soul whose other half shot through with a muso. <i>(Oct 1986,Metro,13</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> We all knew being a third-rate muso didn't pay that well and Cash did a bit of receiving on the side ... <i>(1988,Glory Days,141</i>)

>  [Hdg] Attention all Muso's. <i>(Apr 3, 2000,Salient,35</i>)

>  [Paul Campbell] says he and co-organiser Paul Geange, musicians themselves, started off with "muso" parties in Wairarapa years ago. <i>(Jan 6, 1993,Dominion,3</i>) [NOTES: Party organised by musicians themselves]

>  The famed muso Joe Jones is just about to launch his comeback tour in a beachside pub, with his chosen band, his sessions musos and his roadie working hard to rehearse the numbers, fix the electrics, fine-tune the lighting cues, run the sound-check. <i>(Jun 13, 2002,Dominion,31</i>)

>  Even the entertainment was mostly from local musos. <i>(Jun 1992,Cuisine,4</i>)

>  Roger Fox and his merry troup.. arrived in a nice shiny new NZR bus, looking more like tourists coming to see the hippies than musos. <i>(Mar 1980,In Touch (Wellington),7</i>)

>  Besides astounding local musos and setting the biz in a buzz, Lewis produced a creditable future single, slotted for release later this year. <i>(Sep 1979,Rip It Up (Auckland),10</i>)

### mussel
 
 <i>n.</i> an aquatic mollusc with two hinged shells, the edible, marine form of which attaches itself to rocks, etc.; in New Zealand, mainly <i>Mytilus</i> or <i>Perna</i> spp. Common salt-water kinds are a blue mussel, a green-lipped mussel (commercially farmed), and a large horse-mussel. mussel farming the cultivation of green-lipped mussels from ropes attached to rafts or buoys. See (or check) razor mussel, date mussel, horse mussel, green mussel, blue mussel, pipi 2, freshwater mussel.

>  Having lived for years in the south of France, he is at home there as anywhere, and equally at ease with a glass of Grand Cru Burgundy or a billy of mussels cooked on the beach over a driftwood fire. <i>(Jan 2001,Cuisine,133</i>)

>  Most of the others [sc. mussels] - flea mussels, mud mussels, date mussels and nesting mussels - are too small to be worthy of the pot. <i>(Feb 19, 1994,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  <b>PARKINSON</b> We ... traded with them for cloth, crayfish, and muscles. <i>(Nov 1, 1769,Journal,101</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> ~ kuku, kutai<i> Mytilus edulis aoteanus, Perna canaliculus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 206. <i>Pinna Zelandica. Gray. Yate's</i> New Zealand ... [260] The gigantic mussels, <i>Cook</i> Third Voy. ii., Polack, i. 324, are probably <i>Pinnae</i>, as they have the habit he describes. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/259</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/259-260]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> <i>Mussels, cockles</i>, and <i>clams</i> also resemble our English sorts. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,161</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> The kuku (Mytilus canaliculatus), the mussel, is also very abundant ... <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,141</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> Then follows a series of shell beds, consisting of the remains of the following species, now still inhabiting the estuary;- <i>Chione stutchburyi </i>(Cockle); <i>Huai</i> or Pipi, <i>Mesodesma chemnitzii</i>, Pipi; <i>Amphibola avellana</i> (Periwinkle) Hetikutiku, and <i>Mytilus smaragdinus</i> (Mussel) Kuku; <i>(1879,Geology of the Provinces of Canterbury & Westland,416</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kuku</i>, n. a large mussel <i>(1892,Dictionary of the NZ Language,78</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mussel. n</i>. Some Australasian species of this mollusc are <i>Mytilus latus</i>, Lamark., Victoria, Tasmania, and New Zealand; <i>M. tasmanicus</i>, Tension Woods, Tasmania; <i>M. rostratus, </i>Tasmania and Victoria; <i>M. hirsutus</i>, Lamark., Tasmania, South Australia, Victoria, New Zealand; <i>M. crassus</i>, Tenison-Woods, Tasmania. Fresh-water Mussels belong to the genus <i>Unio. </i> <i>(1898,Austral English,308</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kuku ... 6. Mytilus edulis, M. canaliculus</i>, and other species of mussel. <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,181</i>)

>  <b>THOM,AS</b> Mussels, paua, sea eggs and the whole range of there near and distant cousins were there for the taking. <i>(1970,Way Up North,54</i>)

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We got some other new fish, viz. a small Lumpfish [[ed. fn] <i>Cyclopterus pinnulatus </i>of <i>Descr. Anim.</i>, p.30], a small Blenny, both caught under the rocks at low water mark; some Starfish, Coats of mail water spouts, Centipus, Muscles & Turbines, besides in the Evening a Lamprey ... <i>(Apr 8, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/250</i>)

>  ... there are not wanting Several Sorts [of fish] that are well known; such as Mullet, Cavallas, Guard-Fish, Horse-Mackeral. Amongsyt the crustaceous Tribes we found Craw-fish, Muscles. [sic] Cockles, Scollops, Whelks, Periwinkles, ... <i>(1773,Cook's Journals,2/787</i>) [NOTES: 1773 Wales Journal in Cook Journal (1961) 2/787]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> The rocks are abundantly furnish'd with great quantitys [sic] of excellent muscles, one sort of which that is not very common measures above a foot in length. ed. fn The `great quantitys' would be <i>Mytilus edulis aoteanus</i>; the `not very common' ones <i>Perna canaliculus</i>. If the latter were `above a foot in length' they must have been giants indeed: one of 7 inches would now been reckoned large. (HWO note: Could they have been `horse mussels' rather than `green-lip mussels'? Check) <i>(Feb 1777,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1777 Feb Anderson's Journal in Cook Journals (1967) 3/808 (Queen Charlotte Sound)]

>  <b>BELL</b> We never caught any Flat Fish - and the only Shellfish besides small Musells and Limpets were Cray-fish - <i>(Nov 1791,Journal of Voyage in HMS 'Chatham',51</i>)

>  <b>SAVAGE</b> To such as are fond of cockles, muscles, and all the varieties of small shell-fish, the bay of islands [sic] must prove a most desirable place to visit; ... <i>(1807,New Zealand (various authors/titles),11</i>)

>  <b>SHEPHERD</b> He shot several ducks Red bills a wood hen and killed a hare seal. ... cockles Mussels & oisters are plentiful and excellent. <i>(1826,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1826 Shepherd Journal in 1940 Howard Rakiura 357]

>  <b>McDONNELL</b> The rivers are well stocked with fine fish ... - the very creeks swarm with them. There are abundance of lobsters, crawfish, oysters, prawns, and shrimps; besides clams, peppies, muscles, limpets, and cockles. <i>(1834,Extracts From a Journal,15,1979 ed.</i>)

>  <b>YATE</b> Those saltwater fish] most plentiful, and of greatest note, are, soles, mackarel, cod-fish, a species of salmon, whiting, snapper, mullet, bream, skate, gurnards, and a few smaller kinds, some not near so large as a sprat; with an abundance of crayfish, oysters, shrimps, prawns, muscles, and cockles. <i>(1835,New Zealand (various authors/titles),71</i>)

>  <b>WALTON</b> The mussels produced on our coasts are Lilliputians, compared with those of New Zealand, some of which expand to a foot in length. <i>(1839,Twelve Months' Residence in NZ,29</i>)

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the piscivorous tribes, are those well known in Europe under the appellations of snapper, tench, haddocks ... and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202fn</i>)

 <i>n.</i>

Survey Bulletin,No. 15 (N.S.): 110</i>) [NOTES: (Bell & Frazer-Waiki)]

 <i>n.</i> (informal) person who 'cuts the mustard'

### muster
 
 <i>n.</i> an assembly of intending participants

>  [public notice] PAROA NETBALL MUSTER... <i>(Mar 3, 2006,Whakatane Beacon,23</i>)

>  [heading] <b>College Old Boys, R.F.C. Levin MUSTER</b> <i>(Dec 14, 2005,Horowhenua-Kapiti Chronicle,11</i>)

>  WAIOHAU SOFTBALL CLUB INC AGM & MUSTER <i>(Aug 26, 2005,Whakatane Beacon,40</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a round-up of livestock, especially sheep for shearing, etc. musterer, a person who musters sheep, etc. [Latin monstrare to show] [(The word seems to have been introduced to Canterbury) Acland (1933) notes the following musters on high-country stations. -->So far as I know the usual ones are (beginning in the spring): <i>the cutting and tailing muster; shearing</i> muster; <i>straggle</i> muster (in the old days the shorn sheep were always turned not before the country .us;was straggled, <i>so that they would lead the woolies in); dipping or wearing </i>m.; <i>straggle </i>again; then <i>scratch</i> m. or <i>`lock-over' </i>to get the last of the sheep off the summer country before winter. This last is an undress sort of muster, the men catching what stragglers they can in the basins. There are always other partical musters of the flock; e.g., ewes to ram, or eye-slipping of hoggets.] (See [also tailing muster](t-ten.md#tailing-muster))

>  It knocks the sheep about too much to do other than have one principal muster for docking. <i>(Oct 16, 1912,Otago Witness,16</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The mode of mustering adopted by Ernest was this. .... He made a separate muster of each part [of the run] <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,172</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The mode of mustering adopted by Ernest was this. .... He made a separate muster of each part [of the run] <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,172</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Finlayson went to Arapawanui to try and get Atta for the muster. <i>(Jan 13, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 13 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 123]

>  2404 sheep to be accounted for, which is either a most fearful number of deaths or the worst muster ever yet made. <i>(Dec 4, 1879,Chudleigh Diary,282,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BELL</b> It was expected that a few of Morton's sheep might have got through the dividing fence since the last muster, ... <i>(1899,In the Shadow of the Bush,79</i>)

>  <i>Muster</i>.-A city correspondent asks for the names of the various musters on high country stations. So far as I know the usual ones are (beginning in spring): the cutting and tailing <i>m.</i>; shearing <i>m.</i>; straggle <i>m.</i>(in old days the shorn sheep were always turned out before the country was straggled, so that they would lead the woolies in); dipping or weaning <i>m.</i> (to wean the lambs and dip the ewes); fall (or autumn) m.; straggle again; then scratch <i>m.</i> or `look over' to get the last of the sheep off the summer country before winter. This last is an undress sort of <i>m.</i>, the men catching what stragglers they can in the basins. There are always other partial <i>ms</i>. of the flock; <i>e.g.</i>, ewes to ram or eye clipping of hoggets. <i>(Jan 27, 1934,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Gloss. additions]

>  <b>REVANS</b> I am not yet confident of the mode in which flock and stock musters will be dealt with by the natives <i>(1841,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1841 Revans to Chapman 90]

>  <b>ACLAND</b> The great difficulty in cleaning the sheep consists ... in getting a clean muster; ... <i>(1858,Notes on Sheepfarming in NZ,12</i>)

>  <b>DUPPA</b> Make a second clean muster of stragglers <i>(1860,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1860 Duppa September in 1949 Crawford Sheep and Sheepmen 47]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> As far as <i>I'm </i>concerned, I'm here,/ And camping out upon a muster./ <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,95</i>)

>  <b>WEKEY</b> ... the difficulties of shepherding here must always be great, ...; a full muster at shearing time is never obtained, as the heights ... afford on occasion a retreat to the sheep, ... The conjecture is, that he had been endeavoring [sic] to collect some of the stragglers of his flock, and ... had missed either his hold or footing ... <i>(1862,Otago As It Is,11</i>)

>  ... a full muster at shearing time is never obtained - the heights which to most animals would be a wall and a fence, afford on occasion a retreat to the sheep, ... <i>(Aug 23, 1862,Otago Witness,6</i>)

>  Made the first muster today, we yarded some 3000 sheep. <i>(Oct 12, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,106,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> It is impossible to estimate our loss until the grand muster at shearing, but we may set it down at half our flock ... <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,173,1870 ed.</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> It is customary to invite all your neighbour to your `muster' . <i>Ibid.</i> 38 It is customary to invite all the neighbours to your `muster,' as they can then pick out their missing sheep which have strayed from the adjoining runs. As they complete each flock, it is turned over to a shepherd, who would drive it off with the aid of his dogs to a beat, possibly ten or twenty miles distant ... <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,37</i>)

>  <b>CAIRD</b> My sheep met a shepherd coming down and joined in with the result of his muster. <i>(1874,Sheepfarm,20</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... to these five were added two shepherds and a stockman from one station ... come over from front runs to attend a general muster of our sheep and cattle. <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,143</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> As the sheep are slightly infected with scab we have to dip them periodically to eradicate ... the parasite; but it is a hard job because, on these fern-clad [Wairarapa] runs, it is almost impossible to get an absolutely clean muster of the sheep. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,87,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> ... How long would we spend on the actual muster? <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,54</i>)

>  <i>muster n.</i> see bangtail muster. <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>muster ... 4. Austral. & N.Z.</i> to round up (stock). <i>7. Austral. & N.Z</i>. the act of rounding up stock. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,743</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 496; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 321]]



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) [a paddock or farm property ] to afford a stock muster to take place.

>  <b>SALISBURY</b> It gives one the opportunity to muster the whole area giving a good idea how the paddocks muster, where the natural draws are and perhaps an introduction into any pitfalls which may present themselves. <i>(1994,I Was So Lucky,234</i>)

>  Some river flats in front rising to medium hill with some steep faces. Musters very readily to centrally placed facilities. <i>(Feb 1993,NZ Dairy Exporter,77</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) (intransitive use) to carry out a muster

>  Worthington ... stayed all night going to muster to morrow <i>(Apr 3, 1861,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Thompson came in the evening and we are to muster tomorrow. <i>(May 12, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,87,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Misty in the morning soon cleared off. Mustering all day. <i>(Jul 22, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,202,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Mustered down to the Coal Gully and camped there. <i>(Oct 24, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,202,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The shearing was without incident, except that I got a nasty fall off my horse when mustering, ... <i>(Sep 21, 1876,Chudleigh Diary,252,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> T.A.P. ... and men mustering, took sheep to the woolshed. <i>(Jan 2, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,91</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> To show how different the mustering is in this country, out of 70,000 sheep Campbell ... was over 15,000 short at shearing and has to muster several different times before he can get all the stragglers in. <i>(Feb 2, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 2 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 164]

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,42,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> Walker's men never mustered beyond the `Saddle.' <i>Ibid.</i> 208 The following day they mustered to the forks. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,173</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Kite in afternoon went to Tangoio to muster. <i>(Mar 19, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Mar 19 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 129]

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> C. H. S., ... and T.S. got things ready for going mustering. <i>(Jan 14, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 14 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 123]

>  <b>COTTLE</b> We're mustering and branding. <i>(1891,Frank Melton's Luck,33</i>)

>  <b>TOMLINSON</b> I have mustered on most rough country and always found four dogs sufficient. <i>(1968,Remembered Trails,40</i>)

>  <b>SWANTON</b> Previous to shearing those is the general muster, which means the rounding up and bringing in of all the sheep, good and bad, on the `run'. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,97</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gathering together of stock in one area. [see autumn -, cattle -, clean -, crutching -, delivery -, dipping -, docking -, fall -, foot -, grabbing -, marking -, ram -, sale -, secondary -, shearing -, snow -, split -, spring - , station -, straggle-, tailing -, walking -, weaning-, winter -. ]

>  <b>REVANS</b> I am not yet confident of the mode in which flock and stock musters will be dealt with by the natives. <i>(1841,Letter,90</i>)

>  <b>SHARP</b> How long would we spend on the actual muster? <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,54</i>)

>  <b>SCOTT</b> There was a fair muster of sheep in the [sale-] yards so we did not think we should have much trouble in getting a drive. <i>(1897,How I Stole 10,000 Sheep,28</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> it is impossible to estimate our loss until the grand muster at shearing. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,173</i>)

>  All musters of stock, for ear-marking, or shearing, or removal, must be notified to the occupiers of all adjoining runs. <i>(1902,Settler's Handbook of NZ, The,223</i>)



 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to gather or collect (esp. farm animals) together (usu. on large sheep and beef-cattle farms)

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> I still whistled as I mustered the cows into the yard' determined not to let ... a leaky can put me out. <i>(1924,Follow the Call,10,1975 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Assisted in mustering Ewes and Lambs into the Stock-yard <i>(1846,Journal,36</i>)

>  <b>DEAN</b> Mustered the cattle. <i>(Jul 9, 1851,Letter,293,1937 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1851 July 9 Dean's Letters (1937) 293]

>  <b>ALLOM</b> ... the only farm buildings requisite are, a strong post-and-rail stock-yard for the purposes of milking and occasionally mustering the stock. <i>(1853,Letter,118</i>) [NOTES: 1853 Allom letter in 1853 Earp NZ 118]

>  ... to muster stragglers off Isolated Hill <i>(Jul 31, 1860,St Leonard's Journal</i>)

>  Mr M, WDM & Duncan mustering Eweburn sheep. <i>(Mar 7, 1860,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  Fine Sunday. Mustering sheep <i>(Mar 19, 1865,Puketoi Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> T.A.P. ... mustering wethers run then at woolshed <i>(Jan 1, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,90</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> G.S.P. ... mustering ewes ... <i>(Jan 31, 1867,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point,93</i>)

>  <b>TINNE</b> On Titiowharu [=Titiokura], where I slept the next night, I found the overseer ... `mustering' sheep, which is the next important process to `shearing' in the squatter's annual programme. <i>(1873,Wonderland of the Antipodes,37</i>)

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> ... I had been bothered out of my life with ... attempts ... [179] to muster sheep from grim and impossible ranges ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,178</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> We have just started shearing, and I am kept [41] pretty busy mustering sheep, wool-rolling, pressing, and dipping. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,40</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The sheep had to be mustered ... in the latter part of the spring for cutting and tailing... <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,172</i>)

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> The sheep on our run in Hawkes Bay were never disturbed except at mustering-times ... [37] We generally dispensed with horses when mustering. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,36</i>)

>  <b>D'AVIGDOR</b> They are more easily `mustered' than the sheep of the Australian plains, as they are more afraid of dogs. <i>(1888,Antipodean Notes,74</i>)

>  <i>muster v.t.</i> to assemble cattle, sheep, etc.-n.-count muster n. a round-up of stock for counting. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <i>Muster</i>.- To collect the sheep together on a block of country, for shearing, drafting, or moving them. Hence <i>musterer</i>, a temporary shepherd engaged for <i>mustering</i>. The <i>musterers</i>, however, usually include the permanent shepherd and cadets as well. (C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>MEEK</b> MUSTER: To assemble the sheep for shearing or other purposes. <i>(1952,Station Days in Maoriland,110</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... muster the sheep off such country <i>(1947,Wayleggo,12</i>)

>  <i>Muster</i>: Gather or collect animals together. A clean muster leaves none behind (stragglers). A straggle muster is to collect the stragglers. <i>(1982,MAF Agricultural Glossary,5</i>)

>  On that day I happened to be mustering sheep on a station up Fairlie way. <i>(2005,Huia 6: Contemp. Maori Fiction,145</i>)

>  As part of the [geese culling] operation the station's light plane was used to `muster' the geese to enable them to be shot more easily from the helicopter and from the ground. <i>(Oct 1985,NZ Outdoor,6</i>) [NOTES: Koro Wetere letter]

>  We had breakfast by 6 and started to muster the cattle <i>(Sep 25, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,60,1950 ed.</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to gather stock together into one place.

>  <b>PHARAZYN</b> Assisted in mustering Ewes and Lambs into Stockyards. <i>(Jun 23, 1846,Journal,45</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Starting at dawn, we climbed up the Clayton side of the range, leaving men at intervals to muster along the side. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,18</i>)

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> We had breakfast by 6 and started to muster the cattle. <i>(Sep 25, 1862,Chudleigh Diary,60</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The musterer's job is to muster the sheep off such country into the respective homesteads. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,12</i>)

>  Someone said the other day, trying to get farmers all going in the same direction is like trying to muster cats. <i>(Nov 15, 2001,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Environment) a New Zealand-bred cultivar of Browntop (<i>Agrostis capillaris syn. A tenuis</i>).

>  Muster - pasture browntop cultivar suitable for low fertility and/or acid soils in summer-moist climates. <i>(1995,Grasslands Range of Forage and Conservation Plants,26</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> (Rural) to clear (an area) of livestock to gather them together in one place.

>  We mustered about four miles of the fase of Ben McLeod. I went up some 7000 ft. ... <i>(Oct 20, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,201,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>FORD</b> Mustered the spurs at the back of the hut, brought off some 3,000 sheep. <i>(Mar 1866,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1866 Mar Ford Diary in 1981 Crawford Station Years 180]

>  <b>BARKER</b> ... but it is very difficult to `muster' these ranges. <i>(1867,Station Life in NZ,122,1874 ed.</i>)

>  <b>PHILLIPS</b> Muster Mount Misery <i>(Mar 11, 1869,Journal Kept at Rockwood and the Point</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> All hands mustered `Rocky Range'. Got 62 woolly sheep. Total mustered to date 6630. <i>(Mar 24, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Mar 24 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 130]

>  Mustering Korako. One horse left in bog. <i>(Mar 28, 1891,Chudleigh Diary,374,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BLAKISTON</b> Starting at dawn, we climbed up the Clayton side of the range, leaving men at intervals to muster along the side, and the top man waited while others went through the saddle to muster the southern side. <i>(1952,My Yesteryears,18</i>) [NOTES: [f. c1869]]

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The lease was for 2500 acres but as sheep could wander at will a big stretch of country had to be mustered. <i>(1973,Big Country,12,1977 ed.</i>)

>  [We] go to Lake Heron on Monday to help Mellish muster his run. <i>(Sep 27, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,104,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAR PER</b> Sutherland. Mustering the boundary for straggler lambs. <i>(Jun 1967,Kettle on the Fuchsia, The,69</i>) [NOTES: 1866 June Tripp Day Book in 1967 Harper Kettle on Fuchsia 69]

>  <b>RIVES</b> The last station hand went up to-day to muster some of the spurs and C. and I were left to ourselves. <i>(Feb 24, 1875,Jottings on the Spot,6</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> A few days before the shearing commenced, our services were requisitioned to help muster the outlying parts of the run. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,142,1935 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>v.</i> to form? a muster, to be susceptible of mustering

>  <b>BURDON</b> The sheep that were run on the hills in the seventies were ... Merinos, and they mustered far more easily than the coarser-woolled sheep that are run today. <i>(1938,High Country,108</i>)

### muster an easy beat
 
 <i>phr.</i> (Rural) to have an easy job.

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>muster an easy beat</i> a comfy job (Jim Henderson); eg `Here's me pulling night patrols and you're pen pushing. You mustered an easy beat, mate.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

### muster board
 
 <i>n.</i> [prison context]

>  <b>Lashlie</b> One source of endless humour with the inmates was the process involved in doing muster checks in the bathrooms of the prison wings. <i>(2006,The Journey to Prison,49</i>)

>  <b>Lashlie</b> The officer I was accompanying on his duties was required to do a muster, a muster being a tour of the prison grounds during which the officer matches the faces of inmates to the inmate names listed on a muster board. <i>(2006,The Journey to Prison,46</i>)

>  3.Prisoners will be mustered using a face to name muster board prior to visitors departing the prison. <i>(2012,Internet - see Notes</i>) [NOTES: http://www.corrections.govt.nz/policy-and-legislation/ps-operations-manual/Prisoner-finance-activities/F-10.html]

### muster check
 
 [prison procedure]

>  <b>Lashlie</b> One source of endless humour with the inmates was the process involved in doing muster checks in the bathrooms of the prison wings. <i>(2006,The Journey to Prison,49</i>)

### muster down
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to hunt down stock from high country to safe or lower levels.

>  <b>DREAVER</b> We would muster down to the Catlins River, where one man would leave and ride in to help out in the shearing shed for the rest of the day, while the others had a long drag in with the mob. <i>(1994,Catlins River Wisp Run,57</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> ... in the late autumn they are mustered down on to lower levels. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,223</i>)

### muster in
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to gather and bring stock to a homestead area or woolshed.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> In the spring the sheep are mustered in for shearing and the horses carry ewes or lambs that have 'knocked up.' <i>(1977,Old Duke : the story of a hard-case horse,7</i>)

>  After a number of years, when sheep were mustered in from the back ranges, it was noticed that several would die in the yards ... <i>(1895,TrNZI,27/278</i>)

>  <b>MCKENZIE</b> Due to this [illness] and a heavy fall of snow, he was unable to muster in the remaining sheep. <i>(1951,Wakatipu Pioneers,114</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> To straggle a block of country means of course to muster in the stragglers ... <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,31</i>)

>  <b>DOMINY</b> Brothers and neighbors muster in together. <i>(2001,Calling the Station Home,183</i>)

### muster off
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to complete a muster [by bringing sheep off the hill and gathering all strings into one large mob to be left in a holding paddock or yards.]

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mustered off the sheep that had got on to Kelly's. <i>(Oct 1914,Alexander Mowat Diaries,09</i>)

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> You know where we muster off, don't you? <i>(1983,Stockman Country,27</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Generally, after we had mustered off in the afternoon, the gathering of bedding and the killing of sheep done, we played cards. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,37</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> They all started from the hut to muster off `Nob' mountain. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,208</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> Later, when we mustered off, I examined the ewe; she had only one wound.. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,17</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> This time we were pretty late mustering off ... <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,35</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We finally mustered off at the homestead about midday. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,102</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> There were six of us doing the long valley country, mustering off approximately six ridges, usually taking a ridge each. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,70</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> With everyone in position we were running through soaking snowgrass trying to muster off as soon as possible. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,57</i>)

### muster out
 
 <i>v.</i> (Rural) to muster or clear a paddock or block of stock.

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We spent three days in camp at Boundary Gully, mustering out the head of the creek. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,15</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> The following morning the majority of the gang went up over the dividing spur to muster out the Serpentine. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,70</i>)

>  <b>MOWAT</b> Mustered out Middle Hills and Seymour and I cleaned them, a few being stuck with maggots. <i>(May 1, 1909,Alexander Mowat Diaries</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> The valley was mustered out first ... <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,190</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> The boys are mustering out a holding block this morning ... <i>(1972,Sheep Thief,30</i>)

### muster up
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  <b>ANTHONY</b> ... he had mustered her [a heifer] up with the dogs that morning, and of course she was excited and unsettled. <i>(1924,Me and Gus</i>) [NOTES: c1924 Anthony Me and Gus (1977 Gus Tomlins ed. Sturm) 6]

### muster-off
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a completed muster when stock are boxed and left in a holding paddock.

>  <b>NEWTON</b> From the muster-off, we took the mob down the road and past the homestead to a holding paddock ... <i>(1971,Ten Thousand Dogs,17</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> An hour and a half later I was at the muster-off, feeling I had well and truly done a full day's work ... <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,107</i>)

### mustered
 
 <i>adj.</i> [? The next day his men returned, and the next he and they, with the mustered sheep, left for good for his front boundary.]

### musterer
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a parliamentary whip of the Green Party.

>  Metiria Turei, the Green Party's musterer ... <i>(Oct 16, 2006,NZ Herald,A10</i>)

>  He is the third-ranked Green MP.. And has been appointed party "musterer", the Green equivalent of whip .. We say that the musterer is actually more of a bringing of people together and that we will behave in a cooperative way rather than a competitive way. <i>(Jan 14, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  3 Ewen-Street, Ian (musterer) <i>(2000,NZ Parliamentary Debates,581</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> [1933 Acland a temporary shepherd engaged for mustering. The musterers, however, usually include the permanent shepherds and cadets as well.]

>  In the past week, musterers on horseback and their dogs have combed the rugged high country of the station to round up the wethers and hoggets. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/80</i>)

>  He is the third-ranked Green MP.. And has been appointed party "musterer", the Green equivalent of whip... We say that the musterer is actually more of a bringing of people together and that we will behave in a cooperative way rather than a competitive way. <i>(Jan 14, 2000,Dominion,10</i>)

>  ... George left school at 14 to fulfil his dream of becoming a high-country musterer. <i>(Mar 2005,NZ Geographic,72/25</i>)

>  All the musterers [sic] dogs have come home and Abner came home for some food. ... It always rains at mustering. . <i>(Dec 19, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,114,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>BARKER</b> Tired musterers sit down under the shadow of a great rock .... and have a ten-minutes `spell' and half a pipe. <i>(1872,Christmas Cake in Four Quarters,Ch.. I</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> ...musterers would start again... <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,174</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Musterers started this evening. <i>(Feb 11, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 11 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 126]

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> Musterers started this evening. <i>(Feb 11, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Feb 11 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 126]

>  <b>SWANTON</b> To accomplish this [muster] on large `runs', additional hands called `musterers' have to be engaged. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>BURDON</b> But still [sc. after the fencing of runs] at certain times of the year sheep had to be got in ... and at these times shepherds were required. Thus the work [of shepherds], from being permanent, became seasonal, and gangs of men would go from station to station as they were wanted, ... They were called musterers. <i>(1938,High Country,105</i>)

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>musterer</i>: shepherd <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

>  Bob was a musterer and so spent much of his working life away from home. <i>(May 1994,NZ Historic Places,47/31</i>)

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> I have instructed my new manager..to cooperate with you as to working the two stations with one staff of musterers, shearers and shed hands, whereby we can save a lot of money. <i>(1969,Molesworth,55</i>) [NOTES: Quoted from Acton-Adams c1885]

>  You might easily have become the best fly-musterer in that part of the world. <i>(Aug 31, 1942,NZEF Times,II/62 : 5</i>)

>  They [sc. musterers] can stand days of shingle sidling.. and be quizzical through it all. <i>(Apr 1945,NZ Geographer,I : 27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> It was pretty easy for a musterer, thirsting for a hair of the dog that bit him, to miss that turn-off. <i> Ibid.</i> 85 Normally these musterers are inclined to jib at milking cows but there was no objection this time - cow-spanking would be a lot easier than that fencing job. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,50</i>)

>  The musterers on this place are all of the right sort. <i>(Dec 8, 1911,Maoriland Worker,14</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a gatherer of stock in to a single location on a farm or station.

>  <b>CHUDLEIGH</b> All the musterers dogs have come home. <i>(Dec 19, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,114</i>) [NOTES: (1950)]

>  <b>SWANTON</b> To accomplish this on large 'runs', additional hands called 'musterers' have to be engaged. <i>(1892,Notes on NZ,97</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> On the average station, the mustering gang consists of the "Boss," a head shepherd - a permanent station employee - and four to six musterers. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,13</i>)

### musterer's stick
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a strong hillpole or hillstick used in high-country mustering [usually made from manuka or mingimingi] [[See introduction to Woodhouse Farm and Station Verse]]

>  <b>McCASKILL</b> A musterer's stick would then be pushed through the hamstrings and the carcase lifted. ... Apart from his dogs the musterer's most used piece of equipment was his hill stick, a six-foot piece of manuka with a ferrule fitted at the end to reduce wear. <i>(1969,Molesworth,101</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> A musterer's stick, next to his dogs and boots, is his most valuable possession. <i>(1970,Many a Glorious Morning,85</i>)

>  <b>MCRAE</b> The musterer's stick is known as a pole or hillstick or hillpole. <i>(1998,On the Run,30</i>)

### musterers' hut
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) an isolated, basic whare or shelter used by musterers. [also musterer's hut]

>  My daughter and I in a musterer's hut, the grand icy alpine night settling on the mountains around our ears. <i>(Jun 2001,NZ Geographic,52/44</i>)

>  Bush Hut, one of the 14 musterers' huts on Glenaray station. <i>(Oct 16, 1950,NZ Journal of Agriculture,346</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> That was his first night in a musterers' hut. <i>(1954,On the Hill,30</i>)

>  <b>PORTER</b> Once he had packed out to a musterers' hut .. and lay down on the bunk to rest. <i>(1970,Under the Nor'West Arch,78</i>)

>  <b>PICKERING</b> By including the old tin musterers' huts, the dog-kennel bivouacs .. accommodation of some sort has now run up to almost a thousand sites in the hills. <i>(1988,Hills, The,7</i>)

>  <b>OWEN & PERKINS</b> The storm forced the party down the Godley Glacier and valley to a musterers' hut for a better night. <i>(1958,Speaking for Ourselves,92</i>)

### mustering
 
 <i>n.</i> [1860 September Duppa (1949 Crawford 47) ... collect stragglers for a second mustering]

>  <b>MONEY</b> He was one of the owners of Tarndale, the largest cattle-station in the country, and the next day I passed the homestead, and saw a party of his hands preparing for a mustering expedition. <i>(1871,Knocking About in NZ,85</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> In mustering times, Ernest used to set some of the huge boulders on the sides of [the] cliffs rolling, and they make quite a roaring noise ... This noise startled the sheep .... which always made upwards. <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,169</i>)

>  Piripi ... made an attempt to get the mustering wages raised so I told them to go home and started without them. <i>(Dec 1, 1887,Chudleigh Diary,358,1950 ed.</i>)

>  The result ... was that at nearly every mustering these short-winded sheep used to be left behind ... <i>(1891,NZ Journal of Science,1/173</i>)

>  <b>SCRYMGEOUR</b> Long mustering days, mostly on foot, where in the winter of '86, a cart drawn by two horses, crossed the Waitaki on the ice. <i>(1960,Memories of Maoriland,8</i>)

>  We had as usual the hardest day's mustering today on the Isolated Hills ... <i>(Oct 25, 1865,Chudleigh Diary,202,1950 ed.</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> To show how different the mustering is in this country, out of 70,000 sheep Campbell ... was over 15,000 short at shearing and has to muster several different times before he can get all the stragglers in. <i>(Feb 2, 1873,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1873 Feb 2 G.M.Wilson Diary in 1973 Wierzbicka Wilson Family 164]

>  <b>KENNAWAY</b> Our run was covered with scattered sheep which it was impossible to move by weeks of mustering; ... <i>(1874,Crusts: a settler's fare due south,141</i>)

>  <b>MEREDITH</b> I have bought a very good collie, and he assists in the mustering. <i>(1875,Adventuring in Maoriland in the Seventies,87,1935 ed.</i>)

>  <b>ELWELL</b> The mode of mustering adopted by Ernest was this ... He made a separate muster of each part [of the run] <i>(1878,Boy Colonists,172</i>)

>  <b>JOLLIE</b> ... sheep mustering <i>(1879,Reminiscences,49</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> As the flocks increased, this question of getting the sheep in became a big one - they called it mustering. <i>(1951,High Country,5</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the gathering in and movement of farm stock.

>  All the musterers dogs here come home and Abner came home for some food. ... It always rains at mustering. <i>(Dec 19, 1863,Chudleigh Diary,114,1950 ed.</i>)

>  Charlie came down in evening to arrange about mustering. <i>(Jan 17, 1864,Haldon Station Diary,250</i>) [NOTES: in Vance (1965) High Endeavour]

>  <b>BATHGATE</b> Life on a sheep station is rather a dull one. There are periodical 'musterings' and other duties to be attended to ... <i>(1874,Colonial Experiences,206</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Open tussock country running up to the shingle slopes of the Old Man Range, it proved to be comparatively simple mustering. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,25</i>)

>  <b>KERR</b> Suppose I'd better tell you what mustering is, because there's going to be a fair bit of it. <i>(2000,High Times in the High Country,44</i>)

### mustering beat
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a particular area allotted to a musterer as his responsibility in a muster [usually specified as the top beat, middle beat and bottom beat.]

>  <b>GORDON</b> Like a policeman a musterer treads a beat - the better the man, the higher the beat. <i>(1983,Other Side of Farming, The,1</i>)

>  <b>HALL</b> She was on holiday with her mother and she accompanied me on a mustering beat. <i>(1987,Shepherd's Year,  A,79</i>)

### mustering billy
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a tapered tin utensil with a broad base and narrow neck.

>  <b>STACEY</b> mustering billy - a tapered tin utensil with a broad base and a narrow neck in which the tin cup fits acting as a lid. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,165</i>)

>  <b>McLEOD</b> The reward was to get your name on the silver replica of a mustering billy, the old-fashioned wide-bottomed pot with a mug which fitted inside to form a lid, and was boiled, when time permitted, hanging from the end of a mustering stick over a hastily-lit fire. <i>(1980,Tussock Ranges,203</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> We halted at the Saxon for a boil. Each filled his small mustering billy and held it over the fire on the end of the mustering stick. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,8</i>)

### mustering boots
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LANGDALE-HUNT</b> Some of the oil (black fish or bottle-nosed grampus) was still in the workshop in my time, until my brother, Reg tried it out on his mustering boots one day and was begged ... to leave them outside because they made her feel ill. <i>(1985,Last Entail Male,25</i>)

### mustering camp
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a makeshift overnight shelter for musterers, [usually consisting of tents and tussock beds.]

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> In the old mustering camps, the dog tucker was usually wild pig. <i>(1940,Te Waimate,120</i>)

>  <b>ACLAND</b> <i>(1930,Early Canterbury Runs,138</i>)

>  <b>STUDHOLME</b> Well, out on the old mustering camps, we used to say 'A horse that carries the Bell brand will carry you till he drops.' <i>(1940,Te Waimate,6</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> At an old mustering camp on the fringe of the bush we ran into one of the Departmental packmen. <i>(1952,High Country Journey,179</i>)

>  Anyone who has knocked around mustering camps will have memories of dog deals. <i>(Sep 14, 1967,NZ Farmer,19</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. little holding paddocks had been erected at the various mustering camps. <i>(1972,In the Wake of the Axe,211</i>)

### mustering country
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) land that is steep, in very large blocks, and carrying few stock to the hectare.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> Mustering country, the country of the stockman, appears to be changing so quickly that there's barely time to notice what's happening. <i>(1983,Stockman Country,152</i>)

### mustering dog
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheepdog trained to muster, hunt and head sheep.

>  .. Rose, also a black brindle, was a very useful, handy bitch destined to become one of the famed mustering dogs for that part of Canterbury. <i>(Apr 11, 1968,NZ Farmer,45</i>)

>  <i>Sheep dog</i>.- Dog to work sheep; <i>collie</i> (q.v.). <i>S.ds.</i> run naturally either to bring sheep towards you or to drive them away; and when a puppy shows his preference he is broken in for that work. The dogs that bring sheep are called <i>heading ds</i>. (q.v.) and most of these can be taught to <i>lead</i> (q.v.) if desired. Dogs which take to driving sheep away are broken in to <i>huntaway</i> and are called <i>htwys</i> (q.v.). Some dogs will both<i> hd</i>. and <i>htwy</i> and are called <i>handy ds</i>. They are not generally high-class heading dogs but very useful about hand and sometimes very useful mustering dogs. Some men have a special <i>slewing d</i>., but a well-trained, <i>hdg</i>. dog or <i>htwy</i>. will usually <i>slew</i> (q.v.). (C.) <i>(Nov 25, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  Sheep dogs are of three distinct classes - the show dog, or fancy-worker, the mustering or paddock dog, and the yard or forcing dog. <i>(Mar 1907,NZ Farmer,209</i>)

>  The utility dog is a different class of animal. He is of two kinds - the mustering dog and the yard dog. <i>(1907,NZ Farmer,210</i>)

>  This great strain is known for noise, their terrific stamina and strong running qualities as mustering dogs. <i>(Sep 28, 1961,NZ Farmer,71</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> .. a man who always keeps a first class team of mustering dogs. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,32</i>)
