>  <b>ASPINALL</b> Unfortunately I lost several good mustering dogs. <i>(1993,Farming Under Aspiring,228</i>)

### mustering gang
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a team of musterers. (See [hill gang](hea-hom.md#hill-gang); [mountain gang](mob-mys.md#mountain-gang))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> On the average station, the mustering gang consists of the 'Boss,' a head shepherd - a permanent station employee - and four to six musterers. There is also a packman who packs, on horses, the men's bed rolls and rations, and who does the cooking. <i>(1947,Wayleggo,13</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> For many years St Helens kept a man at the accommodation house, and one of his jobs was baking the bread for the two mustering gangs. <i>(1973,Big Country of the South Island,66</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> On the larger stations the mustering gang and other more or less permanent hands will number seven to ten ... <i>(1951,NZ High Country,10</i>)

>  <b>SHARPE</b> If it happened to a mustering gang on a sheep station, the owner would have a blue fit ... <i>(1966,Fiordland Muster,227</i>)

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> team: in a mustering gang, each man has a team of dogs - never, never a 'pack' of them. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,84</i>)

### mustering hut
 
 <i>n.</i> [cf. shepherd's hut] (See [also musterers' hut](mob-mys.md#musterers-hut))

>  <i>Mustering Hut.</i>- More often simply <i>hut.</i> Built out on a run for men to camp in, it is usually of one room with a fire-place, table, bench, and bunks round the sides.(C.) <i>(Nov 4, 1933,Press, The,15</i>) [NOTES: Acland Glossary]

>  <b>CRESSWELL</b> ... the next night we reached one of the mustering-huts near the head waters of the Rangitata ... <i>(1939,Present Without Leave,188</i>)

>  <b>HARPER</b> Paul Thomson was sent in from a mustering hut one day to collect more cheese ... <i>(1975,Eight Daughters,77</i>)

>  <b>PINNEY</b> The old mustering and boundary huts were of stone and the fences were rabbit-netted. <i>(1981,Early Northern Otago Runs,68</i>)

### mustering plan
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a map and/or discussion showing locations and beats of musters on large stations.

>  <b>EVANS</b> The Hunter Hills country was divided into four areas known as the first, second, third and fourth spurs. When mustering plans were considered, these names were used to guide the musterers as to which area was to be worked on. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,9</i>)

### mustering pole
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long thin stick made of manuka or other hard wood to support a musterer on the tops. (See [hillpole](hea-hom.md#hillpole))

>  <b>PASCOE</b> In the afternoon they camped, lashed blankets to their mustering poles by the side of a large rock ... <i>(1959,Great Days in NZ Exploration,89</i>)

>  Mustering stick: mustering pole. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

### mustering season
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the six months when mustering gangs are employed to work, usually from November to April.

>  <b>FERNANDEZ</b> Off they all flew, Barbara a match for the men on her eager pony, for although the station hacks were bigger, perhaps stronger, more exercised, they were also somewhat weary after the long mustering season. <i>(1973,Tussock Fever,7</i>)

>  <b>PYKE</b> Never was he happier than ... when, as at mustering [57] seasons, madly galloping over the plains, he chased the wild cattle, waking the echoes with the crack of the long stockman's whip, which none could more dexterously [sic] flourish than he. <i>(1873,Wild Will Enderby,2/iii/56-57,1889, 1974 ed.</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> Besides Ray Birdsall there are nine musterers and shepherds, the majority of them there only for the mustering season. <i>(1969,Big Country of the North Island,29</i>)

>  <b>COATES</b> I had only one mustering season there but between musters I was kept on as boundary rider and other station work. <i>(1995,Dining With the Cats,103</i>)

### mustering station
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) an outstation, used mainly by shepherds and musterers.

>  <b>KEENE</b> Te Paki was the home of Yates' son-in-law, A R Crane, who managed the sheep station, while Tapotupotu was a mustering station on the coast where there was a house erected and lived in for a time by Stannus Jones of Auckland before it was taken over by musterers. <i>(1977,To the Northward,88</i>)

### mustering stick
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a long stick, usually made of manuka [used by a musterer on steep shingle or sidlings and for crossing swift mountain rivers and jumping creeks.] (See [hillpole](hea-hom.md#hillpole),[hillstick](hea-hom.md#hillstick),[mustering pole](mob-mys.md#mustering-pole),[nibbie](../N#nibbie),[rakau](ra-red.md#rakau))

>  <b>LOUGHNAN</b> micky-mick: corruption of the Maori name mingiming (Coprosma propinqua) a native shrub which will yield excellent mustering sticks, which in some quarters are preferred even to manuka. <i>(1981,Loughnan Glossary,53</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> By using my mustering stick as a tail skid it was possible to take giant strides down moving shingle and drop down a thousand feet in a few minutes. [mustering stick] <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,71</i>)

>  <b>MCLEOD</b> Mustering sticks are usually nearly shoulder high .. manuka is the commonest wood, but Miki-miki and Hazel are sometimes used. <i>(1951,NZ High Country,24</i>)

>  <b>MACNICOL</b> Archie's mustering stick was exchanged for a shepherd's crook, and his packhorses for a tractor ... <i>(1965,Beyond the Skippers Road,158</i>)

>  <b>ROBERTSON</b> When the gang arrived for tea we had to thread a rope through the tents, tie the rope ends to a bush and use the mustering sticks to raise the tents off the ground, then crawl in on hands and knees. <i>(1986,Century at Waitangi Station, A,49</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also musterer's stick](mob-mys.md#musterers-stick))

>  <b>McLEOD</b> ... with his long mustering stick in his hand, he has a walking beat. ... Mustering sticks are usually nearly shoulder high and must be strong and tough and not too heavy. They are hard to get in some districts and a good one is carefully kept. Manuka is the commonest wood, but Miki-miki and Hazel are sometimes used. <i>(1951,High Country,24</i>)

>  <b>DUFF</b> In <i>A Musterer on Molesworth</i> Bruce Stronach says that his dogs learnt in a few days to eat and sleep tied in a circle round his mustering stick, ... <i>(Jan 27, 1954,Shepherd's Calendar,161,1961 ed.</i>)

>  <b>STRONACH</b> [each one] held it over the fire on the end of the mustering stick, which is a stout staff about six feet long. Mustering sticks are generally of manuka wood, [9] unobtainable on the station. <i>(1953,Musterer on Molesworth,8</i>)

>  <b>MASTERS</b> ... Hermit lay down on the trip and refused to move until Fred had given him several good jabs with the end of his mustering stick, ... <i>(1960,Back-Country Tales,126</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> <i>Mustering Stick</i>: When on the hill, high-country musterers carry a long walking-stick. Usually of manuka, it is about five feet long and is used for vaulting creeks etc. and as an aid to keeping balance when sidling sheep country. Also referred to as a nibby or pole. <i>(1966,Boss's Story, The,188</i>)

>  <b>BRUCE</b> To test [the ice] out he sent his mustering stick [101] down to see if it would stop in this lip or cup. <i>(1977,Life in the Hinterland of the South Island,100</i>)

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> In April 1921 Ron was taken out to help with the autumn muster. One of the musterers ... gave him his first dog ... who would hunt as well as head. Davis also gave him a stout mustering-stick and showed him how to make the best use of it when sidling across a steep face. <i>(1981,Both Sides of the River,78</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> A long mustering stick, redolent of Moses and doom, completed the apparition. <i>(1981,Exiles of Asbestos Cottage, The,92</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We soon learned to prod with our mustering sticks into suspicious-looking mounds of snow in places where sheep naturally would huddle for shelter. <i>(1965,Open Country Harvest,189</i>) [NOTES: From 1970 edition]

### mustering time
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>NESFIELD</b> The sheep on our run in Hawkes Bay were never disturbed except at mustering-times ... [37] We generally dispensed with horses when mustering. <i>(1881,Chequered Career,36</i>)

>  When mustering time came, I got the usual notice ... <i>(1889,Zealandia,1/30</i>) [NOTES: (Reeves)]

>  <i>(1953,Short Stories (various authors),186,1966 ed.</i>) [NOTES: 1953 O.E. Middleton in Stead Short Stories (1966) 186]

>  Never saw such a great pack in all the King Country, as old Charlie's. See him coming in from the back country at mustering-time with a mob ... and his dogs handling each woolly as though its fleece was worth its weight in gold ... <i>(1966,NZ Short Stories,126,1976 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (Middleton `Saving the Breed')]

### mustering yard
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a stock yard used for drafting etc.

>  T.C.K., C.H.S. and Hughie went to the back country to erect mustering yards. <i>(Jan 9, 1879,Tutira Station Diary</i>)

>  <b>KIERNAN</b> T.C.K. ... went to the back country to erect mustering-yards at the `Burnt Bush'. <i>(Jan 9, 1879,Diary</i>) [NOTES: 1879 Jan 9 T.C. Kiernan Diary in 1921 Guthrie-Smith Tutira 123]

### mustering-bag
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a hillbag or grub-bag carried by musterers.

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> .. at the same time we picked out a couple of chops and a few thick slices of bread which, together with salt, pepper, tea and sugar, we put in our leather mustering-bags, to which was attached our billy. <i>(1962,Life's Roundabout, A,26</i>)

### mustering-in
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the bringing together of stock to one large mob at a central location [usually a station or holding paddock.]

>  <b>ANDERSON</b> They are huge river flats, running for miles, so the mustering-in is a big job. <i>(1966,Over the River,84</i>)

### mustering-off
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the joining of all mobs of a muster into one large mob.

>  <b>FOSTER & WRIGHT</b> When we come to mustering off there, if we're there before the helicopter ... <i>(1983,Stockman Country,27</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> On mustering-off on the second day, two or three men take the mob on ... <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,35</i>)

### mute
 
 <i>n.</i> [whalers']

>  Conveyed on the loft - 16 lances, 23 irons, ... 3 boat-spades, and two mutes, ... in addition ... [138] 2 boat-spades, and 4 whifts, ... 2 steer-oars, ... <i>(Jul 5, 1842,Piraki Log,137</i>)

### Mutley
 
 <i>n.</i> police sniffer dog

>  Cartoon characters.. appear, as well as <i>Mutley</i> (aviator 'baddie' Dick Dastardly's green dog in the 1960s Hanna Barbera cartoon <i>Stop the Pigeon</i>) for a police sniffer dog which searches the prison for drugs. <i>(Oct 1999,NZWords,2(2)  2</i>)

### mutta
 
 <i>n.</i> whitebait

>  "In October and November commences the fishing season here - the mutta (sic) or whitebait, entering the rivers and the tide in great quantities."... Renowned West Coast explorer Thomas Brunner made these observations in the 1840s. <i>(Feb 2003,NZ Memories,No. 40/24</i>)

### mutton
 
 <i>n.</i> a sheep

>  The favourite morsel of the unhappy mutton which attracts the kea most strongly is the fat on the kidneys, and having located the spot on the living animal, he deliberately cuts his way to it. <i>(Jan 23, 1890,Otago Daily Times,43</i>)

>  <b>DARE</b> ... it was not long before I was promoted to killing three muttons each week. <i>(1956,Rouseabout Jane,171</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep home-killed or to be home-killed for consumption on a farm property. (See [killer](kee-kiw.md#killer))

>  <b>NEWTON</b> We wanted a mutton one night, and against my better judgment, I took Sam out to run in the killers. <i>(1964,Straggle Muster,74</i>)

>  <b>NEWTON</b> After cutting off a leg for the evening meal, he tied the mutton sheep in a sack and hauled it high among the branches of a birch tree. <i>(1949,High Country Days,43</i>)

>  <b>STACEY</b> When killing a mutton the nibbie is put through the hind legs so two men can support it while the third man finishes the dressing. <i>(1996,Tales of a Tussock Jumper,165</i>)

### mutton bag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <i>mutton n.</i>-mutt'on-bag n. cloth used to protect frozen mutton.-mutt'on-bird n. a migratory sea-bird of the Bass Strait.-mutt'on tree' n. a particular tree in which mutt'on-birds collect.- mutt'on-wood n. a shrub found in coastal areas visited by mutt'on-birds. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

### mutton board
 
 <i>n.</i> ? = mutton-chain

>  The dispute began after the union had applied restrictions to the work of members in the lamb cutting room and labourers on the mutton board in an attempt to improve pay and conditions. <i>(Mar 11, 1981,Evening Post,4/4</i>)

### mutton chain
 
 <i>n.</i> a freezing works processing chain devoted to sheepmeat. (See [chain](c-chi.md#chain))

>  <b>LIND</b> Viscera conveyors were being installed on all mutton chains. <i>(1985,Cut Above, A,151</i>)

>  Mutton chains throughout the country were idle yesterday over a dispute concerning the rate to be paid for a new job on the chain. <i>(Oct 17, 1972,Press, The,1/1</i>)

>  Across the road at Hellaby's they have a beef chain killing up to 850 a day, two mutton chains killing 3,200 each, and a workforce of 1100. <i>(Jul 1986,Metro,76</i>) [NOTES: [mutton chain; beef chain]]

>  <b>Ihimaera ed.</b> He'd spent most of the morning on the broom, watching the seasoned hands on the mutton chain and dreaming of the day when he too would take his place there. <i>(2007,Get on the Waka,102</i>) [NOTES: (Phil Kawana)]

### mutton class
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>BOWEN</b> In this country, Merinos, Corriedales and halfbreds are classed as the fine-wool sheep, and all the other breeds in the long-strong wool or mutton class. <i>(1955,Wool Away!,3</i>)

### mutton dagger
 
 <i>n.</i> penis [ca 1973, 1977-78 Peter Janssen]

>  <b>K ARVIDSON</b> common in Horotiu Freezing Works, with `pork sword' nf P: though mutton has a strong sexual connotation in many entries. <i>(1960,Personal Communication</i>)

### mutton flap
 
 <i>n.</i> the belly flesh of a side of mutton (often shortened to flap); also in transferred uses (see cits) [1920s at least]

>  <b>SARGESON</b> mutton flap: a cheap cut of meat. <i>(1964,Collected Stories,301</i>)

>  <b>HILLIARD</b> He slapped the roll of flesh around her belly. `You've got a fair sort of mutton-flap there, old girl.' <i>(1960,Maori Girl,235</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>muttonflaps</i> labia majora. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

>  <b>Eldred-Grigg</b> Shut my eyes - I still see it! And smell it! Coal smoke, and fags, and mutton flap boiling on the stove. <i>(1995,Mum,38</i>)

### mutton grower
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a breeder or grazier of sheep for meat [rather than wool]

>  Mr Donald Fraser, the well-known Rangitikei farmer, has supplied the following to one of the district papers, and the subject being one of much importance to muttongrowers, we subjoin his remarks ... [muttongrower.] <i>(Mar 1895,NZ Farmer,92</i>)

### mutton gun
 
 <i>n.</i> penis [ca1986 Nov 2 p.c. Dave Rankin, Auckland A freezing works term for penis.] (See [cf. mutton dagger](mob-mys.md#mutton-dagger))

>  <b>GORDON</b> <i>Mutton gun</i>: `tool'. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: Feilding Freezing Works terms]

### mutton lord
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep-holder with large numbers of sheep.

>  <b>CHAMIER</b> Our 'mutton lords' as we call them, prosper and get fat ... <i>(1891,Philosopher Dick,42</i>)

>  <b>WICKHAM</b> The mutton lords live on roast duck and green peas. <i>(1891,Casual Ramblings,59</i>)

### mutton puncher
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd or musterer.

>  Musterer: mutton puncher. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)

>  The mutton-puncher sniffed the nor'west. <i>(Nov 22, 2002,Dominion,C6</i>)

### mutton shell
 
 <i>n.</i> paua

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Muttonbird, sooty shearwater .. .. oi, titi .. .. <i>Puffinus griseus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Muttonbird, sooty shearwater .. .. oi, titi .. .. <i>Puffinus griseus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Muttonbird, sooty shearwater .. .. oi, titi .. .. <i>Puffinus griseus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/705-712</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Outside were stacks of preserved mutton birds, the method of preserving is simple, ... <i> Ibid.</i> 347 We were visited ... by Tyeroa ... he has been together with a few of his tribe on the east side of Stewarts island for the purpose of procuring sea and mutton birds for the winter, ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,345</i>) [NOTES: 1823 June 1 Capt.J.R.Kent <i>Mermaid </i>log in 1940 Howard Rakiura 345]

>  <b>THOMSON</b> ... the finest shells [include] ... the .us;Haliotis iris mutton shell ... <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/31</i>)

### mutton staggers
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GORDON</b> <i>Mutton staggers: to have mutton staggers:</i> really buggered by work so that [one] can hardly work; the height of exhaustion. <i>(May 17, 1951,Personal Communication</i>) [NOTES: ca1951 May 17 Feilding Freezing Works terms p.c. Colin Gordon]

### mutton stew blues
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) [joc.] a condition caused by a restricted diet of mutton stew.

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> There was no chance of the shearers and officials catching 'mutton stew blues' with Connie in charge of the dining room! <i>(1993,Gone West for Shearing,13</i>)

### mutton track
 
 <i>n.</i> a path from one sheep station to another

>  <b>AGNEW</b> For several months I explored the North Island, walking the mutton track of the sheep stations and never once being refused a shakedown. <i>Ibid.</i> 78 His [the swagger's] name was Hank and he wanted to know which were the best mutton tracks to follow, which station owners were the [79] most generous and who had the best cooks. <i>(1974,Loner, The,69</i>)

### mutton tree
 
 <i>n.</i> [?Australian or error]

>  <i>mutton n.</i> ... -mutt'on-bird n. a migratory sea-bird of the Bass Strait.-mutt'on tree' n. a particular tree in which mutt'on-birds collect.- mutt'on-wood n. a shrub found in coastal areas visited by mutt'on-birds. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

### mutton-bill fence
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a fence built for a farmer in return for meat, usually to pay off a debt.

>  <b>CHANDLER</b> Herbie von Tunzelmann would point out various sections of fencing as 'one of Dunery's mutton bill fences' where erection had been done to square a monkey. <i>(1996,Land of the Mountain and the Flood,79</i>)

### mutton-boil
 
 <i>n.</i> a boil or sore attributed to handling raw mutton [ca 1949 Havelock ca 1951 Foxton Freezing Works] (See [cf mutton staggers](mob-mys.md#mutton-staggers))

### mutton-flapper
 
 <i>n.</i> a farmer who depends on SMPs, urban 1984-1985

### mutton-growing
 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) sheepfarming for meat production.

>  Very little cropping was done at this time, grazing, wool and mutton-growing being relied on. <i>(1910,Tapanui Station,20</i>)

### muttonbird
 
 <i>v.</i>

>  His family had a permit to muttonbird on Kundy Island; different families had rights to different islands. <i>(May 21, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> A taua (grandmother) narrated:- `I have been muttonbirding on Horo-mamae (Owen Island) ... I have also gone muttonbirding on Potoma (Evening Island) ... <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,84</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> any of various seabirds whose young are taken for food (considered a delicacy because of their distinctive fishy flavour), especially the sooty shearwater or titi, Puffinus griseus, and in the North Island the grey-faced petrel or oi, Pterodroma macroptera gouldi.

>  To say muttonbirds are common here fails to convey their overwhelming abundance on the islands... In the morning they gather at various launching points around the coast, forming muttonbird gridlock as they await their turn to take off. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/65</i>)

>  Named Titiraupenga because it was once a popular nesting place for sooty shearwater (titi, muttonbird) ... <i>(Jul 2005,Conservation Action,p 32</i>)

>  <b>620</b> <i>mutton-bird n. </i>migratory bird, species of shearwater or petrel, frequenting Pacific Ocean, New Zealand and especially Bass strait, valued for flesh and oil. In New Zealand called titi. <i>(1996,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's)</i>)

>  <i>mutton-bird n.</i> migratory bird, species of shearwater or petrel, frequenting Pacific Ocean, N.Z. and especially Bass strait, valued for flesh and oil. In N.Z. <i>titi. </i> <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),13</i>)

>  <i>Hutton's Shearwater Puffinus huttoni</i> MATHEWS, 1912 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi, pakaha, muttonbird. </i>... Hutton's shearwater breeds only on the South Island. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,99</i>)

>  <b>BULLER</b> `This Petrel, called by Norfolk-Islanders `Mutton-bird' or `Ghost-bird', from its child-like cry at night, ...' <i>(1888,Birds of New Zealand,2/233</i>) [NOTES: 1885 Ibis 268 in 1888 Buller Birds of NZ 2/233]

>  <i>Black Petrel Procellaria parkinsoni</i> GRAY, 1862 ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Taiko, Parkinson's petrel. </i>... This was the largest type of muttonbird available to North Island Maoris, ... <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,90</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> We moved out swarming like muttonbirds in the great mechanised migration from Baggush in early November, 1941 ... <i>(1978,Soldier Country,36</i>)

>  KERMADEC MUTTON-BIRD (<i>Oestrelata mollis</i>, Gould). <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/225</i>)

>  <i>Kermadec Petrel Pterodroma neglecta neglecta </i> SCHLEGEL, 1863) ... At the beginning of this century, more than 200 000 Kermadec petrels bred on Raoul Island. Many of their young were harvested as muttonbirds and in the first three weeks of April 1908, over 2000 were slaughtered. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,79</i>)

>  <i>Sooty Shearwater Puffinus griseus </i>(GMELIN, 1789) ... [97] OTHER NAMES: <i>Titi, New Zealand muttonbird</i>. ... Of all the various petrels serving as `muttonbirds' in New Zealand this is the most important. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,96</i>)

>  (Alderman Islands) GREY-FACED PETREL OR NORTH ISLAND MUTTON-BIRD <i>(Pterodroma macroptera</i> Smith). <i>(1928,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,9/284</i>)

>  <b>OTAGONIAN</b> Potatoes, lampreys and the rancid mutton-bird proved to be the fare provided for our supper. <i>(1867,Rambles with a Philosopher,91</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The chortling and grunting Mutton birds jealously guarding their nests.<i>Ibid.</i> 215 Off the south-west coast of Stewart Island the Moutere Titi, or Mutton Bird Isles, form the chief breeding place of the Petrels. <i>Ibid.</i> 217 The birds go forth, to return in six month's time to the very hole that sheltered each, where a white egg was laid a year ago on the far headlands of the Mutton Bird isles. <i>(1938,Children of Tane,39</i>)

>  A mutton-bird petrel sat close to the stern of the launch. <i>(Apr 27, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

>  Ani lays back...closes her eyes and takes to dreaming. In the pictures of the past, she sees her grandfather, her poua, hunting muttonbirds on Kahu Ariki. <i>(2007,Huia 7: Contemp. Maori Fiction,77</i>)

>  <i>Grey-faced Petrel Pterodroma macroptera gouldi </i>(HUTTON, 1869) ... OTHER NAMES: <i>Oi, muttonbird</i>. ... Around the northern part of the North Island, the grey-faced petrel is the most common breeding petrel. [A North Island bird] <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,72</i>)

>  <i>Oestrelata mollis,</i> Gould. ... This one was obtained on Sunday Island ... [in] the Kermadec Group ... It was known to the settlers there as the `mutton-bird,' ... <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/41</i>)

>  [Tasmania] On Preservation Island [Bass Strait], are several boats crews who go there to get a supply of mutton birds, which is a main article of their food. These birds, so called from the resemblance in flavour to mutton, are migratory. <i>(1824,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1824 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 61]

>  mutton bird - tee' tee' [titi] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 113]

>  Between the island and the Neck are 11 or 12 small islands abounding in Muttonbirds, ... ] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,60,1986 ed.</i>) [NOTES: c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986) 60]

>  On the East side of Ruaboka, is a small bushy Island where are a number of Mutton-Birds, but the natives advised us not to take any of them .... <i>(1827,Boultbee Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1827 Boultbee Journal in 1979 Begg 180]

>  <b>RHODES</b> The six sailors and the settler [on Sunday Island] had entirely subsisted on an aquatic bird they called a mutton bird, and cabbage tree. <i>(1836,Whaling Journal,35,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Its [the Mangorake's, Taranaki] banks are steep, and from one of them Tangutu dug out a titi: this bird, a Procellaria, or mutton-bird as it is commonly called, has many peculiarities. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/148</i>)

>  <b>SELWYN</b> Eels were still the principal food of the people; but they had imported considerble numbers of <i>titi,</i> or mutton-bird, from the rocks about Stewart's Island. <i>(Jan 10, 1844,Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 10 Selwyn Journal in 1851 NZ Part III (Church in the Colonies VIII) 10]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> There is also a sort of <i>Puffin</i> or <i>pelicanoides</i>, commonly called the <i>mutton-bird</i>, which the natives take in large quantities by lighting fires on the seaward face of the hills near the coast, into which these birds, called <i>titi</i> by the natives, fly, and are killed. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,165</i>)

>  The cottages in Stewart's Island are all well garnished with a luxury of the southward - the mutton bird. This I take to be the bird which is sometimes called the sheerwater, or the sooty petrel - a black bird about the size of a duck, with long wings, which all of us have seen on our voyage out skimming over the waves. In the month of December these birds resort to the cky islands about Foveaux's Straits in great numbers, for the purpose of breeding. <i>(Jul 20, 1844,Nelson Examiner</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Monro Notes of a Journey (Nelson <i>Examiner</i> July 20 et seq.) in 1898 Hocken Contributions 254]

>  <b>FALLA et al.</b> SOOTY SHEARWATER <i>Puffinus griseus Other names:</i> Muttonbird, Titi. <i>(1966,Birds of New Zealand,42</i>)

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> MUTTONBIRD. Also known as the sooty shearwater or titi, the New Zealand muttonbird is <i>Puffinus griseus</i> and belongs to the order of sea birds known as petrels. As a name, `muttonbird' appears to have originated among early European settlers in Australasia and is said to refer to the taste of the flesh. At least as probable is the theory that the name refers to the rather woolly appearance of the downy young. In New Zealand other petrels are also given the name `muttonbird'. In fact, the sooty shearwater, which is most abundant on the islands about Foveaux Strait, might well be called the <i>southern</i> muttonbird, and the grey-faced petrel, predominant on the islands off the coast of Auckland Province,the <i>northern</i> muttonbird. In Victoria, Tasmania, Bass Strait, and South Australia, the species is the short-tailed shearwater - an occasional visitor to New Zealand seas. <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,2/616</i>)

>  <b>LELAND</b> <i>muttonbird</i>: The SOOTY SHEARWATER or puffinus grisens [sic ?=<i>Puffinus griseus</i>] is a petrel (type of seagull). <i>(1980,Personal Kiwi-Yankee Dictionary, A,68</i>)

>  <b>BURTON</b> On the southern coasts and in the Bay of Plenty, the greatest delicacy was the muttonbird or sooty shearwater, a type of seabird known to the Maori as<i> titi. </i> <i>(1982,Two Hundred Years of NZ Food & Cookery,9</i>)

>  <i>mutton bird n.</i> any of several shearwaters, having a dark plumage with greyish underparts. In New Zealand, applied to one collected for food, esp. by Maoris. <i>(1982,Collins Concise English Dictionary,744</i>) [NOTES: [1986 NZPOD 496-497; 1985 Collins NZ Compact Dict. 322]]

>  <b>SHORTLAND</b> The `titi' or mutton bird, as it is termed by the whalers, is, I believe, a species of puffin. <i>Ibid.</i> 314 TITI, a sort of puffin, called by the whalers mutton-bird. (Vide. 225.) <i>(1851,Southern Districts of NZ,225</i>) [NOTES: 1844 Jan 18 in 1851 Shortland Southern Districts of NZ 225]

>  <b>WAKEFIELD</b> There is also a sort of Puffin .... commonly called the mutton-bird ... called titi by the natives. <i>(1848,Handbook for New Zealand, The,165</i>)

>  Fed on mutton birds, biscuit and potatoes. <i>(Mar 3, 1849,Torlesse Papers,64,1958 ed.</i>)

>  Fortunately there were 2 boats ... which belonged to Terewiti (Cloudy Bay) natives who had been catching mutton birds at Roebuki. [Ruapuhe Island at the eastern entrance to Foveaux Strait]. <i>(Mar 1, 1849,Torlesse Papers,63,1958 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HURSTHOUSE</b> The young Titi (mutton-bird), a species of puffin, is caught by the Southern natives in great quantities ... <i>(1857,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/121</i>)

>  <b>THOMSON</b> Six species of the <i>Procellaridae</i> are found on the sea-coasts. The Titi, or mutton bird, and the Toroa, or albatross, are the most celebrated birds of this family. <i>(1859,Story of New Zealand, The,1/28</i>)

>  <b>BUTLER</b> The great feature of the southern seas is the multitude of birds ... Huge albatrosses, molimorks (a smaller albatross), Cape hens, Cape pigeons, parsons, boobies, whale birds, mutton birds and many more, wheel continually about the ship's stern, ... <i>(1863,First Year in Canterbury Settlement,I/12</i>)

>  Another great and real delicacy came to us about this time - viz., the "mutton-birds". <i>(Oct 9, 1880,Observer (Auckland),Suppl. 2</i>)

>  The link is, of course, as intimate as that between a preserved mutton-bird and a thistle, between a dram of scotch and a greenstone mere. <i>(Mar 2, 2003,Sunday Star Times,C8</i>)

>  <b>HYDE</b> Sometimes she would insist on cooking mutton-birds, from whose enormous breasts spouted streams of oil. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,74</i>) [NOTES: 1989 EDITION]

>  <b>HYDE</b> Sometimes she would insist on cooking mutton-birds, from whose enormous breasts spouted streams of oil. <i>(1937,Wednesday's Children,74</i>) [NOTES: 1989 edition]

>  Many people make the mistake.. of boiling mutton-birds, perhaps for hours, before they roast them. <i>(Oct 19, 1938,Auckland Weekly News,5</i>)

>  "What, no muttonbird!" exclaimed a grinning member of the Maori Battalion when inspecting a German mess tin which had just arrived with the compliments of a German cook. <i>(Apr 23, 1945,NZEF Times,IV/200 : 3</i>)

>  <b>PARK</b> Excited, forgetting everything, she sat down at the table, looking with eager appetite at the mutton-birds, rich and greasy, lying upon the plates of steaming potatoes, and then shyly at the boys who elbowed and chiacked their way to the table. <i>(1951,Witch's Thorn, The,67</i>)

>  <b>TORLESSE</b> Paid 5/- for 24 lbs mutton birds. <i>(1958,Torlesse Papers,63</i>) [NOTES: 1849 citation]

>  The men would come down from the timber settlements on Friday evening, eat supper with Mum and the kids and repair to The Pig;.. sustained by beer hauled in from Taihape and nourished by greasy mutton birds. <i>(Oct 1999,North & South,130</i>)

>  If the new Muttonbirds single hasn't done well overseas.. NZ commercial radio will not exercise any initiative and make it a hit themselves. <i>(May 25, 1992,Salient,12</i>)

>  Miss Bell carried a stack of 133 mutton birds.. for nearly 2 miles without stopping. <i>(Jun 7, 1890,NZ Observer,6</i>)

>  The Mutton Birds are really taking off - the recently hatched Kiwi pop band has only been round a year, and already they have rave reviews. <i>(Apr 23, 1992,Evening Post,18</i>)

>  The Muttonbirds release their self-titled debut album this week. <i>(Aug 17, 1992,Salient,24</i>)

>  Willie had a muttonbird, Its coat was soft and greasy. His father caught it off its guard, Which isn't very easy. <i>(1930,NZ Railways Magazine,5(1) ; 12</i>)

>  As we hear the short-tailed puffin at night flying overhead, I count it also as one of our Tutira birds. It is called by the bushmen the `mutton-bird,' but can be only ... <i>Puffinus caudus.</i> <i>(1895,TrNZI,28/375</i>)

>  <b>N T CLARKE</b> ... I have not yet been able to make much progress in negotiating, on behalf of the Government, with the Natives for the purchase of Stewart's Island, on account of the absence of many of the Natives interested, who are engaged catching titi (mutton birds), nor shall I be able to do much till they return. <i>(Mar 30, 1864,Letter</i>) [NOTES: 1864 Mar 30 N.T.Clarke in 1979 Wilson Titi Heritage 136]

>  <b>HUTTON</b> 127. PUFFINUS TRISTIS. Forst. Mutton Bird. Oii. <i>Nectris amaurosoma.</i> COUES <i>(1871,Birds of New Zealand,45</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mutton-bird, n.</i> The word is ordinarily applied to the Antarctic Petrel, <i>Aestrelata lessoni.</i> In Australasia it is applied to the Puffin or Short-tailed Petrel, <i>Puffinus brevicaudus,</i> Brandt. The collection of the eggs of this petrel, the preparation of oil from it, the salting of its flesh for food, form the principal means of subsistence of the inhabitants, halfcaste and other, of the islands in Bass Straits. [1839 Aust] <i>(1898,Austral English,309</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> `The titi, or mutton-bird, is a seabird which goes inland at night just as the light wanes. The natives light a bright fire, behind which they sit, each armed with a long stick. The titis, attracted by the light, fly by in great number, and are knocked down as quickly as possible; thus in one night several hundreds are often killed, which they preserve in their own fat for future use.' <i>(1855,Te Ika a Maui,382</i>)

>  <b>PASCOE</b> ... Mutton Birds ... <i>(1957,Mr Explorer Douglas,289</i>) [NOTES: c1899 Pascoe Mr Explorer Douglas (1957) 289 (Birds of South Westland)]

>  <b>BAUCKE</b> <i>(1905,Where the White Man Treads,202</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> We also got four kinds of Petrel in their burrows,- the Mutton Bird, Parara, Titi Wainui, and the Kuaka. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,16</i>)

>  ... the most important [Chatham Island] edible sea-birds: AS WRITTEN AS PRONOUNCED ENGLISH hopo ho[macron]p' albatross hakoakoa ha[macron]ko[macron]k' mutton bird taiko tchai[macron]k' pied shear water <i>(1928,BP Bishop Memoirs,Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382 (358)</i>) [NOTES: 1928 Baucke Manuscript in Skinner and Baucke The Morioris (Bishop Museum Memoirs Vol. IX No. 5 pp. 343-382) 358]

>  <b>BLACKMORE</b> Mutton birds may be cooked by boiling, grilling, or roasting ... <i>(1928,Cooking  of NZ Fish,97</i>)

>  The tuatara lives in the same burrows as are used by the oii, the titi and the toanui. All three of the petrels are included in the term mutton-birds. <i>(Sep 11, 1922,Quick March,15</i>)

>  <b>GREY</b> The boatmen called a shearwater, the kind I have seen all over the Pacific, a mutton bird. <i>(1926,Angler's Eldorado,35</i>)

>  <i>mutton n</i>. ... -mutt'on-bird n. a migratory sea-bird of the Bass Strait.-mutt'on tree' n. a particular tree in which mutt'on-birds collect.- mutt'on-wood n. a shrub found in coastal areas visited by mutt'on-birds. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>BASHAM</b> <i>MUTTON BIRDS</i> Mutton birds are rich in oil, containing vitamins, iodine and bone making material ... high medicinal value. The flesh is tender, and, when cooked, resembles the flesh of wild duck, the flavour being more akin to ... fish. <i>(1939,Aunt Daisy's Cookery Book,20</i>)

>  <b>POWELL</b> MUTTON BIRD (<i>Puffinus griseus</i>), Titi or Oi ... was and still is in the South an important item in the diet of the Maori people. <i>(1947,Native Animals of NZ,79</i>)

>  <b>RICHARDS</b> Muttonbirds were so called from their fancied flavour of mutton. The first known mention of the name is in a letter from an officer stationed on the newly-founded convict station on Norfolk Island. He writes in 1793 that the island swarms with muttonbirds so called by the convicts or sometimes bird sheep. <i>(1952,Chatham Islands,84</i>)

>  Mutton-bird An edible sea bird of Bass Strait and New Zealand. The Maori name is `titi'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),269</i>)

>  <i>muttonbird n</i> : any of several Australasian sea birds often used (as by the Maori) for their meat, oil, and feathers: as<i> a</i> (1): a short-tailed shearwater<i> (Puffinus tenuirostris)</i> of Australia and New Zealand (2) : a sooty shear-water <i>(Puffinus griseus) </i>of New Zealand<i> b</i> : any of several petrels (as <i>Pterodroma macroptera, P. lessoni,</i> and <i>P. neglecta </i> <i>(1961,Merriam-Webster Third International,1493</i>)

### muttonbird hole
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Our progress was painfully slow, the entire surface being literally honey-combed [177] with mutton-bird holes ... <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/176</i>)

### muttonbird island
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also Titi Islands](ten-tot.md#titi-islands))

>  The first big drafts of mutton-birds from the Mutton-bird Islands have reached the market. <i>(May 4, 1939,Taranaki Herald,6</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> On some of the muttonbird islands old methods prevailed down into my time. <i>(1944,Maori Place-Names of Otago,84</i>)

>  <b>WODZICKI</b> Goats have also been placed...on small mutton bird islands in order to destroy punui (<i>Stilbocarpa lyallii</i>) and thus improve the islands as mutton bird grounds. (R. Trail in <i>litt.</i>, 1947) <i>(1950,Introduced Mammals of NZ,155</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... may I help you to enjoy the rare privilege of a visit to a mutton-bird Island? <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,38</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> The strange birdcall [hakuwai] seems to have been limited to only some of the muttonbird islands in the South Cape area. These were Poutaima, Big Island, Solomons and the Green Island, though a similar strange bird, called by whalers the Breaksea Devil, was said to be heard on Breaksea Island. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,157</i>)

>  <b>JENKIN</b> The strange birdcall [hakuwai] seems to have been limited to only some of the muttonbird islands in the South Cape area. These were Poutaima, Big Island, Solomons and the Green Island, though a similar strange bird, called by whalers the Breaksea Devil, was said to be heard on Breaksea Island. <i>(1970,NZ Mysteries,157</i>)

>  <b>MOORE & IRWIN</b> <i>Stilbocarpa lyallii</i>, the punui of Stewart Island, has less hairy leaves and reddish-purple flowers. On the mutton-bird islands where there is low <i>Olearia</i> forest, these plants can form a dense undergrowth ... <i>(1978,Oxford Book of NZ Plants,96</i>)

>  <b>SANSOM</b> Herekopare, the nearest muttonbird island to Halfmoon Bay ... <i>(1982,In the Grip of an Island,154</i>)

### muttonbird leaf
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>GLEN</b> Biddy came back from Stewart Island with a mutton bird leaf tree ... <i>(1980,Bush in Our Yard,150</i>)

### muttonbird oil
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>LANCASTER</b> Split fish drying everywhere in the sun, ... rancid odours of whale-oil, mutton-bird-oil, berry-oil, smells of pungent fern-root being scraped for food, ... <i>(1938,Promenade,253</i>)

>  <b>BEATTIE</b> The [Otago] Maoris used to rub hinu-titi or muttonbird oil on their heads and into their hair. <i>(1939,First White Boy Born in Otago,23</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  They tore up every piece of linen and rag they could find and bound up and dressed them with mutton bird oil. <i>(Apr 17, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

### muttonbird plant
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Cotula Featherstonii</i> [[1952 Richards Chatham Islands 48 <i>C[otula] Featherstonii</i>. ... Found only on the Chathams on the dry peaty soil in which the mutton birds have made their burrows, the plants probably live on the birds' manure.]]

>  <b>HOLMES</b> <i>Cotula featherstoni</i> (Mutton-bird Plant) - a perennial grey-coloured plant of 10-15 cm. ... It is associated with petrels and other burrowing birds. <i>(1984,Chatham Islands,94</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> Other interesting Chatham Island plants are the mutton-bird plant (<i>Cotula Featherstonii</i>), which grows only near the holes of the petrels; ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,124</i>)

### muttonbird scrub
 
 <i>n.</i> a mixed coastal scrub mainly of Stewart Island. (Stewart Island) <i>Olearea Colensoi<i/> and <i>Senecio rotundifolius</i> (both together or separately); also <i>mutton-bird shrub </i>

>  Manuka, flax, broadleaf, <i>Dracophyllum</i> and muttonbird scrub - all common on Stewart Island, 100 km away - are missing on the Snares. <i>(Mar 2003,NZ Geographic,62/65</i>)

>  On windswept Ackers Point, at the tip of the peninsula, the main vegetation is muttonbird scrub; muttonbirds (sooty shearwaters) nest in burrows beneath it in spring. The name "muttonbird scrub" has a derogatory ring to it, but the various tree daisies, with their white flowers and tough leathery leaves, furry white beneath, that fall under the muttonbird rubric are all beautiful shrubs. <i>(Mar 2002,NZ Geographic,No. 56/79</i>)

>  <b>HAWES</b> He crashed down towards the river through the wild Irishman, mingimingi, manuka and tree-daisy - leaving behind him a mixed fragrance of rotten egg from smashed stinkwood and roses from bruised mutton-bird scrub. <i>(2000,Dream of Nikau Jam, The,20</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> S. rotundifolius is the mutton-bird scrub of Stewart Island [cf leather-leaf] <i>(1940,Plants of NZ,477</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Along the coastal fringes [of Stewart Island] it consists of a characteristic scrub known locally as `Mutton-bird scrub', the principal constituents ... being Puheretaiko (<i>Senecio Rotundifolius</i>), Tete-a-weka (<i>Olearia Angustifolia</i>) and Daisy Tree (<i>Olearia Colensoi</i>). <i>(1940,Rakiura,xvii</i>)

>  <b>MATTHEWS</b> <i>Senecio rotundifolius</i> mutton-bird scrub <i>(1905,Tree Culture in NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>cock</b> Where the coastal scrub of Stewart Island is densest, it has received the name of `mutton-bird scrub'. This consists largely of the puheritaiko, a very fine shrubby groundsel (<i>Senecio rotundifolius</i> ) ... <i>(1910,NZ Plants,76</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Amongst this bracken's miserable short fronds ... grow groundsel trees scattered in park-like isolation. Mutton Bird scrub is its island name ... <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,13</i>)

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Senecio sciadophilus</i> mutton-bird scrub <i>(1919,NZ Plants & Their Story</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>LINKLATER</b> Ferns and tree-ferns flourish [on Stewart Island], ... supple-jack entwines your heels, and stinkwood is garlanded with little red flowers. There is red or black beech, there is mutton-bird scrub with thick green leaves that have white undersides, there are, perhaps, a dozen others. <i>(1953,Year of Space, A,203,1954 ed.</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... he was stooping to peer into [19] the mutton-bird scrub ... <i>Ibid.</i>47 The familiar mutton-bird scrub, tupare, covers much of the ground and there is a handsome relative, also called tupare, which grows only on a few of the islands in [48] Foveaux Strait and on the Snares. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,18</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> ... he was stooping to peer into [19] the mutton-bird scrub ... <i>Ibid.</i>47 The familiar mutton-bird scrub, tupare, covers much of the ground and there is a handsome relative, also called tupare, which grows only on a few of the islands in [48] Foveaux Strait and on the Snares. <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,18</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Senecio reinoldii</i> MUTTONBIRD SCRUB <i>... Puuheretaaiko ... Muttonwood ... Shrub or small tree. Leaves leathery ... </i>The term `muttonbird scrub' is often used generally for coastal scrub dominated by any of the tree daisies [<i>Olearia</i> and <i>Senecio</i> spp.], frequently mixed with inaka. <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,102</i>)

>  <b>HOLDEN</b> Reckon that fern and muttonbird scrub beat me ...like a jungle up there. <i>(1982,Wild Pig,62</i>) [NOTES: (Stewart Island)]

>  Botanically, the island consist of lowland podocarp forest (covering mnore than two-thirds of the island), coastal "muttonbird scrub" and upland shrublands, the last of these often with granite outcrops jutting through the vegetation. <i>(Sep 2002,NZ Geographic,59/58</i>)

### muttonbird season
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  [Arid Island, hauraki] is seldom visited, except by maories, in the Mutton-bird season - the end of November. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/164</i>)

>  <b>DEMPSEY</b> Herekopere ... where once I'd spent three days in the mutton-bird season ... -- <i>(1964,Little World of Stewart island,37</i>)

### muttonbird shrub
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Senecio rotundifolius</i>

>  <b>COCKAYNE</b> <i>Senecio rotundifolius </i>mutton-bird shrub <i>(1924,Cultivation of NZ Plants, The</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>MARTIN</b> Another daisy shrub growing in great abundance round the coast of Stewort Island is that known as the LEATHERWOOD or MUTTONBIRD SHRUB (<i>S[enecio] puffini</i>), the leaves of which were at one time frequently used [211] as postcards ... . <i>(1961,Flora NZ,210,1929 ed.</i>)

### muttonbird tree
 
 <i>n.</i> (See [also mutton tree](mob-mys.md#mutton-tree))

>  The bush is mainly Olearia Colensoi.. This tree is known as the mutton bird tree; and we found the reason for the whole of the ground on the island is honeycombed with mutton bird holes <i>(Jan 30, 1890,Otago Witness,15</i>)

>  <i>Olearia Colensoi Senecio rotundifolius </i> <i>(1889,Zealandia</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  This tree [<i>Olearia</i>] is known in Stewart Island as the mutton-bird tree; and we soon found the reason, for the whole of the ground ... is honey-combed with mutton-bird holes. <i>(1891,TrNZI,23/494</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mutton-bird tree</i>, a tree <i>Senecio rotundifolius,</i> Hook.: so called because the mutton-birds, especially in Foveaux Straits, New Zealand, are fond of sitting under it. <i>(1898,Austral English,310</i>)

>  Kai ... is a ... somewhat uninteresting island, as the sombre-coloured mutton bird tree is almost the only vegetation. <i>(Nov 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,115</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> The `muttonbird trees' (Olearias) have a very limited life anyway, so nature maintains a supply of dead trees which is sufficient to keep up the supply of firewood for domestic purposes. <i>(1979,Titi Heritage,64</i>)

### muttonbird woods
 
 <i>n.</i> here perhaps a confusion of `woods', `muttonwood' (See [muttonwood](mob-mys.md#muttonwood))

>  In hunting [Stewart Island Kiwi] his plan was to ...intercept them on their way from their open feeding-grounds to the shelter of the `mutton-bird woods,' ... <i>(1897,TrNZI,29/205</i>) [NOTES: (Buller)]

### muttonbirder
 
 <i>n.</i> a person who catches young mutton-birds for food or sale. [see birder]

>  A muttonbirder stabbed in the neck this week during a dispute over a bird has returned to the island to continue catching the delicacy while his alleged attacker is awaiting a court appearance. <i>(May 13, 2010,Taranaki Daily News,5</i>)

>  Only one or two other muttonbirders were on the island when the man disappeared. It was assumed Mr Whaitiri was familiar with the island, he said. Muttonbirders were allowed on the islands from March 15 to prepare for the six-week season, which starts on April 1. <i>(Mar 27, 2003,Southland Times,Stuff</i>)

>  Along the beach a small group of muttonbirders are loading the last of their gear on to a small row boat. <i>(Jun 9, 2007,Southland Times,1</i>)

>  The occupants of the boat were.. mutton birders. <i>(Apr 17, 1890,Otago Witness,19</i>)

>  Dean Te Au is going because he belongs to the most exclusive sect in New Zealand. He is a muttonbirder, a member of a tiny club with rights to occupy for a few weeks each year one of the clutch of islands where the titi burrow into the peaty ground and nest. <i>(Jan 4, 2003,Listener (New Zealand Listener)</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> He heard the harkowhy up in Canaan on the Takaka Hill, the ghost-bird never seen but many times heard by muttonbirders and the odd fisherman in the mist and fog in Stewart Island, crying like a chain rattling. <i>(1983,Down from Marble Mountain,142</i>)

>  <b>GUTHRIE-SMITH</b> Even then Leask pressed fish hooks on us, ... for the last lot of mutton-birders had ben storm and nearly starved. <i>(1914,Mutton Birds & Other Birds,19</i>)

>  ... there has been some clearing round the mutton-birders' huts. <i>(1917,TrNZI,49/168</i>) [NOTES: (Poppelwell)]

>  <b>BUCK</b> This method of preserving the flesh of birds is closely akin to that followed to-day by the mutton-birders on the southern islands ... <i>(1937,Moa-Hunters of NZ,194</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> The mutton-birders remain for but six or eight weeks, and they keep the huts and `whares' as clean and tidy as the nature of these dwellings will permit. <i>(1940,Rakiura,211</i>)

>  <b>BAILEY & ROTH</b> I am a gay muttonbirder,/ ... I'm an expert at catching the titi. <i>(1967,Shanties,136,c.1940 ed.</i>)

>  "The natives ought to receive 2s 6d a bird," declared an Invercargill man who had just returned from a few days spent among the mutton-birders. <i>(Jun 21, 1932,Oamaru Mail,4</i>)

>  <b>AVERY</b> I've eaten oysters in a stew at Stewart Island And met a mutton-birder down at Foveaux Strait. <i>(1974,Ken Avery Songbook, The,Unpaged</i>)

>  Mutton-birders insist on securing the full maximum retail price for birds at the wharf at Bluff. <i>(Jun 5, 1948,Dominion,6</i>)

### muttonbirding
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  The tradition of muttonbirding is unique to the Bluff. <i>(May 21, 2006,Sunday Star Times,A8</i>)

>  <b>STOKES</b> <i>28th April</i>: nearly all the residents [of Stewart Island were] off `mutton-birding'. <i>(Apr 28, 1850,Acheron Journal</i>) [NOTES: 1850 Acheron's Journal Capt J.L. Stokes in 1940 Howard Rakiura 386]

>  <b>HOWARD</b> Mutton-birding is the only modern survival of native enterprise, the only phase of primitive economic development which has withstood the destructive competition ... introduced by the white man. <i>(1940,Rakiura,210</i>)

>  <b>FULLER</b> Torches (rama) were used extensively for night travel, for muttonbirding and other nocturnal hunting. <i>(1978,Maori Food and Cookery,14</i>)

>  Muttonbirding begins on 1 April. Formerly the birders travelled by sea, but now many hire a helicopter. <i>(1985,Reader's Digest Book of NZ Birds,97</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>HOCKEN</b> Arrived here [at Taieri], they found the few huts deserted, the natives having gone on a mutton birding expedition. <i>(1898,Contributions to the Early History of New Zealand,37</i>)

>  We now induced the Captain to tell us something of his mutton-birding experiences. <i>(Nov 1903,NZ Illustrated Magazine,117</i>)

>  <b>HOWARD</b> The natives and half-castes of Stewart Island ..., the descendants of those nominated in the Deed of Sale as sole proprietors of the mutton-birding rights, betake themselves to the islets ... <i>(1940,Rakiura,207</i>)

>  <b>SHAW & FARRANT</b> He found there that Weller's whaling station on Maturata Island was almost abandoned and the natives away on a mutton-birding expedition. However, one of his party, on a mokihi (raft) constructed of korari sticks floated up the lovely gorge with the flood tide, and returned with two boats manned by natives from the Maori kaik beyond the gorge. <i>(1949,Taieri Plain,3</i>)

>  <b>GORDON</b> The birds always return to the same spot in spite of the age-old mutton-birding practice. <i>(1938,Children of Tane,216</i>)

>  Rakiura is drifting in and out of focus. McQueen has been there, and to Ruapuke, but not to the Titi Islands, although Whaitiri has muttonbirding rights. <i>(Mar 29, 2000,Listener (New Zealand Listener),58</i>)

>  Some time ago I watched a documentary on television about muttonbirding. <i>(Apr 23, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),11</i>)

### muttonbirdish
 
 <i>adj.</i>

>  <b>POSPOSIL</b> Next day I ate only chocolate and drank rum, and with burning throat was damning the brother Slav and his mutton-birdish hospitality. <i>(1935,Wandering on the Islands of Wonders,84</i>)

### muttoneer
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  If having a good time means looking like a disco fool, muttoneers are up for it... There's a sure-fire way to stymie nastie ageists. Get a younger man. Fabulous muttoneers have generations to select from - but unfortunately old rams are not much chop. <i>(Aug 13, 2000,Sunday Star Times,D1</i>)

### muttoner
 
 <i>n.</i> (Rural) a shepherd.

>  shepherd - muttoner. <i>(2001,Questionnaire Respondents (DB)</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> a fast cricket delivery that mashes finger against bat hand. <i>[D.J. Cameron</i>, New Zealand Herald, <i>25 February 1989] </i> <i>(1989,Dinkum Kiwi Dictionary, The,69</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) a sheep to be home-killed for consumption on a farm property.

>  <b>EVANS</b> As he grew older, he took on the slaughtering of the sheep for mutton. One the same day as he baked the bread, he would kill and dress two muttoners. <i>(1975,Silver Tussock, The,135</i>)

### muttonfish
 
 <i>n.</i> an old word for paua or abalone

>  Heaphy's use of the expression "mutton fish" verifies the paua's status as a staple food with coastal dwellers around this time. <i>(Jul 1998,NZ Geographic,68</i>)

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> 88. <i>Haliotis Iris.</i> ... `The foot black when alive. The `mutton-fish' of the colonists; eaten boiled, but very tough. ... - <i>Dr. Sinclair. </i> <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,2/239</i>) [NOTES: 1842 Gray's Fauna (Mollusca) in 1843 Dieffenbach Travels in NZ 2/239]

>  There was a luscious `Paua' or Mutton-fish decaying on the beach, a great screaming of hungry Dominicans overhead, ... <i>(Mar 1904,NZ Illustrated Magazine,482</i>)

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Kororiwha, 1</i>. n. <i>Haliotis australis, sea-ear, mutton-fish</i>; a univalve mollusc. [so 1957/146] <i>(1917,Dictionary of the Maori Language,170</i>)

>  The paupa, <i>Haliotis iris</i>, also called mutton-fish by settlers, was first brought to England by Captain Cook. <i>(1935,NZ Journal of Science & Technology,16/297</i>) [NOTES: (Phillipps)]

>  <b>WILLIAMS</b> <i>Paua,</i> n. 1. <i>Haliotis</i> of several species,<i> sea-ear, mutton-fish;</i> <i>(1958,Dictionary of the Maori Language,273</i>)

>  mutton-fish A shellfish in Australia said to taste like mutton; in New Zealand it is called by the Maori name `paua'. <i>(1958,Whitcombe's Modern Junior Dictionary (8th edn),269</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>muttonfish</i> paua. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

>  <b>TENNISON-WOODS</b> <i>(1882,Fish and Fisheries of New South Wales,92</i>)

>  On landing [sc. at Bluff] we noticed some fishermen at the wharf selling handsome mutton fish shells, of which the prismatic colours are so beautiful that very pretty ornaments and jewellery are fashioned out of them. <i>(Sep 25, 1890,Otago Witness,35</i>)

>  muttonfish - pa'hiwah [paua] <i>(1835,Boultbee Journal,110</i>) [NOTES: c1826-1827 list of Maori words in c1835 Boultbee Journal (1986); 1826-28 Boultbee Vocabulary in 1979 Begg 283]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> [note: Markham had been to Tasmania] They dry the Pippies, Mutton fish, Snappers, Shark &c for their Feasts. <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),47,1963 ed.</i>)

>  <b>McNAB</b> [James Davidson's evidence] I know a fish called mutton fish, which is much eaten by the men, but I never heard of its being unwholesome or producing worms; ... there was a man name [sic] Graham, Palmer's boat steerer who was ill, and Palmer attended him; ... <i>(1913,Old Whaling Days</i>) [NOTES: 1838 May 16 Palmer Trial Evidence in 1913 McNab Old Whaling Days 209]

>  <b>POLACK</b> Among the larger tribes that sport in shoals in the vicinity of the shore, are, sperm-whales, called <i>para</i> [as-acute] <i>paraua</i> [a3-acute] (physeter macrocephalus).. ; and an infinity of others, besides a great variety of testaceous and crustaceous fishes that almost cover the rocks under water, such as clams, muscles, oysters, crays, cuttles, limpets, wilks, gigantic muscles, a foot in length, cockles, sea-ears, sea-eggs, star-fish, mutton-fish, &c.; from the latter the pearl is procured which they insert in the eyes of their carved images and boxes. <i>Ibid.</i> 2/286 Pearl shell may be obtained from the Pawa or Mutton-fish <i>(1840,Manners & Customs of the NZers,1/202 fn</i>)

>  <b>HEAPHY</b> The native method of catching this [kahawai] is singular, and worthy of notice. A hook made of bone, with a piece of the glistening shell of the `mutton fish' attached to it, but without any farther bait, is trailed at a short distance behind a canoe, which is being paddled with the greatest possible speed. <i>(1842,New Zealand (various authors/titles),48</i>)

>  <b>TAYLOR</b> I noticed little heaps of a large kind of Haliotis or mutton fish shells in each kumara ground: ... <i>(Dec 1847,Journal,V/164</i>)

>  <b>MACKIE</b> The beautiful pearly shell of the mutton fish is also [113] common. <i>(May 25, 1853,Traveller under Concern,112,1973 ed.</i>)

>  <b>HAAST</b> In the pools on the rocky shores, the Haliotis Iris (mutton fish) is the principal inhabitant, .. <i>(1861,Report on Topographical Exploration of Nelson,141</i>)

>  <b>HAY</b> In the bays and tidal rivers are the mango or sharks - the most highly-prized food-fish - the tamure or schnapper, the whapuka or rock-cod, the kahawai or mackerel, the porahi or herring, the kanae or mullet, the patiki or sole, and many others. On the shores are oysters, mussels, cockles, mutton-fish, crabs, and other shell-fish in profuse abundance. In the fresh-water creeks are eels (tuna), lampreys (pipiharau), and whitebait (inanga). <i>(1882,Brighter Britain,2/151</i>)

>  <b>DOMETT</b> Mutton-fish ..... Haliotis Iris. <i>(1883,Ranolf and Amohia,2/338</i>)

>  <b>MORRIS</b> <i>Mutton-fish a marine univalve molusc</i>, Haliotis naevosa, <i>Martyn: d from its flavour when cooked. The empty earshell of Haliotis</i>, especially in New Zealand, <i>Haliotis iris</i>, Martyn, is known as <i>Venus' Ear</i>; Maori name, <i>Paua<i> (q.v.). A species of the same genus is known and eaten at the Cape and in the Channel Islands. (French name <i>Ormer,</i> sc. <i>Oreille de mer.</i>) <i>(1898,Austral English,310</i>)

### muttonfish shell
 
 <i>n.</i> paua shell

>  <b>POLACK</b> ... the eyes [of the carved figures] were formed of pieces of the pearl, paua, or mutton fish-shell, ... <i>(1838,New Zealand  (Polack),1/92</i>)

>  Pieces of pawa or mutton-fish shell. <i>(1840,NZ Journal,24/298</i>) [NOTES: E.J. Wakefield]

>  <b>SEWELL</b> The mutton fish shell very beautiful, others common place enough, many very like our own. <i>(1853,Journal,1/149,1980 ed.</i>)

### muttonscrag
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  I would include among New Zealand English words: <i>muttonscrag </i> <i>(May 6, 1952,VUC First Year English Class</i>)

### muttonwood
 
 <i>n.</i> <i>Olearia colensoi</i>, mutton-bird scrub, -mutton-bird wood (See [also leatherwood](lay-liv.md#leatherwood))

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Olearia Colensoi</i> mutton-wood <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <i> mutton n</i>. ... -mutt'on-bird n. a migratory sea-bird of the Bass Strait.-mutt'on tree' n. a particular tree in which mutt'on-birds collect.- mutt'on-wood n. a shrub found in coastal areas visited by mutt'on-birds. <i>(1929,Whitcombe's Etym. Dict. Aust-NZ Suppl.,8</i>)

>  <b>WILSON</b> <i>Senecio reinoldii</i> MUTTONBIRD SCRUB ... <i>Puuheretaaiko</i> ... Muttonwood ... Shrub or small tree. Leaves leathery ... <i>(1982,Stewart Island Plants,102</i>)

>  <i>Mut&#769;.ton-wood&#769;</i>. (-w&obreve.&obreve.d&#769;.),<i> n. (Bot.)</i> A composite tree of New Zealand <i>(Olearia Colensoi)</i> so called because it grows on islands frequented by mutton birds. <i>[Local, N. Z.] </i> <i>(1898,Merriam-Webster Third International,2027</i>)

>  Take Olearia colensoi, the Leather or Muttonwood, a pretty green shrub that prefers a cool, wet site and grows in stands so dense that it can be impassable, any attempt at progress resulting in cuts and tears from its saw-toothed leaves. <i>(Feb 9, 2002,Dominion Weekend Magazine,8</i>)

### Mutts
 
 <i>n.</i> the Mongrel Mob

>  The Mutts (Mongrel Mob) broke that treaty. <i>(Feb 16, 2002,Manawatu Evening Standard Saturday Magazine,9</i>)

>  In a statement made to the police six days after the killing, he said the group had heard "barking" before being chased by "the mutts" [slang for Mongrel Mob members]who had been armed with knives, baseball bats and a gun. <i>(Jul 16, 2002,Dominion Post,A1</i>)

>  They knew what the barking meant: the "mutts" (Mob) were coming... "As the mutts were approaching us they were saying 'c'mon niggers' and barking." <i>(Jul 20, 2002,Weekend Herald,A5</i>)

### muzzied
 
 <i>adj.</i> fuddled; drunk [= SOED muzzed, muzzy]

>  John Richard Bourke got his wages last Saturday,. . ., and at once started to blow in for a lot of it on booze ... - the remarkable things happened afterwards, when John Richard was comfortably muzzied. <i>(Mar 5, 1910,NZ Truth,245/7/3</i>)

### MVC
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Mana Volunteer Coastguard

>  The embattled Mana Volunteer Coastguard (MVC) will be managed by a special commissioner until new management is appointed in September. <i>(May 21, 2001,Evening Post,2</i>)

### MWD
 
 <i>abbr.</i> Ministry of Works and Development

>  <b>McGILL</b> At Tairoa he pointed to the MWD camp where 15 or 20 Dalmatians lived while they tunnelled the line through the Buller Gorge. <i>(1985,G'day Country,154</i>)

### my  arse
 
 <i>exclam.</i> exclamation of disbelief or abuse. Perhaps felt as an abbrev. of the frequent <i>kiss my arse!</i> or <i>up my arse!</i>

>  <b>HYDE</b> `Who's going to cut loose?' `cut loose my arse' <i>(1938,Nor the Years Condemn,187</i>)

### my arse is a red cabbage
 
 <i>phr.</i> [[P8, AC: ??omit.]]

>  <b>McGILL</b> my arse is a red cabbage c/p indicative of total confidence; eg `If he jumps over two metres, my arse is a red cabbage.' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

### my colonial oath
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>WRIGHT</b> But, my blooming straight Colonial! there are two things in that cart/ ... and them things is <i>grit and heart/. </i> <i>(1897,Station Ballads,33</i>) [NOTES: `The Hawker's Cart']

>  `My oath!' said the Bloke. `My Colonial oath!' <i>(1946,Johnny Enzed in Italy,35</i>)

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>my colonial oath! </i>a mild expletive used usually as reinforcement, with variations such as <i>my oath!, my bloody oath!, my bloody colonial oath! </i>NZA. <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

>  Come now, just get out of my boat, for I wont take'ee ashore - my colonial oath I wont. <i>(Apr 19, 1884,4</i>)

>  I called nearly everything a <i>faquement</i> (which name, by the way, I never yet saw in print, and cannot attempt to define); I protested on the authority of <i>my colonial oath</i>; learned to <i>plant</i> anything I wished to preserve; to <i>blow</i> about the <i>hammerings</i> I had given to men, whom I should not have dared to have touched with the end of a barge pole; to <i>finance</i> at cards whenever it was feasible; to give <i>fits</i> to whoever opposed me in discussions; and swear a <i>hurricane</i> if anything crossed my temper. <i>(Aug 26, 1871,Timaru Herald,2</i>)

>  "We know more of both English and Australian courses than does Mr Stead." My colonial oath he does! <i>(Feb 29, 1908,NZ Truth,4</i>)

>  It has been very fine here lately, but when it does rain - 'Oh, my colonial oath,' Wellington can take a back seat and southerly busters can retire abashed. <i>(Mar 8, 1915,Evening Post,2</i>)

### my elbow
 
 <i>exclam.</i>

>  <b>McGILL</b> <i>my elbow! </i>exclamation of surprise or disbelief, used by Frank S. Anthony in <i>Me and Gus</i>, perhaps as a variation of `my eyes!' <i>(1988,Dictionary of Kiwi Slang, A,74</i>)

### My Fair Young Lady
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>SUTTON-SMITH</b> There are games recorded in this period for which there is no record before 1900 though they may well have existed under other names. <i>West Coast</i>: Dunk and Davey, Spang Weazling; <i>Wellington</i>: Who Goes Round My Stone Wall, Beg o' My Neighbor, Bellahonie, Run a Mile, On and Off the Bridge, Here Come Two Nuns, Colours, Isaac and Rebecca. Scrag, Diabolo, Bumble Puppy, Homai, Fives; <i>Auckland:</i> My Fair Young Lady, Rushes and Reeds, Surrender the Tower, Walls, Ball Hopscotch, ball games, Solomon Says; <i>Hawkes Bay</i>: Wolf Wolf Go Home, Homai; <i>Canterbury:</i> Poor Old Tom, Rats and Rabbits and Rips, Steps, Skin the Snake, Steal a Peg, Toodle-em-Buck, The Gag; <i>Golden Bay</i>: Who's That Walking Round My House Tonight, I've Come to See Poor Mary Jane, Steps and Stairs, Creeping, Branding, O'Leary; <i>Otago:</i> Sir Roger de Coverley, Who's That Knocking at My Door. <i>(1981,History of Children's Play,231</i>)

### my oath
 
 <i>exclam.</i> (informal) certainly! an exclamation of assent or enthusiasm (See [also my colonial oath](mob-mys.md#my-colonial-oath); [bloody oath](bla-bri.md#bloody-oath))

>  `Do you like going to church?' asked a visitor of a Methodist minister's son. `My oath!' said the lad. `It's a real boshter to hear Dad talking for a solid hour and a half, and Ma never allowed to say a word!' <i>(Sep 8, 1906,NZ Truth,64/1/3</i>)

>  When one comes to think of it, instead of saying `Rightly, sir,' Connelly would have remarked, `They picked it, my oath,' or words to that effect, and instead of saying, `I alone killed Bourke,' he would probably observe, `I was on my lonesome, boss.' [Explanatory sentence for `They picked it, my oath' `Have you been rightly or wrongly convected?' asked the discredited 'Tec. `Rightly,sir,' said Connelly.] <i>(May 29, 1909,NZ Truth,205/5/2</i>)

>  My oath, she's the bird [=`cow'] that pays the rent. <i>(Jun 9, 1906,NZ Truth,51/5/8</i>) [NOTES: (Jim the Milker, Taranaki)]

>  [Officer to group of returned men outside hotel marked Gallipoli Arms] `Now then boys, ... At the word `charge!' sail in & help yourselves.' [Balloons have men in group agreeing with:] `Hooray!' `Bully for you skipper.' `My oath Capn.' <i>(1908,Free Lance</i>) [NOTES: 1918 Messenger cartoon in Free Lance `When Our Boys Come Home' in .ho;1980 Grant Unauthorised Version 103]

>  My oath! he was making a welter of it. <i>(May 15, 1918,Kia-ora Coo-ee,3/5,1981 ed.</i>)

>  ... well, they doesn't tike it 'xactly but, my oath! They plays up 'Ell wiv our chaps ... <i>Blue mackerel Scomber autralasicus</i> ... An Indo-Pacific species ... Often simply called mackerel, from its close similarity to the common mackerel (<i>Scomber scomber</i>) of the Northern Hemisphere ... In New Zealand it has often been called English mackerel and Pacific mackerel, but it is becoming more widely and appropriately known now as blue mackerel. <i>(Nov 1, 1918,Quick March,17</i>)

>  <b>COOK</b> `Perhaps you'll square up?' `My oath!' sad the debtor. <i>(1925,Far Flung,12</i>)

>  <b>SCANLAN</b> `Hot driving today?' `My oath!' <i>(1932,Pencarrow,248</i>)

>  <b>PARTRIDGE</b> <i>Oath!, my</i>. A mild expletive: mostly Australian and New Zealand: late C. 19-20. Ex the more trival senses of S.E. <i>oath.</i> See also <i>colonial oath</i>. <i>(1937,Dictionary of Slang,577</i>)

>  That's not a Wog, son - that's a Thirty-Niner, my oath. <i>(Oct 19, 1942,NZEF Times,12</i>)

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> `And on top of that we'll be eligible for Social Security.' `My oath yes.' <i>(1945,Gunner Inglorious,140</i>)

>  `My oath,' I said. `Many a Jap binjie is going to get a hoodickey stuck in it before sparrow-hiccough. ...' <i>(1945,Sixes & Sevens,7</i>)

>  <b>SLATTER</b> She's a great world. My oath, it is. <i>(1959,Gun in My Hand, A,151</i>)

>  `My oath I'll ring the Mayor,' Larry said. `I'll ring the Prime Minister if those lights aren't put on tonight.' <i>(1962,Landfall Country,119</i>) [NOTES: Ballantyne `And the Glory']

>  <b>HELMER</b> I dragged him over to his bed and pulled off his boots ... `Sweet dreams,' I said, chuckling `'ckin' oath,' he mumbled and started to snore. <i>(1964,Stag Party,114</i>)

>  <i>oath, my; my colonial</i> (phrase) a mild expletive <i>(1966,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Whitcombe's),620</i>)

>  <i>oath my; my colonial (Phr.)</i> mild expletive. <i>(1968,NZ Contemp. Dict. Suppl. (Collins),14</i>)

>  <b>O'SULLIVAN</b> The man who had called for order said `Hear, hear,' and this was taken up by several others, some of whom said `Oath' or `Too right' or `That's the line to take'. <i>(1976,Miracle,14</i>)

>  No fear, ehoa! I the sick man all right! My oath! <i>(1947,Book, a Miscellany,No. 9/4</i>)

>  <b>MARSH</b> `Italians! My oath!' <i>(1980,Photo-Finish,202</i>)

>  <i>my oath</i> <i>(Feb 1981,Avondale College Slang Words in Use</i>) [NOTES: (Goldie Brown Collection)]

>  <i>oath ... my oath NZ & Austral. sl</i>. mild expletive or exclamation: yes! of course! <i>(1986,NZ Pocket Oxford Dictionary,516</i>) [NOTES: [cf. 1982 Collins Concise Eng. Dict. 775]]

>  <b>JACKSON</b> ... my bloomin' oath I am. <i>(1943,Passage to Tobruk,19</i>)

>  <b>PICARD</b> He's got a slog on, and could about sink a long beer ... Won't he have earned it? My oath. <i>Ibid.</i> 22 All right, don't bash a hole in the planet by bawling out `My oath' so lo <i>(1906,Some Ups and Downs in NZ&A,6</i>)

>  At last I got so savage that I used to let out at them in real colonial style, and my oath, I got as good as I gave them. <i>(Aug 26, 1867,Hawke's Bay Weekly Times,209</i>)

### mynah
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  Our society also turned out some Indian minahs ands Australian magpies, but as far as I know, neither succeeded... Minahs are, however, numerous in some parts of the North Island. <i>(1898,TrNZI,30/274</i>)

>  The Indian Mynah.. is reported.. to be plentiful near Wanganui. <i>(Apr 27, 1922,Auckland Weekly News,15</i>)

### myrtle
 
 <i>n.</i> various New Zealand myrtle-like plants, such as ramarama, rohutu; manuka, ?kanuka [also bush myrtle [1919 Jul 29] Ver. (WB).= Vernacular names used by settlers supplied by W. Best, Otaki (And) Myrtus bullata bush myrtle 1919 W Best (Otaki) Vernac. Andersen) (bush probably `growing in the bush'; possibly `growing as a bush-, or timber-, tree' (not as a shrub) cf. BUSH MANUKA) Myrtus bullata bush myrtle] (See [also bush myrtle](../A#also-bush-myrtle))

>  <b>MURRY</b> [?KANUKA: not RATA or ?RAMARAMA] Capt. Cook has given so good a description of the Spruce Fir, that it is impossible to mistake it. But he has not taken any notice of the Pitch Pine - Birch - And large Myrtle. ... The Myrtle is not so large, it grows near the water, has a red bark, and is known by the smell of its leaf which very much resembles the smell of the leaf from which its [sic] takes its name, it is of use for turners or Cabinet makers, makes excellent block Pins, and from its hardness may be converted to many uses with which I am unacquainted. <i>(Jan 1796,Journal,2/534</i>) [NOTES: 1796 Jan Murry's Journal of the `Britannia' in 1908 McNab Hist Recs. 2/534]

>  <b>FORSTER</b> We ... found a fine Myrtle tree, the Leaves of which made good Tea. ... <i>March Ye 31st</i> In the afternoon we got flowers of the New Zeeland Teatree, & also a new Spidewell ... The next morning we tried all the three different Teas; viz: the Myrtle [[ed. fn] <i>Leptospermum scoparium</i>], the Spruce & the New Zeeland one [[ed. fn] <i>L. ericoides ?</i>]; of which the first seems the most palatable. <i>(Mar 30, 1773,Resolution Journal of J.R. Forster, The,2/244</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> The supple-jack is to be met with in all parts of the woods; and there is here also a species of the myrtle similar to that found in various parts of New South Wales. Another part of it grows in the country about Dusky Bay, an infusion of which was drank [sic] by the crew of the Endeavour, as a substitute for tea. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),2/246</i>)

>  <b>NICHOLAS</b> On the hills we saw some of the myrtle species, but the tea-plant, so common in Otaheite, and said to grow here, [233] and from which the natives of that island extract a spirituous liquor, we could not meet with it in the progress of our excursion. <i>(1817,New Zealand (various authors/titles),1/232</i>) [NOTES: [=? MANUKA??]]

>  <b>MARKHAM</b> Hattay [crossed out by Markham] or Ettay [<i>heti</i>] is a Tea tree, quite different to the Tea tree bush, a sort of myrtle; as Parr a Fort and Hipparr [<i>he pa</i>] a Fort in Cooke, / is put down The Hip or Hit, is only the Article. Tay, it is useless, it only grows eight to twelve feet and the bark rough, and does not change. It seems very often Solitary. Away from every other Tree. [HWO: M. is describing TI (cabbage-tree) and contrasting it with MANUKA TEA-TREE `a sort of myrtle'] <i>(1834,New Zealand (various authors/titles),33,1963 ed.</i>)



 
 <i>n.</i> (North Island) rata; rata vine

>  ... long list of timber trees whose fine stems are almost all disfigured with rank parasites. One of these parasites, the red myrtle, winding in and [ampersand] out of its patron becomes itself an enormous tree stifling the original growth ...[sic] as the first tree rots from its grasp. It is covered in season with lovely scarlet and [ampersand] gold flowers in shape like those of the English myrtle. <i>(Mar 25, 1851,Richmond-Atkinson Papers,1/79,1960 ed.</i>) [NOTES: (J.C. Richmond)]

>  <b>FEATON</b> Aka [Maori name] .. Myrtle [Settlers' name] .. Metrosideros scandens .. Climber <i>Ibid.</i> 178 Torotoro [Maori name] .. Myrtle [Settlers' name] .. Metrosideros scandens ..Climber <i>(1889,Art Album NZ Flora,177</i>)

>  Among vegetable anomalies we find creeping ferns and creeping myrtle. <i>(1841,Establishment of New Plymouth Settlement,81,1940 ed.</i>)



 
<b>2</b> <i>n.</i> <i>Lophomyrtus bullata,</i> ramarama;(<i>Lophomyrtus obcordata. (formerly </i>Myrtus) [1846 CSJ; 1848 NZJ (NZ Myrtle) (Andersen); 1909 TNN; 1917 DDD (native myrtle) (Andersen); 1910 GHB 1916 OTR (NZ myrtle) (Andersen)]

>  <b>DIEFFENBACH</b> Near the beach appear shrublike veronicas [fn Veronica ligustrifolia.], myrtles [fn Myrtus bullata.], fuchsias, solanum [fn Solanum laciniatum.], the karaka tree [fn Corynocarpus laevigata.], tutu [fn Coriaria sarmentosa.], and flax [fn Phormium tenax.]. <i>(1843,Travels in NZ,1/26</i>)

>  Myrtles (<i>Myrtus Obcordata </i>and <i>pedunculata</i>). Two handsome shrub trees, common near Dunedin. <i>(1869,TrNZI,1/III/32</i>) [NOTES: (Buchanan Botany Otago)]

>  <i>Myrtus obcordata</i> Myrtle <i>(1870,TrNZI,2/122</i>) [NOTES: (Armstrong Botany Canterbury)]

>  <b>KIRK</b> <i>Myrtus obcordata</i> myrtle <i>(1889,Forest Flora</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>NAIRN & SON</b> <i>Myrtus obcordata</i> myrtle <i>(1912,Catalogue (Nairn & Son)</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

>  <b>McLINTOCK</b> Myrtle, New Zealand .. .. ramarama, rohutu .. .. <i>Myrtus </i> <i>(1966,Encyclopaedia of New Zealand, An,3/7</i>)

>  MYRTLE/<i> MYRTUS OBCORDATA</i>/ Shrub reaching 5m. <i>(1982,Field Guide to Common NZ Trees & Shrubs,10</i>)

>  <b>LAING & BLACKWELL</b> <i>(1906,Plants of NZ</i>) [NOTES: (And)]

### myrtle tea
 
 <i>n.</i>

>  <b>FORSTER</b> But the constant supply of fresh fish ... together with the spruce-beer and the myrtle-tea, contributed to keep us healthy ... even in this damp climate. <i>(1777,Voyage Round the World,1/143</i>)

### mystery bag
 
 <i>n.</i> a sausage [also bags of mystery]

>  <b>HENDERSON</b> Sausages! Gee whiz! ... By some artful dodgery, the `mystery bags' had changed hands ... <i>(1961,Friends in Chains,120</i>)

### Mystery Creek
 
 <i>n.</i> the NZ national Agricultural Fieldays, held at Mystery Creek

>  [Hdg] Hard sell at Mystery Creek... Welcome to Mystery Creek!... Driving to the site to start the short-term radio station, two rural broadcasters prepare their passenger for the 16th National Agricultural Fieldays. <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  M<ore than 40,000 people a day have been flocking to the National Fieldays at Mystery Creek in Hamilton this week. <i>(Jun 13, 2003,Dominion Post,C7</i>)

>  This year 10 rural broadcasters will be based on the Fielday site, at Mystery Creek... Important information always emerges from the conference each year and the Fieldays are the biggest Southern Hemisphere showplace for the latest farm, machinery and products. <i>(Jun 11, 1983,Listener (New Zealand Listener),69</i>)



 
<b>1</b> <i>n.</i> (Rural) the site of national agricultural field days since 1974.

>  Sales from this year's national agricultural field days at Mystery Creek could be a record, organisers say. <i>(Jun 22, 2000,Taranaki Daily News,16</i>)

>  Welcome to Mystery Creek! Driving to the site to start the short-term radio station, two rural broadcasters ... <i>(Jul 21, 1984,Listener (New Zealand Listener),16</i>)

>  One of Mystery Creek's most popular exhibits of last year is returning for a repeat visit. <i>(May 26, 1993,NZ Farmer,17</i>)

>  Rewards for such dedication include meeting top VIPs, the satisfaction of a job well done and free entry to Fieldays and some other Mystery Creek events. <i>(2002,Taranaki Daily News,21</i>)

>  Mystery Creek is best known for its agricultural field days, but it is also home to a wine company hoping to tempt investors' palates. <i>(Jan 15, 2006,Sunday Star Times,D7</i>)

### mystery envelope
 
 <i>n.</i> an envelope containing a prize of varying value, sold for a stated amount as a fund-raising device

>  <b>Geary</b> Buying mystery envelopes can break up a day. I bought three from a schoolgirl on the corner. They're building a sports complex. <i>(2003,Man of the People, A,10</i>)

### mystic mitt
 
 <i>n.</i> a pot scourer of brass foil attached to a woven fabric base (a trade name) [Orsman: (mit sic) ca 1930's at least (Havelock); no NZ hits on Google 2007 but a few from elsewhere]
